# E-SKIN Firmware Workflow

## Purpose

This folder contains the firmware workflow and notes for the STM32 mainboard and Teensy 4.1 bridge.

## Current data path

```text
Sensors -> STM32 mainboard -> Host SPI -> Teensy 4.1 -> USB CDC -> PC backend -> Visualization
```

DAPLink is used for STM32 SWD programming and debugging. The Teensy is programmed separately with Teensy Loader or Arduino CLI.

## STM32G474CET6 CubeMX configuration

The current STM32 project is:

```text
hardware/new/firmware/ESKIN_STM32/
```

The device is an STM32G474CET6 in an LQFP48 package. The project is generated by STM32CubeMX 6.18.0 as a CMake project using STM32Cube FW_G4 V1.6.3.

### Debug and clock

| Function | Configuration |
|---|---|
| SWDIO | PA13, `SYS_JTMS-SWDIO` |
| SWCLK | PA14, `SYS_JTCK-SWCLK` |
| Debug mode | Serial Wire; JTAG is not used |
| Initial system clock | HSI, 16 MHz default generated configuration |
| Toolchain | CMake with GCC/ARM GNU toolchain |

### SPI1: STM32 master to the external MAX11633 ADC

`COL_ADC1` is an external MAX11633 ADC. It is not the STM32 internal ADC1 peripheral.

| Signal | Pin | Configuration |
|---|---|---|
| SCK | PA5 | SPI1 SCK |
| MISO | PA6 | SPI1 MISO |
| MOSI | PA7 | SPI1 MOSI |
| CS1 | PB0 | GPIO output, software-controlled chip select |
| EOC1 | PB10 | GPIO input; may later be changed to EXTI |

SPI1 is configured as full-duplex master, 8-bit, Motorola frame format, MSB first, CPOL low, first edge, and software NSS. The ADC transaction must drive PB0 low only for the transaction and return it high afterwards.

### SPI3: Teensy host link

The Teensy 4.1 is the SPI master and the STM32 is the SPI slave.

| Signal | Pin | Configuration |
|---|---|---|
| NSS / chip select | PA15 | SPI3 hardware NSS input |
| SCK | PB3 | SPI3 SCK, slave mode |
| MISO | PB4 | SPI3 MISO, slave mode |
| MOSI | PB5 | SPI3 MOSI, slave mode |
| HOST_IRQ | PB8 | GPIO output from STM32 to Teensy |
| HOST_SYNC | PB9 | GPIO input from the host side |
| External NRST | PG10 | GPIO output for the external reset net |

SPI3 is configured as full-duplex slave, 8-bit, Motorola frame format, MSB first, CPOL low, first edge, and hardware NSS input. The Teensy must control PA15 and provide the SPI clock.

### MUX1 GPIO

`ROW_MUX1` is a CD74HC4067:

| Signal | Pin | Startup level |
|---|---|---|
| S0 | PA0 | Low |
| S1 | PA1 | Low |
| S2 | PA2 | Low |
| S3 | PA3 | Low |
| EN1 | PA4 | High (disabled until scanning starts) |

### Startup levels

The generated `MX_GPIO_Init()` currently sets the safety levels below:

```text
PG10 / external NRST : High
PB0  / ADC_CS1       : High (ADC deselected)
PA4  / MUX_EN1       : High (MUX disabled)
PB8  / HOST_IRQ      : Low
PA0..PA3 / MUX address: Low
```

These levels prevent the external device from remaining in reset, prevent the ADC from being selected during boot, and keep the multiplexer disabled until the scan routine explicitly enables it. If CubeMX code is regenerated, re-check these output levels in `GPIO Settings` because generated files may be overwritten.

### Current implementation status

The first blocking FSR acquisition implementation is now present in `Core/Src/main.c`. It selects rows 0--15 on MUX1, enables the MUX, requests an AIN0--AIN15 scan from the MAX11633, waits for active-low EOC, reads sixteen 12-bit samples, and stores them in `fsr_frame[16][16]` as `[row][column]`. The implementation uses SPI1 polling and a 1 ms row-settling delay; it is intended for bring-up and is not yet a high-rate solution.

The MAX11633 setup command currently selects internal clock mode 10 and the internal 2.5 V reference (`0x68`). The conversion command (`0xF8`) requests a scan from AIN0 through AIN15. Confirm the command timing and reference choice against the fitted ADC variant before relying on calibrated values. The current code transmits the frame over SPI3 using polling; DMA and higher-rate buffering are still future work.

## Initial STM32--Teensy frame protocol

After a successful FSR scan, STM32 asserts `HOST_IRQ` (PB8) high. The Teensy detects the IRQ, pulls chip-select low, and clocks exactly 515 bytes from the STM32 SPI3 slave:

```text
byte 0        0xA5 frame magic
bytes 1..2    uint16 sequence number, little-endian
bytes 3..514  256 uint16 ADC samples, little-endian, row-major [row][column]
```

The payload is 16 rows × 16 columns; each sample is a 12-bit MAX11633 result stored in a 16-bit word. The Teensy writes the validated binary frame to USB CDC, then waits for `HOST_IRQ` to return low.

## Teensy 4.1 Arduino firmware

The bridge sketch is located at:

```text
hardware/new/firmware/teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE.ino
```

It uses only the built-in Arduino `SPI` library and Teensy USB Serial. No third-party Arduino libraries are required. Install the Teensy board support package, select `Teensy 4.1`, and select a USB type that provides `Serial`.

| Teensy 4.1 | STM32 mainboard |
|---|---|
| SCK (13) | PB3 / SPI3_SCK |
| MOSI (11) | PB5 / SPI3_MOSI |
| MISO (12) | PB4 / SPI3_MISO |
| CS (10) | PA15 / SPI3_NSS |
| IRQ (2) | PB8 / HOST_IRQ |
| GND | GND |

The sketch starts at 1 MHz, SPI mode 0, MSB first. Both boards must share ground and use compatible I/O voltage levels.

### Corrected HOST connector pin numbering (2026-08-09)

The connector **pin numbers themselves**, not merely the viewing direction,
were confirmed to be reversed relative to the earlier annotated image. The
authoritative electrical numbering for new-module wiring is:

```text
Mainboard J1 pin 1  = FPGA_3V3_IN -> Teensy 4.1 3.3V pin
Mainboard J1 pin 2  = GND         -> Teensy 4.1 GND pin
Mainboard J1 pin 3  = GND         -> Teensy 4.1 GND pin
Mainboard J1 pin 4  = HOST_SCK    -> Teensy 4.1 digital pin 13 / SCK
Mainboard J1 pin 5  = HOST_MOSI   -> Teensy 4.1 digital pin 11 / MOSI
Mainboard J1 pin 6  = HOST_MISO   -> Teensy 4.1 digital pin 12 / MISO
Mainboard J1 pin 7  = HOST_nCS    -> Teensy 4.1 digital pin 10 / CS
Mainboard J1 pin 8  = HOST_IRQ    -> Teensy 4.1 digital pin 2
Mainboard J1 pin 9  = HOST_SYNC   -> not connected in the current bridge
Mainboard J1 pin 10 = NRST        -> DAPLink nRESET, not Teensy
```

| Correct J1 pin | Signal | Teensy 4.1 pin printed on the board |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | FPGA_3V3_IN | `3.3V`, only when Teensy is the single power source |
| 2 | GND | `GND` |
| 3 | GND | `GND` |
| 4 | HOST_SCK | digital pin `13` / SCK |
| 5 | HOST_MOSI | digital pin `11` / MOSI |
| 6 | HOST_MISO | digital pin `12` / MISO |
| 7 | HOST_nCS | digital pin `10` / CS |
| 8 | HOST_IRQ | digital pin `2` |
| 9 | HOST_SYNC | Not connected by the current combined bridge |
| 10 | NRST | DAPLink nRESET; not a Teensy data GPIO |

Earlier workflow entries that describe `J1 pin 10` as `FPGA_3V3_IN` or `J1
pin 1` as `NRST` used the reversed annotation and must not be used as the wiring
reference. Signal names and the Teensy GPIO assignments remain unchanged; use
the corrected table above as the authoritative pinout.

### Four-module shared-Teensy connection record (2026-08-15)

This is the proposed wiring record for the four-module scalability test. It is
a design record, not a claim that the four-module harness has already passed a
hardware test. The corrected J1 numbering above applies independently to every
module.

#### Shared signals

| Signal | Every module J1 pin | Teensy 4.1 connection |
|---|---:|---|
| `HOST_SCK` | J1-4 | pin 13 / SCK (shared) |
| `HOST_MOSI` | J1-5 | pin 11 / MOSI (shared) |
| `HOST_MISO` | J1-6 | pin 12 / MISO (shared) |
| Ground | J1-2 and J1-3 | common Teensy GND / power-supply 0 V |

#### Per-module chip-select and interrupt signals

| Module | J1-7 `HOST_nCS` | J1-8 `HOST_IRQ` |
|---|---|---|
| Module 0 | Teensy pin 10 | Teensy pin 2 |
| Module 1 | Teensy pin 14 | Teensy pin 3 |
| Module 2 | Teensy pin 15 | Teensy pin 4 |
| Module 3 | Teensy pin 16 | Teensy pin 5 |

#### Power, reset and unused signal

- J1-1 (`FPGA_3V3_IN`) on each module must be fed from a separately fused,
  regulated external 3.3 V branch for the four-module experiment. Do not
  power four modules in parallel from the Teensy's 3.3 V regulator.
- J1-9 (`HOST_SYNC`) remains not connected by the current bridge firmware.
- J1-10 (`NRST`) remains the per-board DAPLink nRESET connection; it is not a
  Teensy data GPIO. Program or reset modules one at a time unless a separate
  reset fan-out has been designed and verified.

#### Bus rules and firmware status

- Keep all four chip-select lines HIGH at idle and assert only one CS LOW at a
  time. A deselected STM32 must release MISO to high impedance.
- The four IRQ outputs are separate push-pull signals; never tie them together.
- Use a common ground and star-distribute the external 3.3 V supply. Record
  rail voltage, current and per-module voltage drop before enabling the bus.
- The current bridge firmware validates only the single-module pin-10/pin-2
  mapping. Pins 14/15/16 and 3/4/5 are the proposed allocations for Modules
  1--3; multi-module CS/IRQ scheduling and the `MUL1` envelope still need to be
  implemented and tested.

### Selectable single-module scalability test (2026-08-15)

The first test implementation is deliberately conservative: the existing
combined STM32 image is flashed to each board, while a selectable Teensy
sketch chooses one CS/IRQ slot at compile time. It emits the unchanged 1188-byte
`ESK1` stream, so the existing GUI remains usable. This validates Module 0 or
Module 1 independently before simultaneous scheduling is introduced.

```powershell
& "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\tools\commands\flash_scalablity_test.cmd" COM9 0 all
& "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\tools\commands\flash_scalablity_test.cmd" COM9 1 all
```

Sources and the STM32-side test contract are in:

```text
teensy/applications/scalablity/
stm32/applications/scalablity/
```

This is not yet a two-module simultaneous bridge: it intentionally exercises
one physical module per upload. The next revision will add round-robin CS/IRQ
service and a module ID envelope without changing the inner `ESK1` payload.

#### Module 1 upload record (2026-08-15)

The selectable Module 1 Teensy image was compiled and uploaded successfully
through `COM9`. The build selected `SCALABILITY_MODULE_ID=1`, which maps to
Teensy CS pin 14 and IRQ pin 3. Reported image usage was 40,588 bytes Flash,
28,256 bytes RAM1 and 12,416 bytes RAM2. This confirms build/upload only; a
runtime frame capture and two-module simultaneous test are still pending.

#### Module 0 no-frame capture (2026-08-15)

After returning the selectable Teensy bridge to Module 0 (`CS=10`, `IRQ=2`),
the physical Module 0 wiring was tested for approximately five seconds on
`COM9`. USB diagnostics were received (`1,014` bytes and three `#ESKDBG`
lines), and the bridge observed IRQ activity, but no valid frame was accepted:

```text
ESK1 magic occurrences: 0
irq=8, ok=0, magic=8, header=0, crc=0
```

This separates the failure from the GUI and USB CDC path: Teensy is running,
but the Host SPI transaction is not returning a valid `ESK1` frame. The most
likely remaining causes are that the STM32 is not executing the expected
`combined_system` image, PB8/HOST_IRQ is held in its BOOT0/bootloader state, or
the STM32 SPI3 NSS/SCK/MISO path is not completing even though the connector
continuity appears correct. The next check is STM32-side: verify user-Flash
execution and `HOST_IRQ` idle-low, then inspect CS/SCK/MISO with a logic
analyser before changing the GUI or parser.

At the same time, `pyocd list` detected the DAPLink probe but reported
`Target: n/a`, so the STM32 user-Flash image could not be independently
confirmed during this capture.

### Python frame reader

The binary USB stream can be inspected with:

```text
hardware/new/firmware/teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/read_teensy_fsr.py
```

Install the dependency and run it with the Teensy COM port:

```powershell
py -m pip install pyserial
py read_teensy_fsr.py --port COM7
```

Use `--print-matrix` to print all 256 samples for each frame:

```powershell
py read_teensy_fsr.py --port COM7 --print-matrix
```

The reader resynchronises on the `0xA5` magic byte, checks frame length, decodes the little-endian sequence number and 16 x 16 uint16 samples, and reports sequence gaps and min/max values.

## Preparation

Install and verify the following tools before creating the STM32 firmware project:

1. Install Visual Studio Code.
2. Install the official `STM32CubeIDE for Visual Studio Code` extension pack.
3. Install the `CMake Tools` and `Cortex-Debug` extensions.
4. Install Python 3 and pyOCD:

   ```powershell
   py -m pip install -U pyocd
   ```

5. Install the STM32G4 CMSIS-Pack:

   ```powershell
   pyocd pack install stm32g474
   ```

6. Verify the available STM32G474 targets:

   ```powershell
   pyocd list --targets
   ```

   The expected target for an STM32G474CE device is usually `stm32g474cetx` or `stm32g474ceux`, depending on the package type.

7. Connect the DAPLink probe to SWDIO, SWCLK, GND, VTref, and preferably NRST, then verify the probe:

   ```powershell
   pyocd list
   ```

8. Create or import the STM32 project as a CMake project. The STM32CubeMX `.ioc` file should generate the CMake files used by Visual Studio Code.

9. Build the project in Visual Studio Code and keep the generated `.elf` and `.hex` files for programming and debugging.

10. Flash the STM32 target with pyOCD, using the exact target name reported by `pyocd list --targets`:

    ```powershell
    pyocd flash -t stm32g474cetx build\\main.hex
    ```

The traditional Eclipse-based STM32CubeIDE is not required for this workflow. It may still be used separately for project generation or debugging, but the Visual Studio Code workflow is based on CMake. Generic DAPLink probes are programmed through pyOCD or OpenOCD; the STM32 Visual Studio Code debugger should not be assumed to support every CMSIS-DAP probe directly.

### Windows build tools

The CMake project requires the following command-line tools:

| Tool | Purpose | Installation |
|---|---|---|
| CMake | Generates the native build files from `CMakeLists.txt` | Install the Windows x64 installer from <https://cmake.org/download/> and add CMake to `PATH` |
| ARM GNU Toolchain | Compiles and links firmware for the Cortex-M4 (`arm-none-eabi-*`) | Download the Windows x86_64 package from <https://developer.arm.com/downloads/-/arm-gnu-toolchain-downloads>, extract it, and add its `bin` directory to `PATH` |
| Ninja | Executes the CMake build graph | Download `ninja-win.zip` from <https://github.com/ninja-build/ninja/releases>, extract `ninja.exe`, and add its directory to `PATH` |

On the current Windows installation, the tools are installed under `D:\study\programming` and the PATH entries are:

```text
D:\study\programming\CMake\bin
D:\study\programming\ArmGnuToolchain\bin
D:\study\programming\Ninja
```

After changing PATH, restart VS Code and verify:

```powershell
cmake --version
arm-none-eabi-gcc --version
arm-none-eabi-gdb --version
ninja --version
```

Build the generated project from the `ESKIN_STM32` directory:

```powershell
cmake --preset Debug
cmake --build --preset Debug
```

Expected outputs include `build\Debug\ESKIN_STM32.elf` and `build\Debug\ESKIN_STM32.hex`.

## Development workflow

1. Define and version the STM32--Teensy SPI protocol.
2. Create/build the STM32G474 mainboard firmware.
3. Configure the STM32 host SPI as a slave with hardware NSS and DMA.
4. Acquire sensor data on the STM32 using timer-triggered sampling and double buffers.
5. Signal a ready frame to the Teensy with the IRQ line.
6. Implement the Teensy SPI-master bridge and USB CDC output.
7. Validate frame length, sequence number, endianness, and CRC.
8. Connect the output to the existing PC backend and Hardware Live visualization.
9. Flash/debug the STM32 through DAPLink/SWD.
10. Flash the Teensy bridge separately and run an end-to-end test.

## Recommended first milestone

Use a synthetic STM32 test frame (counter, ramp, or checkerboard) before enabling the real sensors. Confirm:

- SPI mode, clock polarity/phase, chip-select timing, and byte order;
- frame sequence continuity and CRC validation;
- Teensy USB transfer without text logs mixed into the binary stream;
- correct FSR orientation and accelerometer ordering in the PC visualization.

## Data-rate check

For a 16 x 16 two-layer FSR array plus 16 three-axis accelerometers, one frame is approximately 1,132 bytes in the current `ESKN` format. At 700 frames/s this is about 792 kB/s (6.34 Mbit/s) before SPI overhead. Bring up the link at a low rate first, then verify the required SPI clock and buffering at the target rate.

## DAPLink notes

- DAPLink/CMSIS-DAP: STM32 SWD flash, reset, and debug.
- Teensy Loader/Arduino CLI: Teensy 4.1 firmware upload.
- Runtime data path: Teensy USB CDC only.
- Do not assume that DAPLink mass-storage drag-and-drop supports every STM32 target; verify the target flash algorithm or use a CMSIS-DAP-compatible programming tool.

## STM32G474CET6 programming record

This section records the complete programming procedure used for the current `ESKIN_STM32` firmware.

### Hardware connection

The target board was powered externally and connected to the Jixin.pro CMSIS-DAP/DAPLink probe:

| DAPLink signal | STM32 target |
|---|---|
| SWDIO | PA13 |
| SWCLK | PA14 |
| GND | Mainboard GND |
| VTref | Mainboard 3.3 V |
| nRESET | STM32 dedicated NRST, LQFP48 pin 7 |

`PG10` was not used for the DAPLink reset connection; it is a separate ordinary GPIO on the mainboard.

### Build command

The project was built from:

```text
hardware/new/firmware/ESKIN_STM32/
```

The generated image was:

```text
build/Debug/ESKIN_STM32.elf
```

Build command:

```powershell
cmake --build --preset Debug
```

The build completed successfully and linked `ESKIN_STM32.elf`.

### Probe detection

The probe was detected as:

```text
jixin.pro CMSIS-DAP
Unique ID: LU_2022_8888
Target: stm32g474cetx
```

When `Target: n/a` was reported, the board power, VTref, SWD wiring, and dedicated NRST connection had to be checked before continuing.

### Initial programming attempt

The initial command used the default pyOCD SWD speed:

```powershell
pyocd flash -t stm32g474cetx build/Debug/ESKIN_STM32.elf
```

This attempt failed during board uninitialisation with:

```text
SWD/JTAG communication failure (Unexpected ACK '0')
```

This indicates an unstable SWD connection, target reset timing, or target power/clock issue. The ELF file and firmware image were not changed by this failed attempt.

### Successful programming retry

The successful retry used a lower SWD frequency and reset-under-connection:

```powershell
pyocd flash `
  -t stm32g474cetx `
  --frequency 100000 `
  --connect under-reset `
  build/Debug/ESKIN_STM32.elf
```

Successful pyOCD result:

```text
Erased 10240 bytes (5 sectors)
Programmed 9216 bytes (9 pages)
Identical 2048 bytes (2 pages)
```

The updated STM32 firmware containing the initial 16 x 16 FSR scan and SPI3-to-Teensy frame transport is now programmed on the target.

### Post-programming validation

The programming result confirms Flash access only. Runtime validation still requires:

1. Resetting the target and starting a Cortex-Debug session.
2. Confirming that `main()` is reached.
3. Checking `fsr_scan_status` and `fsr_frame[16][16]`.
4. Connecting the Teensy SPI bridge and validating `HOST_IRQ` plus the 515-byte frame.

## Software and integration test record

### Test run: 2026-07-23

| Test | Result | Evidence / interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| STM32 CMake rebuild | PASS | `cmake --build --preset Debug` reported `ninja: no work to do`; the previously built ELF is current. |
| STM32 source compile | PASS | The latest `ESKIN_STM32.elf` linked successfully after the retry-recovery change. |
| Python receiver syntax | PASS | `read_teensy_fsr.py` compiled successfully with Python. |
| Offline 515-byte protocol parser | PASS | A synthetic `0xA5` frame with sequence 7 and values 0..255 decoded correctly into a 16 x 16 matrix. |
| DAPLink target detection | NOT PASS | `pyocd list` detected `jixin.pro CMSIS-DAP_LU`, but currently reported `Target: n/a`; the board must be powered and SWD/VTref wiring rechecked before debug testing. |
| Teensy COM9 open | PASS | Python opened `COM9` successfully, confirming the USB serial port is available. |
| Runtime frame reception | NOT PASS | Zero bytes were received during a 3-second COM9 read. No valid `0xA5` frame was observed. |

### Issues and attempts

1. The first Python receiver attempt failed because COM9 was occupied by Arduino Serial Plotter. Closing the plotter released the port.
2. A receiver process was then left running after a tool timeout; the specific `read_teensy_fsr.py` processes were identified and terminated to release COM9.
3. After COM9 was available, the receiver opened successfully but received no bytes. This indicates that the Teensy USB path is present, but the STM32-to-Teensy frame trigger has not been observed.
4. The current `main.c` retries after ADC/host timeouts instead of stopping permanently. However, no runtime frame can be produced until the STM32 completes the MAX11633 scan and raises `HOST_IRQ`.
5. The latest STM32 image was rebuilt and successfully reflashed with pyOCD at 100 kHz using `under-reset`; this does not by itself prove that the ADC or SPI3 link is working.

### Immediate next diagnostic

Use Cortex-Debug to inspect `fsr_scan_status` and `HOST_IRQ`. If `fsr_scan_status` is `HAL_TIMEOUT`, debug the MAX11633 wiring first (`PB0` CS, `PB10` EOC, `PA5/PA6/PA7` SPI1). If the status is `HAL_OK` but `HOST_IRQ` never rises, debug the SPI3/Teensy wiring (`PB8`, `PA15`, `PB3/PB4/PB5`).

### Follow-up check: 2026-07-23

The latest host-side check found:

```text
pyocd list: Jixin.pro CMSIS-DAP detected, Target: n/a
COM9: Teensy USB serial device detected
COM10: DAPLink USB serial device detected
```

Because the target is currently `n/a`, runtime STM32 variables could not be inspected in this check. The source review confirms that `HOST_SendFrame()` is called only when `FSR_ScanFrame()` returns `HAL_OK`; an ADC EOC timeout prevents any SPI3 frame and therefore produces zero bytes on COM9. Restore target detection first, then inspect `fsr_scan_status` and `HOST_IRQ` with Cortex-Debug.

A further low-speed recovery attempt was made with `pyocd flash -t stm32g474cetx --frequency 100000 --connect under-reset`. It failed during `DebugPortSetup` with `Unexpected ACK '5'`, confirming that the current issue is target-side SWD communication or target power/reset wiring rather than the ELF image.

A final retry at an even lower 10 kHz SWD frequency with `under-reset` also failed during board uninitialisation with `Unexpected ACK '5'`. Further frequency reduction is unlikely to help until target power, VTref, NRST, SWDIO, and SWCLK wiring are physically verified.

### SPI pattern isolation test: 2026-07-24

To isolate the STM32-to-Teensy electrical path without involving the ADC or FSR scan, a new `SPI_PATTERN_TEST` directory was added beside the production firmware. The original STM32 and Teensy files were not modified. The test uses one SPI byte, `0x55` (`01010101`), because SPI transfers complete bytes rather than seven-bit strings. The STM32 test snippet configures the existing SPI3 slave to return `0x55` when the Teensy supplies NSS and clock. The Teensy Arduino sketch acts as SPI master at 100 kHz, clocks one byte every 100 ms, and prints the received byte and PASS/FAIL over USB serial at 115200 baud.

Validation is pending hardware execution. A PASS result (`RX: 0x55 bits=01010101 PASS`) confirms SPI3 wiring, mode, chip-select timing, and Teensy USB output only; it does not validate MAX11633, MUX1, or the production 515-byte frame. The STM32 test file is intentionally an integration snippet and is not included in the production CMake target.

### Power-domain and SCK connector diagnosis: 2026-07-25

#### Resolved fault

The previous condition in which the STM32 HOST_SCK input remained low was traced to a poor connector solder joint. The connector was resoldered, after which the signal at the actual STM32 `PB3/HOST_SCK` pin changed correctly between low and high.

The earlier report called this pin `PD3`. In the mainboard v2.2 schematic and the STM32 firmware it is `PB3` (STM32G474CETx LQFP48 pin 40). The GPIO mirror output is `PB4/HOST_MISO` (pin 41).

#### Circuit review

The v2.2 mainboard schematic shows the following power topology:

- HOST connector `J1` pin 10 is `FPGA_3V3_IN`.
- `FPGA_3V3_IN` passes through `R1 = 0 ohm` directly to the global mainboard `+3.3V` rail.
- SWD header pin 1 is also connected directly to the same global `+3.3V` rail.
- There is no ideal diode, load switch, series isolation diode, or power multiplexer between these two inputs.
- Therefore, connecting both Teensy 3.3 V and DAPLink 3V3 makes two regulator outputs drive the same rail directly.
- The Jixin CMSIS-DAP documentation identifies its `3V3` pin as a 3.3 V power output. It is not required for SWD when the target has its own power; SWD programming requires GND, RST, SWDIO, and SWCLK.

The current SCK mirror test software was also reviewed:

- Teensy GPIO 13 is an output and GPIO 12 is an input.
- STM32 PB3 is a no-pull GPIO input.
- STM32 PB4 is a push-pull GPIO output that copies PB3 every 1 ms.

No software direction conflict was found in this specific mirror test.

#### Recorded power and GPIO observations

| Case | Connections | Measurements and serial result | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1.1 | DAPLink USB and Teensy USB both connected to the PC; DAPLink 3V3 connected to the mainboard | `SCK_LOW->0 SCK_HIGH->0 FAIL`; Teensy 3.3 V approximately 3.0 V; mainboard 3.3 V input stable at approximately 3.0 V; PB3 switches 0-3.0 V; PB4 remains approximately 0.3 V | DAPLink and Teensy 3.3 V regulator outputs are hard-paralleled through the mainboard. The 3.0 V rail and PB4 at 0.3 V are invalid. PB4 is either not being driven because the MCU is not running reliably, or the PB4/MISO path is clamped/open. |
| 1.2 | DAPLink USB and Teensy USB both connected to the PC; DAPLink 3V3 disconnected from the mainboard | `SCK_LOW->0 SCK_HIGH->0 FAIL`; Teensy rail approximately 3.2 V; mainboard 3.3 V input approximately 2.9 V; PB3 switches 0-3.2 V; PB4 remains approximately 0.3 V | Removing the second 3.3 V source improves the Teensy rail, but the 0.3 V drop to the mainboard and PB4 remaining at 0.3 V indicate excessive load, a high-resistance power connection, target brownout/reset, or a separate PB4/MISO connection fault. SWD signal pins can also parasitically feed an inadequately powered target. |
| 2 | Only Teensy USB connected to the PC; the unpowered DAPLink 3V3 wire remains connected to the mainboard | `SCK_LOW->0 SCK_HIGH->1 PASS`; Teensy maximum approximately 2.2 V; mainboard 3.3 V input switches 0-2.1 V; `PB3/HOST_SCK` switches 0 to approximately -0.8 V; `PB4/HOST_MISO` switches 0-0.2 V | This PASS is not valid proof of correct operation. The unpowered DAPLink 3.3 V output is being back-powered and/or loading the rail. A supply rail that follows the test signal is a severe power integrity fault. The STM32 can repeatedly brown out and accidentally cross the Teensy logic threshold, producing an apparent PASS. |
| 3 | Only Teensy USB connected to the PC; DAPLink 3V3 disconnected | `SCK_LOW->1 SCK_HIGH->1 FAIL`; maximum rail approximately 2.57 V; mainboard 3.3 V input switches 0-2.5 V; `PB3/HOST_SCK` switches approximately 0.3 to -0.65 V; `PB4/HOST_MISO` switches 0-2.57 V | The Teensy supply still collapses without DAPLink. This indicates an overloaded/shorted 3.3 V rail, a high-resistance/intermittent 3.3 V connector contact, incorrect connector orientation, or parasitic powering through a GPIO protection diode. A value near 2.5-2.6 V is consistent with a 3.2 V GPIO feeding an unpowered rail through a diode drop. |

The user subsequently confirmed that the measured nodes were PB3 and PB4; the earlier PA3/PA4 wording was only a naming error. These readings therefore apply directly to HOST_SCK and HOST_MISO.

#### Consolidated diagnosis

The original PB3/SCK problem was a connector solder fault and is now resolved. The present primary fault is power distribution, not SPI timing:

1. DAPLink 3V3 and Teensy 3.3 V must not be connected to the mainboard simultaneously.
2. An unpowered DAPLink must not remain attached through its 3V3 output.
3. The mainboard 3.3 V rail is not stable when powered from Teensy. It must not switch with SCK or fall to 2.1-3.0 V.
4. The single PASS in case 2 occurred under an invalid collapsing supply and must be treated as a false/accidental PASS.
5. The negative reported voltage requires oscilloscope verification with the probe ground connected directly to mainboard GND. If it is a real excursion rather than a reference/probe artefact, it indicates undershoot or ground bounce outside the intended 0-3.3 V range.

#### Safe next test

Do not continue the PB3-to-PB4 logic test until the power rail passes the following isolation test:

1. Disconnect DAPLink 3V3 permanently from the mainboard. For SWD, leave only GND, SWDIO, SWCLK, and dedicated NRST connected.
2. Disconnect all Teensy GPIO signal wires from the mainboard.
3. With all power removed, measure resistance from mainboard `+3.3V` to GND and inspect the recently repaired connector, especially pin 10 (`FPGA_3V3_IN`), for a bridge or high-resistance joint.
4. Power the mainboard from one current-limited, regulated 3.3 V source at `FPGA_3V3_IN`. Do not parallel it with either DAPLink or Teensy 3.3 V. Record the current consumption.
5. Confirm a stable 3.3 V at the source, J1 pin 10, and the STM32 VDD pins. The rail must remain stable while no GPIO is connected.
6. If the source remains 3.3 V but J1/VDD is lower, repair the power connector/trace. If the source itself enters current limit, locate the board short or overloaded device before reconnecting Teensy.
7. After stable power is established, connect common GND first, then SCK only, then MISO. Repeat the static 1 s low/high mirror test.
8. Measure PB4 directly at STM32 pin 41 and at the HOST_MISO connector. If pin 41 follows PB3 but the connector does not, repair the PB4-to-connector route. If pin 41 remains low with stable VDD, NRST high, and the mirror image confirmed running, investigate firmware execution or damage/clamping at PB4.

#### Firmware-aware analysis of the image under test

The firmware build and flash history were audited together with the power measurements.

##### Version state

- The actual build working copy is `D:\tmp\ESKIN_STM32_PATTERN`.
- Its archived `main.c` and the working-copy `main.c` are byte-identical.
- The latest ELF was built on 2026-07-25 at 15:09:01.
- Latest ELF SHA-256:

```text
D0AEAFCC181D5C9DBA3927BBC35269AB5E1E82E238BF8CBB2569CCD44D3A9C8C
```

- Disassembly of this ELF confirms that the final machine code configures PB3 as a no-pull input, PB4 as a push-pull output, reads PB3, writes that state to PB4, delays 1 ms, and repeats.
- The latest no-pull ELF was built, but the subsequent pyOCD attempt waited for a probe and did not report a successful flash. The last positively recorded flash was the earlier SCK mirror image. Therefore, until Flash is read back or the latest ELF is reflashed successfully, the MCU should be assumed to contain the earlier mirror variant with the PB3 internal pull-down.
- The pull-down difference does not explain PB4 remaining near 0.3 V. Teensy GPIO 13 actively drives PB3, and PB3 was measured switching between low and high. Both mirror variants configure PB4 as push-pull output and should produce a full logic level after the mirror loop is reached.

Direct read-back was not possible during this review because no CMSIS-DAP probe was detected. The system-default `D:\app\python\Scripts\pyocd.exe` installation also currently lacks the `colorama` and `psutil` dependencies, so its environment must be repaired before a binary read-back comparison.

##### Startup path relevant to PB4

The mirror operation is not the first code executed. The current image performs:

```text
HAL_Init
  -> SystemClock_Config
  -> MX_GPIO_Init
  -> MX_SPI1_Init
  -> MX_SPI3_Init
  -> reconfigure PB3/PB4 as GPIO
  -> PB3-to-PB4 mirror loop
```

`SystemClock_Config`, `MX_SPI1_Init`, and `MX_SPI3_Init` call `Error_Handler()` on failure. `Error_Handler()` disables interrupts and loops forever, with no heartbeat or error output. If the 3.3 V rail collapses, the MCU may repeatedly brown out, reset, or stop in an initialisation failure before PB4 is configured as a push-pull output. In that state PB4 can be high-impedance and a meter may report an invalid residual value such as 0.3 V.

This code path matches the measurements:

- PB3 switching proves that the Teensy signal reaches the STM32 package pin after the connector repair.
- It does not prove that the STM32 reached the mirror loop.
- PB4 at approximately 0.3 V is consistent with the MCU not reaching the PB4 reconfiguration, repeated brownout/reset, an external clamp, or a PB4 route fault.
- The collapsing 3.3 V rail is therefore sufficient to invalidate the GPIO test even though the mirror source code and ELF machine code are correct.

##### Additional reset configuration issue

The current `.ioc` contains:

```text
PG10-NRST.Signal=GPIO_Output
```

Generated code labels PG10-NRST as `NRST`, writes it high, and attempts to initialise it as push-pull output. On STM32G4, the physical PG10-NRST pad mode is selected by the `NRST_MODE` option bytes. With the production-default reset mode, GPIO functionality is unavailable and the external active-low reset remains functional. If `NRST_MODE` is ever changed to GPIO mode, DAPLink nRESET can no longer reset the target and the generated push-pull-high configuration can conflict with an external reset driver.

For reliable DAPLink operation, reserve PG10-NRST as the reset input/output and remove its `GPIO_Output` configuration in the `.ioc`. This is a genuine configuration defect, but it does not by itself explain PB4 at 0.3 V if the option bytes remain in their default reset mode.

##### Code-aware next validation

After the mainboard has one stable 3.3 V source:

1. Restore pyOCD operation and connect the probe without its 3V3 output.
2. Reflash the latest no-pull ELF and record a successful verification, or read back Flash and compare it with the SHA-256-identified ELF.
3. Use a minimal GPIO-only diagnostic that does not initialise SPI1 or SPI3 before the mirror test.
4. Add a separate heartbeat output after PB3/PB4 initialisation. A heartbeat proves that the MCU reached and remains in the mirror loop; absence of heartbeat distinguishes reset/init failure from a PB4 trace fault.
5. Correct `PG10-NRST` in the `.ioc` before regenerating production code.

#### Ideal behaviour with the available Teensy-only supply

The user does not currently have an independent current-limited bench supply. If the Teensy 4.1 must temporarily power the mainboard, DAPLink 3V3 must remain disconnected. The ideal test configuration is:

- Teensy USB connected to the PC.
- Teensy 3.3 V connected to mainboard J1 pin 10 (`FPGA_3V3_IN`).
- Teensy GND connected to mainboard J1 pin 8 or pin 9.
- Teensy GPIO 13 connected to mainboard HOST_SCK/PB3.
- Teensy GPIO 12 connected to mainboard HOST_MISO/PB4.
- DAPLink 3V3 disconnected; preferably disconnect the complete DAPLink during this GPIO-only test.

Expected electrical behaviour:

| Node | Expected low phase | Expected high phase |
|---|---:|---:|
| Teensy 3.3 V | Stable approximately 3.2-3.3 V | Stable approximately 3.2-3.3 V |
| Mainboard J1 pin 10 / STM32 VDD | Stable approximately 3.2-3.3 V | Stable approximately 3.2-3.3 V |
| Teensy GPIO 13 | Approximately 0 V for 1 s | Approximately 3.2-3.3 V for 1 s |
| STM32 PB3/HOST_SCK | Approximately 0 V for 1 s | Approximately 3.2-3.3 V for 1 s |
| STM32 PB4/HOST_MISO | Approximately 0 V | Approximately STM32 VDD, following PB3 within approximately 1 ms |
| Teensy GPIO 12 | Logic 0 | Logic 1 |
| STM32 PG10-NRST | Stable high, close to STM32 VDD | Stable high, close to STM32 VDD |

Expected serial output at 115200 baud:

```text
Teensy HOST_SCK retest: GPIO12 should follow GPIO13
SCK_LOW->0 SCK_HIGH->1  PASS
SCK_LOW->0 SCK_HIGH->1  PASS
...
```

The mainboard 3.3 V rail must not change with SCK. PB3 or PB4 must not become negative. `PB4 approximately 0.3 V`, `SCK_LOW->1`, `SCK_HIGH->0`, a mainboard rail below approximately 3.1 V, or a cycling rail are all invalid results.

The currently compiled no-pull image and the most likely currently flashed pull-down mirror image have the same ideal serial result. The only difference is the weak internal PB3 pull-down; it cannot hold PB3 low against an actively driven Teensy GPIO 13.

Because the Teensy 4.1 external 3.3 V budget is limited, disconnect power immediately if its 3.3 V pin or the mainboard rail falls below approximately 3.1 V, cycles with GPIO activity, or any component becomes warm. Do not reconnect DAPLink 3V3 to raise the voltage.

#### Root-cause analysis of the Teensy 3.3 V voltage drop

A normal PB3/PB4 GPIO transition cannot pull a healthy 3.3 V rail from approximately 3.2 V down to 2.2-2.6 V. The observations indicate a power-path fault or excessive load.

Ranked causes:

1. **Confirmed source contention/back-power when DAPLink 3V3 is attached.** DAPLink 3V3 and Teensy 3.3 V are connected to the same mainboard rail without isolation. When both probes are USB-powered, their regulators can source current into each other. When DAPLink USB is removed but its 3V3 wire remains connected, Teensy can reverse-power the unpowered DAPLink. This matches the worst approximately 2.2 V case.
2. **Probable mainboard overload or partial short.** With DAPLink completely removed, the Teensy rail still fell to approximately 2.57 V. If the voltage measured directly at the Teensy 3.3 V pin also falls, the Teensy regulator is being overloaded or entering current/thermal protection.
3. **Probable high-resistance 3.3 V connection.** A Teensy-side value near 3.2 V and a mainboard-side value near 2.9 V means approximately 0.3 V is being lost in the wire, connector, solder joint, or PCB route. The already-confirmed HOST connector solder fault makes J1 pin 10 another high-priority inspection point.
4. **Possible GPIO parasitic powering.** If J1 pin 10 is open, intermittent, reversed, or highly resistive, a high PB3/CS/MOSI signal can feed the STM32 VDD rail through an input protection diode. A rail around 2.5-2.6 V is compatible with a 3.2 V GPIO minus a diode drop. A mainboard 3.3 V rail that follows SCK is strong evidence for this condition.
5. **Possible USB input or measurement-reference problem.** If Teensy VUSB/VIN also falls, the USB cable, hub, or PC port is limiting current. Negative PB3 measurements can also be exaggerated by a wrong oscilloscope ground or long probe ground lead, but this cannot explain a confirmed drop measured directly on the Teensy 3.3 V pin.

Teensy-only discrimination test:

| Step | Connections | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| A | Teensy USB only; nothing connected to 3.3 V or GPIO | Record Teensy 3.3 V and VUSB/VIN. This is the no-load baseline. |
| B | Connect only Teensy GND and 3.3 V to mainboard GND and J1 pin 10; no GPIO and no DAPLink | If Teensy 3.3 V falls, the mainboard load/short is the primary problem. If Teensy stays stable but J1/VDD is lower, the power connector/path has excessive resistance. |
| C | If step B is stable, add GPIO13-to-PB3 only | If the rail now falls or follows GPIO13, the board was not correctly powered through J1 pin 10 or PB3 is shorted/clamped. |
| D | If step C is stable, add PB4-to-GPIO12 | If the rail now falls, inspect the PB4/MISO route and confirm GPIO12 remains configured as input. |

Also measure Teensy VUSB/VIN during step B. Stable approximately 5 V with a collapsing 3.3 V output points to Teensy regulator overload; a collapsing VUSB/VIN points to the USB cable/port or upstream supply.

#### Live boot-source diagnosis: PB4 stuck near 0.3 V (2026-07-25)

Observed condition:

- DAPLink 3.3 V was disconnected from the mainboard.
- The Teensy remained the only board power source.
- PB4/HOST_MISO stayed near 0.3 V instead of mirroring PB3/HOST_SCK.

Live inspection through DAPLink, without initially resetting or flashing the MCU, produced the following results:

- No residual `pyocd`, GDB server, or GDB process was running.
- The CMSIS-DAP probe was detected normally.
- The STM32 core was running, but `PC = 0x1FFF3B26` and `VTOR = 0x1FFF0000`.
- These addresses are inside the STM32 system-memory bootloader, not the user application in internal Flash.
- The internal Flash still contained a valid vector table (`SP = 0x20020000`, reset handler `0x08001C61`).
- GPIOB showed PB3 and PB4 both in alternate-function mode rather than the intended test configuration.
- Therefore PB4 was not being driven by the mirror firmware. The measured approximately 0.3 V was a high-impedance residual/clamped voltage, not a valid GPIO logic-high output.

The option-byte value was read as `FLASH_OPTR = 0xFFEFF8AA`, including:

- `nSWBOOT0 = 1`: the boot source is selected from the physical BOOT0 pin at reset.
- `nBOOT1 = 1`: a high BOOT0 level selects system memory.
- On this board, PB8 is shared with `HOST_IRQ` and the STM32G4 BOOT0 function.

Confirmed cause:

- PB8/HOST_IRQ was sampled high or was insufficiently defined during the earlier power-up/reset sequence.
- The MCU consequently started the ROM system bootloader instead of the user Flash firmware.
- Disconnecting DAPLink 3.3 V removed one power source, but did not by itself change the already sampled boot mode.

Recovery performed:

1. Confirmed PB8 was low at the time of the recovery.
2. Issued one normal STM32 reset through DAPLink; no erase or reprogramming was performed.
3. After reset, the program counter moved to `0x080004F0`, confirming execution from user Flash.
4. GPIO configuration after boot was verified:
   - PB3: input with pull-down.
   - PB4: GPIO output.
5. The target was resumed and left running.

Expected result after recovery:

- Teensy drives SCK/PB3 low: PB4 should read low.
- Teensy drives SCK/PB3 high: PB4 should read high.
- The Teensy test should report `SCK_LOW->0 SCK_HIGH->1 PASS`.

Permanent prevention:

- Preferred hardware solution: fit an approximately 10 kOhm pull-down from PB8/HOST_IRQ/BOOT0 to GND so the boot pin is defined during every power-up. This draws only about 0.33 mA when STM32 later drives HOST_IRQ high.
- Firmware-option solution: program the option bytes so boot from main Flash does not depend on the physical PB8 level (`nSWBOOT0 = 0`, with the software BOOT0 selection set for main Flash). This is a persistent device configuration change and should only be performed deliberately.
- Do not actively drive PB8 low from Teensy after normal startup because PB8 is also an STM32 HOST_IRQ output and active opposing outputs would create contention.

If PB4 still measures approximately 0.3 V after the confirmed Flash boot, the remaining check is hardware-only: disconnect Teensy GPIO12/MISO and compare the voltage directly at STM32 PB4 (package pin 41) with the HOST_MISO connector pad. A low voltage at only one point would distinguish an open connection from an external clamp.

#### CLK resolution and complete SPI pattern restoration (2026-07-25)

Final CLK result:

- The user confirmed that the HOST_SCK/CLK problem was resolved.
- This closes the PB3/HOST_SCK connector-path diagnostic.
- The GPIO-only PB3-to-PB4 mirror programs were removed from the active isolated test sources.

Restored STM32 test:

- Project source: `SPI_PATTERN_TEST/ESKIN_STM32_PATTERN/Core/Src/main.c`.
- SPI3 remains an 8-bit, MSB-first, Mode-0 slave with hardware NSS.
- PA15 = SPI3_NSS, PB3 = SPI3_SCK, PB4 = SPI3_MISO, and PB5 = SPI3_MOSI, all using AF6.
- PB8/HOST_IRQ is low at idle, goes high immediately before the one-byte blocking transfer, and returns low after completion or timeout.
- Each transaction returns `0x55` (`01010101`) through `HAL_SPI_TransmitReceive()`.
- A 1000 ms STM32 timeout, `HAL_SPI_Abort()` recovery, and a 10 ms IRQ-low interval are retained.

Restored Teensy test:

- Sketch: `SPI_PATTERN_TEST/teensy_spi_pattern_receiver/teensy_spi_pattern_receiver.ino`.
- Teensy 4.1 is the SPI master: GPIO10 = CS, GPIO13 = SCK, GPIO12 = MISO, GPIO11 = MOSI, and GPIO2 = HOST_IRQ.
- SPI parameters are 100 kHz, MSB first, and Mode 0.
- GPIO2 is configured as `INPUT_PULLDOWN` to reduce the risk of PB8/BOOT0 floating during an STM32 reset.
- The receiver waits for IRQ high, applies 1 ms setup delays, clocks one byte, waits for IRQ low, and prints the received byte over USB serial.

Build and flashing record:

1. The long-path OneDrive build directory was missing `build.ninja`.
2. Reconfiguration in that directory failed because the generated compiler-test object path exceeded the Windows/CMake safe object-path length.
3. The restored STM32 source was synchronised to the existing short-path working copy at `D:\tmp\ESKIN_STM32_PATTERN`.
4. The short-path CMake/Ninja build passed:
   - RAM used: 1792 bytes.
   - Flash used: 10060 bytes.
   - The unused FSR-production helper warnings are expected because this is an isolated SPI test.
5. The resulting ELF was flashed with pyOCD at 100 kHz using `under-reset`:
   - Erased: 10240 bytes in 5 sectors.
   - Programmed: 10240 bytes in 10 pages.
   - Identical: 0 bytes.
6. A post-flash live check briefly halted and then resumed the target:
   - `PC = 0x08000794`, proving execution from user Flash.
   - GPIOB MODER/AFR confirmed PB3, PB4, and PB5 in SPI3 AF6 mode.
7. The Teensy sketch compiled successfully with `teensy:avr` core 1.62.0 and was uploaded to the detected Teensy 4.1 at `Port_#0004.Hub_#0002`.

Artifact identities:

```text
STM32 main.c SHA-256:
5B49E9174AE3ED018EB09FE24F3CB37FFDF9E8E504B43CEB22D5E6977E217968

STM32 ELF SHA-256:
5ABC30F14BFF51A4FF2CD47AC6A8EA678463BBC9EC14FA6EF086BDD9D2387946

Teensy receiver SHA-256:
5C92CCAC37B33AD41C131CB061164B62B0271157EDEDE3371A89849A880B7155
```

End-to-end result:

COM9 was opened for approximately five seconds after both uploads. The startup line was correct and eight consecutive successful transactions were captured:

```text
Teensy SPI pattern receiver: expecting STM32 0x55
RX: 0x55  bits=01010101  PASS
RX: 0x55  bits=01010101  PASS
RX: 0x55  bits=01010101  PASS
RX: 0x55  bits=01010101  PASS
RX: 0x55  bits=01010101  PASS
RX: 0x55  bits=01010101  PASS
RX: 0x55  bits=01010101  PASS
RX: 0x55  bits=01010101  PASS
```

Conclusion:

- HOST_IRQ, HOST_nCS, HOST_SCK, HOST_MISO, SPI Mode 0 timing, the STM32 SPI3 slave, the Teensy SPI master, and Teensy USB serial output are now validated together.
- This test does not yet validate SPI1/MAX11633, MUX1 scanning, FSR samples, or the 515-byte production frame.
- PB8/BOOT0 still requires a permanent hardware pull-down or a deliberate option-byte configuration for guaranteed cold-boot selection.

## Update policy

After any firmware, protocol, pin assignment, build, flashing, or PC-parser change, update this file with:

- date and change summary;
- affected board/firmware version;
- protocol changes and compatibility notes;
- validation result and any known issue;
- next action.

## 2026-07-25 — Return to complete 16 x 16 FSR acquisition

### Firmware configuration restored

The production acquisition path is active again:

```text
FSR layer 1
  -> MUX1 (PA0..PA3 address, PA4 active-low enable)
  -> MAX11633 ADC1 (PB0 CS, PB10 active-low EOC, SPI1)
  -> STM32 16 x 16 frame
  -> SPI3 slave (PA15 NSS, PB3 SCK, PB4 MISO, PB5 MOSI)
  -> Teensy 4.1 SPI master
  -> Teensy USB CDC
  -> read_teensy_fsr.py on the PC
```

The v2.2 netlist confirms the analog topology: MUX1 selects one row and applies
the 3.3 V excitation through 1 kOhm, while ADC1 simultaneously measures the 16
column nets. Each column has a 10 kOhm pull-down. ADC channel order is reversed
at the column FPC: AIN0 maps to FPC pin 16 and AIN15 maps to FPC pin 1.

The host frame remains 515 bytes:

```text
byte 0       : 0xA5 frame marker
bytes 1..2   : uint16 little-endian sequence number
bytes 3..514 : 256 uint16 little-endian samples in row-major order
```

### Changes made before the test

STM32:

- Added explicit MAX11633 reset (`0x10`) and setup (`0x68`) commands.
- Used scan command `0xF8` for AIN0 through AIN15.
- Corrected each MUX row operation to address -> enable -> 1 ms settle ->
  convert/read -> disable.
- Added volatile scan stage, last-error stage, active-row, completed-scan,
  completed-frame, and error counters for SWD inspection.
- Kept every ADC result masked to the valid 12-bit range.

Teensy:

- Restored `INPUT_PULLDOWN` on GPIO 2 because STM32 PB8 is both HOST_IRQ and
  BOOT0.
- Used SPI Mode 0, MSB first, and 100 kHz for validated bring-up.
- Added 1 ms guard time before and after asserting chip select.
- Added a timeout while waiting for HOST_IRQ to be released.
- Removed the per-frame USB `Serial.flush()` stall.

PC receiver:

- Rejects false frame alignment if any decoded ADC sample exceeds `0x0FFF`.
- Supports finite tests with `--frames` and `--max-time`.
- Reports sequence gaps and can print the complete 16 x 16 matrix.

The MAX11633 data sheet was rechecked after a review suggested using `0xF9`.
For this ADC, conversion-register bit 0 is explicitly "don't care"; it is not a
single-ended/differential selector. Both `0xF8` and `0xF9` therefore select
CHSEL=15 and SCAN=00, but `0xF8` is retained. Scan mode 00 returns one result
for every channel from AIN0 through AIN15.

### Initial runtime failure and diagnosis

The first PC capture returned zero valid frames. Live SWD variables showed that
the ADC/MUX work was completing, but every host transfer was timing out:

```text
fsr_completed_scans  = 333
fsr_completed_frames = 0
fsr_last_error_stage = HOST_TRANSFER
fsr_error_count       = 332
```

Teensy USB diagnostics alternated between `WAIT_IRQ` and `BAD_MAGIC 0x00`.
This isolated the fault to the 515-byte STM32-to-Teensy transaction rather than
the MAX11633 scan.

A normal STM32 reset was then issued while Teensy GPIO 2 held PB8/BOOT0 low.
This returned the MCU to a clean user-Flash/SPI state. No option bytes were
changed.

### Successful end-to-end result

A bounded raw COM9 read contained exactly one diagnostic line followed by one
complete frame:

```text
23 20 ... 0d 0a  a5 94 01 ...
                     ^ sequence 0x0194 = 404
```

The PC receiver then captured 10 consecutive frames:

```text
sequence: 417, 418, 419, 420, 421, 422, 423, 424, 425, 426
valid frames: 10/10
sequence gaps: 0
sample range across the run: 0..3681
invalid samples above 4095: 0
```

A subsequent complete matrix frame was also decoded:

```text
sequence: 484
minimum: 0
maximum: 3665
first sample: 6
last sample: 2
```

### Host-transfer throughput correction

The first apparently successful version was still not acceptable for continuous
operation. A 30-second aggregate run produced only 29 frames:

```text
sequence: 456..484
gaps: 0
sample range: 0..3622
rate: 0.96 frame/s
```

This rate matched the 1000 ms STM32 host timeout. Teensy was receiving all 515
outbound bytes, but `HAL_SPI_TransmitReceive()` still waited for the unused
full-duplex MOSI receive side and frequently timed out.

An interrupt-armed `HAL_SPI_TransmitReceive_IT()` experiment was built and
flashed to test ready-before-IRQ ordering. It was rejected after runtime
validation:

```text
Teensy: BAD_MAGIC 0x00 / BAD_MAGIC 0x01
STM32 scans: 76
STM32 completed frames: 0
STM32 errors: 75
```

The SPI3 TX FIFO did contain a correct `0xA5` frame, but the HAL interrupt/FIFO
path did not sustain this transfer reliably in the current configuration. All
SPI3 interrupt changes were removed.

The final implementation uses blocking transmit-only mode:

```c
HAL_SPI_Transmit(&hspi3, host_tx_frame, HOST_FRAME_BYTES,
                 HOST_SPI_TIMEOUT);
```

This exactly matches the active data direction: STM32 MISO to Teensy MISO.
Teensy MOSI remains physically connected but its dummy bytes are not used by
the STM32 application. HAL clears the receive overrun generated by the unused
two-line receive path when the transmit operation closes.

Final build and flash:

```text
Flash used: 13288 bytes
pyOCD: 100 kHz, under-reset
erased: 14336 bytes / 7 sectors
programmed: 13312 bytes / 13 pages
flash errors: none
```

```text
elapsed=3.989s rate=25.07 frame/s
```

The PC receiver now supports `--summary-only` so long tests produce one
aggregate result instead of hundreds of per-frame lines.

### Final NRST cleanup and runtime state

PG10-NRST was incorrectly listed in the `.ioc` file and generated application
code as a push-pull GPIO output. The pin is now left as the dedicated hardware
reset input:

- removed `PG10-NRST.Signal=GPIO_Output` and its label/lock entries;
- removed PG10 from the configured GPIO pin list;
- removed the generated `NRST_Pin` macros;
- removed the GPIOG clock, output-level write, and GPIO initialisation from
  `MX_GPIO_Init()`.

The final build used 2832 bytes of RAM and 13220 bytes of Flash. pyOCD at
100 kHz under reset erased 10240 bytes, programmed 9216 bytes, and found 4096
bytes already identical.

Final-image validation:

```text
Captured 100 valid frame(s).
Summary: sequence=318..417 gaps=0 samples=0..3680
elapsed=3.969s rate=25.20 frame/s
```

An SWD `halt` was then intentionally observed to interrupt an active
`HOST_TRANSFER`. The following stream fell to approximately 0.96 frame/s even
though its data remained valid. This is a debugger-induced peripheral phase
loss, not an ADC failure. A normal reset restored the final running state:

```text

Summary: sequence=293..312 gaps=0 samples=0..3701
elapsed=0.773s rate=25.88 frame/s
```

Do not halt or single-step the MCU while measuring continuous SPI throughput.
If a debug halt occurs during HOST transfer, issue a normal reset before
resuming acquisition.

## 2026-07-25 — Live hexagonal FSR heatmap

Created:

```text
teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/live_fsr_heatmap.py
```

The display reuses the 16 x 16 binary parser and maps its 256 cells into the
same normalized hexagonal outline used by the PCB/frontend geometry. The colour
scale represents the raw 12-bit ADC code from 0 to 4095.

Features:

- dedicated background serial thread so the plot remains responsive;
- latest-frame queue so drawing cannot delay Teensy acquisition;
- frame number, measured frame rate, sequence-gap count, range, and peak cell;
- pointer hover showing raw row, column, ADC code, AIN channel, and column FPC
  pin;
- fixed 0--4095 scale by default, with optional per-frame `--autoscale`;
- optional `--flip-rows` and `--flip-columns` for matching the installed PCB
  orientation without changing raw matrix labels;
- `--demo` mode for checking the geometry without hardware.

Dependencies:

```powershell
python -m pip install pyserial numpy matplotlib
```

Live command:

```powershell
cd .\teensy41\ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE
python -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9
```

Useful alternatives:

```powershell
python -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9 --autoscale
python -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9 --flip-columns
python -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --demo
```

Close Arduino Serial Monitor before starting the heatmap because only one
Windows process can own COM9 at a time.

Validation:

```text
HEATMAP_GEOMETRY_TEST_PASS
polygons: 256
outline vertices: 6

LIVE_FRAME_TEST_PASS
sequence: 612
shape: 16 x 16
sample range: 0..3585
sequence gaps: 0
```

### Fixed dark-column observation

The first hardware heatmap showed two fixed dark bands spanning nearly every
row. Because all polygons and grid edges remain visible, these are valid cells
whose ADC codes are close to zero, not missing graphics.

With the default orientation (no row/column flip), the bands correspond
approximately to:

```text
raw matrix column   ADC channel   column FPC pin
C2                  AIN2          14
C3                  AIN3          13
C10                 AIN10          6
C11                 AIN11          5
C12                 AIN12          4
C13                 AIN13          3
```

Each column net has a 10 kOhm pull-down. Therefore a disconnected FPC contact,
an open FSR column trace, or a column that is not being driven through a pressed
FSR cell naturally reads close to zero and appears black on the fixed 0--4095
colour scale.

Diagnostic interpretation:

- If a dark cell rises when it is pressed, the column is electrically active
  and the black baseline is normal.
- If every row in the column remains near zero while pressing, inspect/reseat
  the listed FPC contacts and check power-off continuity to the matching ADC
  AIN input.
- If the ADC pin voltage rises but its reported code remains zero, investigate
  the ADC/package connection; if the voltage also remains zero, the fault is
  before the ADC.

The screenshot also reported approximately 1 frame/s. This is independent of
the dark-column colour and indicates the previously observed SPI phase loss
after an SWD halt. A normal STM32 reset, without subsequently halting the
target, should restore approximately 25 frame/s.

### Code-versus-hardware isolation result

A uniform synthetic 16 x 16 matrix was passed through the same heatmap geometry
and orientation functions:

```text

cells: 256
input/output value: 2048
```

Therefore the geometry and colour mapper can render every row and column.

After a normal STM32 reset, 50 real frames were captured directly from COM9:

```text

sequence gaps: 0
shape: 50 x 16 x 16
```

```text

C00       16          0       11       0.8
```

## 2026-07-25 - Per-cell zero/full heatmap calibration

`teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/live_fsr_heatmap.py` now provides two one-click
calibration controls:

- **Calibrate zero**: remove all pressure and keep the array unloaded while 40
  unique live frames are collected.
- **Calibrate full**: apply the same known full-load condition to every cell
  and hold it while 40 unique live frames are collected.

The median of the 40 samples is stored separately for every one of the 256 FSR
cells. Median acquisition rejects isolated serial/ADC spikes better than a
single-frame reference.

After both maps exist, cell `(r, c)` is displayed as:

```text
pressure[r,c] =
    clip((raw[r,c] - zero[r,c]) / (full[r,c] - zero[r,c]), 0, 1)
```

The calculation deliberately retains the sign of `full - zero`, so it supports
both voltage-divider polarities: ADC codes may either rise or fall with
pressure. The colour bar then changes from raw 12-bit ADC codes to calibrated
pressure from 0% to 100%.

If `abs(full - zero)` is less than 50 ADC codes for a cell, that cell is marked
invalid and shown in grey instead of amplifying noise into a false pressure
reading. The threshold can be changed with:

```powershell
python -u live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9 --min-calibration-span 50
```

Hovering over a calibrated cell reports its normalized percentage, current raw
ADC code, zero reference, full reference, ADC input, and FPC pin. The title
reports the peak valid cell and the number of invalid cells.

Calibration is saved automatically beside the script as:

```text
teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/fsr_calibration.json
```

It is loaded automatically on the next run. A different file or a different
sample count can be selected with:

```powershell
python -u live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9 `
  --calibration-file experiment_01.json `
  --calibration-frames 60
```

Operational sequence:

1. Start the heatmap and wait for live frames.
2. Remove every load from the FSR array and click **Calibrate zero**.
3. Do not touch the array until the status reaches 40/40 and confirms the save.
4. Apply a repeatable, spatially uniform full-load reference to all 256 cells.
5. Click **Calibrate full** and hold the load until 40/40 is complete.
6. The heatmap immediately switches to each cell's own 0%--100% range.

The full calibration must be spatially uniform. Pressing the array by hand one
cell at a time while the 40 frames are collected does not create a valid
simultaneous full-load map.

Verification completed:

```text
Python compile check: PASS
Increasing-ADC normalization: zero 100, full 1100, raw 600 -> 0.500 PASS
Decreasing-ADC normalization: zero 1100, full 100, raw 600 -> 0.500 PASS
Invalid-span handling: absolute span 20 at threshold 50 -> NaN/grey PASS
Calibration JSON save/load round trip: PASS
Headless GUI startup: PASS
Visual layout check: PASS
```

No STM32 or Teensy firmware was changed or reflashed for this feature. It is a
PC-side visualization and calibration change only.

## 2026-07-25 - Heatmap refresh synchronized to acquisition

The live heatmap previously checked its frame queue every 40 ms, nominally
25 Hz. The measured hardware rate is approximately 25.1--25.9 frame/s, so the
two independent schedules could slowly change phase and occasionally cause the
one-element display queue to replace a frame before it was drawn.

The default `--refresh-ms` queue-check interval is now 10 ms. This is not an
independent 100 Hz heatmap animation: `update()` returns without drawing when
there is no new hardware frame. Consequently, the visible heatmap changes once
per received STM32 frame and follows the actual hardware acquisition rate,
normally approximately 25 Hz, with no more than approximately 10 ms of GUI
polling latency.

The serial reader remains in its background thread so plotting cannot delay
acquisition. The newest-frame queue is retained to prevent a slow GUI window
from building an increasingly stale backlog.

Verification:

```text
Python compile check: PASS
Default GUI queue check: 10 ms
Redraw condition: new hardware frame only
Expected visible update rate: measured acquisition rate, approximately 25 Hz
STM32 firmware changed: no
Teensy firmware changed: no
```

## 2026-07-25 - One-frame-per-second and false unloaded pressure

The calibrated heatmap was observed at 1.0 frame/s with zero sequence gaps.
This is not a plotting refresh limitation: the GUI now redraws whenever a new
frame arrives, but COM9 was delivering only approximately one frame each
second. The combination of a valid sequential frame and a period close to one
second matches the previously diagnosed STM32 `HOST_SPI_TIMEOUT` recovery path.
A valid 515-byte frame can reach Teensy before the STM32 polling call reports
the failed/incomplete end-of-transaction state and spends its 1000 ms timeout.

A low-frequency normal SWD reset was issued without reflashing:

```powershell
pyocd reset -t stm32g474cetx --frequency 10000 --connect under-reset
```

The command completed successfully. Because the running heatmap owns COM9, an
independent receiver could not open the port to verify the post-reset rate.
The heatmap title is the authoritative immediate check: it should return to
approximately 25 frame/s after the normal reset. If it remains at 1 frame/s,
power-cycle the STM32/Teensy combination while keeping PB8/BOOT0 low and do not
halt the MCU with an SWD debug session.

The saved calibration was also inspected:

```text
zero range:                 0..3492 ADC
full range:                 1..2586 ADC
absolute span median:       1862.25 ADC
span below 50 ADC:          65 cells
span from 50 to 100 ADC:    17 cells
```

For the cell shown in the screenshot:

```text
R0, C7
raw:    2815
zero:   2878.5
full:   2580.5
span:   -298
result: (2815 - 2878.5) / (2580.5 - 2878.5) = 21.3%
```

The displayed pressure is therefore the correct result of the saved electrical
references, but it is not proof of mechanical load. The unloaded raw code moved
63.5 counts toward its full-load reference after zero calibration. Supply or
reference drift, FSR relaxation, crosstalk, unstable contacts, and a long
40-second calibration performed at 1 frame/s can all cause this effect.

The heatmap now labels rates below 10 frame/s as `SLOW LINK: reset STM32`.
Zero/full calibration is blocked below 10 frame/s and an in-progress
calibration is aborted if the link becomes slow. This prevents another 40-frame
reference from being collected over approximately 40 seconds in the timeout
state.

Required recovery order:

1. Confirm approximately 25 frame/s in the title.
2. Leave the array unloaded and redo **Calibrate zero**; it should complete in
   approximately 1.6 seconds.
3. Apply the repeatable uniform reference load and redo **Calibrate full**.
4. Remove the load and inspect raw/zero/full on any remaining non-zero cell.
   A repeatable raw shift then indicates electrical/mechanical baseline drift,
   not a heatmap calculation error.

## 2026-07-26 - Heatmap remained at "Waiting for FSR frames"

The heatmap opened COM9 and loaded `fsr_calibration.json`, but its title remained
`Waiting for FSR frames from COM9...`. This state means the serial port opened
successfully but the parser had not received one complete valid binary frame
beginning with `0xA5`. It is not a valid all-zero pressure frame.

The Teensy bridge prints `# WAIT_IRQ level=0` when STM32 PB8/HOST_IRQ remains
low. The binary PC parser discards this ASCII diagnostic while searching for the
`0xA5` marker, so the GUI can remain on the waiting screen without showing the
underlying IRQ message.

Recovery and inspection performed:

```powershell
pyocd reset -t stm32g474cetx --frequency 10000 --connect under-reset
```

The reset completed. A low-frequency SWD read then showed GPIOB IDR and ODR as
`0xFFFFFEBF`; bit 8 was low at the instant of the read, so HOST_IRQ/PB8 was not
asserted. Because an under-reset debug connection can leave the core halted
during inspection, the target was explicitly resumed:

```powershell
pyocd commander -t stm32g474cetx --frequency 10000 `
  --connect under-reset -c "continue" -c "exit"
```

Result:

```text
Successfully resumed device
```

The running heatmap owns COM9, so a second process cannot independently verify
the first recovered frame. The existing window should begin updating after the
resume. If it remains waiting, the next isolation check is Teensy GPIO2:

- GPIO2 remains low: STM32 is not reaching `HOST_SendFrame()`; inspect the ADC
  scan/EOC path and confirm the MCU booted from Flash.
- GPIO2 goes high: STM32 has a frame but Teensy is not completing the SPI
  transaction; inspect PA15/NSS, PB3/SCK, PB4/MISO, and common ground.

## 2026-07-26 - Non-zero cells immediately after full calibration

The link recovered to 25.0 frame/s with zero sequence gaps. The remaining
coloured cells are therefore not caused by the previous one-frame-per-second
communication fault.

Calibration defines two static electrical reference values; it does not force
future unloaded readings to zero. The screenshot gave the following example:

```text
R15, C15
raw ADC after releasing load: 843
stored zero reference:          1
displayed full reference:    2464
normalized result: (843 - 1) / (2464 - 1) = 34.2%
```

The heatmap calculation is consistent with those inputs. The sensor/electrical
reading had not returned to the previously captured zero reference. Likely
causes are FSR mechanical hysteresis/creep after the full-load press, residual
preload from the stack or fixture, supply/reference movement, and matrix
crosstalk or unstable contacts. A static two-point calibration cannot by itself
distinguish these effects from a real applied pressure.

The newly saved JSON map was inspected:

```text
valid cells (absolute span >= 50 ADC): 187
invalid cells:                          69
zero median:                             1 ADC
full median:                          2529 ADC
full > zero valid cells:               182
full < zero valid cells:                 5
```

Four entire columns account for 64 of the 69 invalid cells:

```text
C2, C3, C12, C13: 16 invalid rows each
```

This matches the previously identified fixed low-response column groups and is
not a heatmap polygon error. The remaining five invalid cells are isolated
low-span channels.

Recommended calibration sequence for the FSR material:

1. Apply and release the reference load several times to precondition the FSR.
2. Release all pressure and allow the raw ADC readings to settle.
3. Click **Calibrate zero** and keep the array untouched for 40 frames.
4. Apply a spatially uniform full-load reference and click **Calibrate full**.
5. Release the load and wait for mechanical recovery.
6. If the unloaded raw codes settle at new offsets, click **Calibrate zero**
   once more. This updates zero while retaining the saved full map.

Large residual readings such as 843 ADC counts should not be hidden with a
software deadband, because doing so would also remove genuine light pressure.
They should first be separated into mechanical preload/hysteresis versus
electrical baseline movement by observing the raw ADC value over time.

## 2026-07-26 - Permanent correction for recurring 1 Hz SPI transfer

The system again fell from 25 frame/s to 1.0 frame/s while still reporting zero
sequence gaps. A live SWD inspection of the original build gave:

```text
fsr_scan_status:       HAL_OK
fsr_stage:             HOST_TRANSFER
fsr_last_error_stage:  HOST_TRANSFER
fsr_completed_scans:   93
fsr_completed_frames:  25
fsr_error_count:       69
host_sequence:         96
```

This proves that ADC/MUX scanning continued, Teensy received sequential valid
frames, but most STM32 host-transfer calls failed after the data had already
been clocked out.

### Root cause

SPI3 is configured as a two-line, full-duplex slave. Every STM32 MISO byte
therefore also clocks a dummy MOSI byte into the STM32 RX FIFO. The current
firmware used:

```c
HAL_SPI_Transmit(&hspi3, host_tx_frame, HOST_FRAME_BYTES,
                 HOST_SPI_TIMEOUT);
```

Inspection of `stm32g4xx_hal_spi.c` showed that `HAL_SPI_Transmit()` calls
`SPI_EndRxTxTransaction()`, which waits for all three conditions:

```text
TX FIFO empty
BSY clear
RX FIFO empty
```

The receive FIFO was not being drained during transmit-only operation. The HAL
could therefore wait for `FRLVL_EMPTY` until the 1000 ms
`HOST_SPI_TIMEOUT`, even though Teensy had already received all 515 valid bytes.
This directly explains the approximately 1 Hz rate with zero PC sequence gaps.

The earlier workflow conclusion that transmit-only mode safely cleared the
overrun was incomplete: HAL clears OVR only after its end-of-transaction checks,
so it can spend the entire timeout waiting for the non-empty receive FIFO
first.

### Firmware correction

STM32 now provides a 515-byte dummy receive buffer and uses full-duplex polling:

```c
static uint8_t host_rx_dummy[HOST_FRAME_BYTES];

HAL_SPI_TransmitReceive(&hspi3, host_tx_frame, host_rx_dummy,
                        HOST_FRAME_BYTES, HOST_SPI_TIMEOUT);
```

This consumes every dummy MOSI byte while the real frame is transmitted on
MISO. Diagnostic variables were retained for future SWD inspection:

```text
host_spi_error_code
host_spi_sr
host_spi_tx_remaining
host_spi_rx_remaining
```

Teensy now holds NSS low for an additional 100 microseconds after its last byte:

```cpp
delayMicroseconds(100);
digitalWrite(STM32_CS_PIN, HIGH);
```

This gives the STM32 time to consume the final receive byte and complete FIFO
checks before the slave transaction closes.

### Build and programming

STM32 build:

```text
RAM:    3360 bytes / 128 KiB (2.56%)
Flash: 13284 bytes / 512 KiB (2.53%)
Build: PASS
```

The STM32 ELF was programmed through DAPLink at 100 kHz under reset. Teensy
4.1 was compiled with Arduino CLI and `teensy:avr:teensy41`; the upload command
completed on COM9.

An initial five-frame measurement before the final STM32 reflash/reset still
showed 1.09 frame/s, consistent with the peripheral remaining in the previous
failed state. After the final corrected STM32 image was programmed, live SWD
diagnostics showed:

```text
fsr_completed_scans:   1109
fsr_completed_frames:  1109
fsr_error_count:       0
host_spi_error_code:   0
host_spi_tx_remaining: 0
host_spi_rx_remaining: 0
```

Final independent COM9 validation:

```text
valid frames: 100/100
sequence: 1722..1821
sequence gaps: 0
elapsed: 3.972 s
rate: 25.17 frame/s
ADC range: 0..3925
```

Result: the recurring 1 Hz mode was reproduced, isolated to SPI3 host transfer,
corrected in both endpoints, rebuilt, flashed, and verified at the expected
continuous frame rate.

## 2026-07-26 - No frames after restart: STM32 booted into system ROM

After the SPI throughput correction, COM9 was still present as the Teensy USB
serial device (`VID:PID 16C0:0483`) and no old heatmap or receiver process held
the port. A direct binary capture nevertheless received no valid frame.

A raw three-second serial read showed:

```text
# WAIT_IRQ level=0
# WAIT_IRQ level=0
# WAIT_IRQ level=0
```

This proved that Teensy was running but STM32 PB8/HOST_IRQ remained low. Live
SWD inspection then identified the exact boot state:

```text
PC:   0x1FFF3B20
VTOR: 0x1FFF0000
```

Addresses in `0x1FFFxxxx` belong to the STM32 system-memory ROM bootloader, not
the application stored in Flash at `0x08000000`. RAM locations corresponding to
the application diagnostic variables also contained unrelated ROM data. The
ADC scan and `HOST_SendFrame()` application were therefore not running.

PB8 is both `HOST_IRQ` and the STM32 BOOT0 sampling path. The Teensy configures
GPIO2 as `INPUT_PULLDOWN`, but this is only a weak internal pull and is absent
or undefined while Teensy itself resets or enters its loader. If STM32 samples
PB8 high during that interval, it starts the ROM bootloader and never produces
an FSR frame.

Recovery performed:

```powershell
pyocd reset -t stm32g474cetx --frequency 10000 `
  --connect under-reset --method hw
```

With the Teensy application already running and holding PB8 low, the hardware
reset returned execution to Flash:

```text
PC: 0x0800164C
target resumed successfully
```

Final independent COM9 validation:

```text
valid frames: 100/100
sequence: 986..1085
sequence gaps: 0
elapsed: 3.986 s
rate: 25.09 frame/s
```

Permanent cold-boot correction remains recommended:

- add an external approximately 10 kOhm resistor from PB8/HOST_IRQ/BOOT0 to
  ground; it draws only approximately 0.33 mA while HOST_IRQ is high, or
- deliberately configure the STM32 boot option bytes so the physical BOOT0 pin
  cannot select system memory.

The resistor is the transparent hardware fix. Option-byte programming is a
persistent target configuration change and must not be performed casually;
retain SWD recovery access and document the exact option values before using it.

## 2026-07-26 - Software-only BOOT0 solution assessment

The user cannot add the recommended external PB8/BOOT0 pull-down. The failure
recurred and was inspected again before changing any persistent configuration:

```text
PC:         0x1FFF3B5A
FLASH_OPTR: 0xFFEFF8AA
```

This was another system-ROM boot, not the corrected SPI link falling back to
1 frame/s. In the current option value:

```text
nSWBOOT0 (bit 26): 1 -> BOOT0 is taken from physical PB8
nBOOT0   (bit 27): 1 -> software BOOT0 value would select main Flash
nBOOT1   (bit 23): 1
```

The STM32G4 reference-manual boot table states that `nSWBOOT0 = 0` and
`nBOOT0 = 1` select main Flash independently of the PB8 pin. Therefore the
software-only permanent correction is to clear only `FLASH_OPTR.nSWBOOT0` while
preserving `nBOOT0`, protection, reset-mode, watchdog, bank, and all other
option fields.

No option byte was changed during this inspection because it is persistent
device configuration and requires explicit approval. STM32CubeProgrammer and
OpenOCD were not installed, so a future update would require either installing
STM32CubeProgrammer or using a carefully verified direct option-programming
sequence.

Temporary recovery was performed again:

```powershell
pyocd reset -t stm32g474cetx --frequency 10000 `
  --connect under-reset --method hw
```

Verification:

```text
PC after reset: 0x08000C7C
valid frames:   20/20
sequence:       723..742
rate:           25.36 frame/s
```

Until the option bit is deliberately changed, the required power-up order is:

1. allow Teensy firmware to start so GPIO2 applies its internal pull-down to
   PB8/HOST_IRQ/BOOT0;
2. then hardware-reset STM32 through NRST.

Any simultaneous cold start, Teensy upload, or Teensy reset can leave PB8
undefined when STM32 samples BOOT0 and can reproduce the ROM-boot condition.

## 2026-07-26 - Permanent software boot selection applied

The user explicitly approved the persistent option-byte correction because the
hardware cannot be modified.

Before programming:

```text
FLASH_OPTR: 0xFFEFF8AA
nSWBOOT0:   1 (physical PB8/BOOT0 pin selected)
nBOOT0:     1
PC during failure: 0x1FFF3B5A (system ROM)
```

A one-time self-check was added to the STM32 application using the official ST
HAL option-byte API. It changes only these two declared user fields:

```c
option_bytes.OptionType = OPTIONBYTE_USER;
option_bytes.USERType = OB_USER_nSWBOOT0 | OB_USER_nBOOT0;
option_bytes.USERConfig = OB_BOOT0_FROM_OB | OB_nBOOT0_SET;
```

This clears `FLASH_OPTR.nSWBOOT0` and explicitly preserves `nBOOT0 = 1`.
Protection, reset mode, watchdog, bank, RDP, BOR, and all unrelated option
fields are untouched. On later starts, the function reads the active option
bytes and immediately returns because the requested state is already present.

The project built successfully:

```text
RAM:    3392 bytes / 128 KiB (2.59%)
Flash: 16672 bytes / 512 KiB (3.18%)
```

The approved image was flashed with DAPLink at 100 kHz under reset. The option
reload reset completed and readback proved the exact one-bit change:

```text
before: FLASH_OPTR = 0xFFEFF8AA
after:  FLASH_OPTR = 0xFBEFF8AA
changed mask:       0x04000000 (nSWBOOT0 only)
PC after reload:    0x0800160C
```

The physical PB8 level can no longer select system ROM; the software nBOOT0
value selects main Flash on every reset.

Final independent transport validation:

```text

sequence: 952..1051
sequence gaps: 0
rate: 25.16 frame/s
```

The Heatmap display that still showed 1 Hz was an old pre-correction window and
its last measured title. The verified COM9 source was already delivering
25.16 frame/s. A fresh Heatmap process was started:

```text

command: python -u live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9
```

Permanent software boot selection and the SPI RX-FIFO throughput correction
are now both active.

that cell's zero reference. The upper endpoint is no longer measured or loaded
from the old `full` JSON field. Twelve-bit ADC full scale is used uniformly:

```text
100% = ADC 4095
```

The displayed value for cell `(r, c)` is now:

```text
pressure[r,c] =
```
