## 2026-07-26 - Root cause of repeated return to 1 frame/s

The link again measured approximately 1 frame/s after it had passed a clean
25 frame/s test. This time the permanent boot option was still correct:

```text
FLASH_OPTR: 0xFBEFF8AA
PC:         0x080011C8 (main Flash)
```

Therefore ROM boot was no longer the cause. A direct five-frame COM9 test,
independent of Heatmap, measured:

```text
valid frames: 5/5
sequence: 432..436
elapsed: 4.268 s
rate: 1.17 frame/s
```

Corrected live SWD diagnostics from the active ELF symbols showed:

```text
fsr_completed_scans:   1151
fsr_completed_frames:  1032
fsr_error_count:       119
fsr_last_error_stage:  HOST_TRANSFER
```

The crucial system-level cause was PC USB backpressure:

1. STM32 finishes one 16 x 16 scan, raises HOST_IRQ, and blocks waiting for the
   Teensy SPI master.
2. Teensy clocks the 515-byte frame, then calls the blocking
   `Serial.write(spiRx, 515)`.
3. When Heatmap/PC serial reading is closed between tests, the USB CDC transmit
   buffer fills after only a few seconds.
4. Teensy blocks inside `Serial.write()` and stops servicing the next
   HOST_IRQ.
5. STM32 waits 1000 ms in its SPI call, records a HOST_TRANSFER error, and
   retries. This creates the observed approximately 1 frame/s mode.
6. Reopening Heatmap does not reliably resynchronize a transaction that is
   already alternating between USB blockage and STM32 timeout.

This explains why every clean reset initially produced 25 frame/s and why the
rate degraded again after the PC reader was closed or restarted.

### Permanent transport correction

The Teensy bridge now treats the PC connection as a lossy newest-frame output,
not as a flow-control dependency for the STM32:

```cpp
if (Serial && Serial.availableForWrite() >=
                  static_cast<int>(FRAME_BYTES)) {
  Serial.write(spiRx, FRAME_BYTES);
} else {
  ++droppedUsbFrames;
}
```

If the PC is absent or slow, Teensy drops only the USB copy of that frame. It
still completes every STM32 SPI transaction immediately, so STM32 acquisition
cannot be stalled by USB.

Teensy compilation and upload completed successfully:

```text
FLASH code: 12548 bytes
RAM1 variables: 5344 bytes
board: teensy:avr:teensy41
port: COM9
```

Controlled validation:

```text
Initial connection:
  valid frames: 50/50
  sequence: 401..450
  gaps: 0
  rate: 25.07 frame/s

COM9 closed with no PC reader for 10 seconds:
  old blocking path would overflow and trigger the 1 Hz failure

Reconnect without resetting STM32 or Teensy:
  valid frames: 50/50
  sequence: 1045..1094
  gaps: 0
  rate: 25.27 frame/s
```

Result: the repeated 1 Hz failure was caused by Teensy USB CDC backpressure,
not Heatmap rendering, ADC timing, or BOOT0 after the option-byte correction.
The bridge now remains synchronized while the Heatmap is closed and resumes at
approximately 25 frame/s immediately when the PC reconnects.

A fresh visible Heatmap was started:

```text
PID: 20184
window: Figure 1
status: responding
```

## 2026-07-26 - Protocol-to-physical-cell mapping audit

The complete path was checked against the current STM32 firmware, Teensy
bridge, Python decoder, MAX11633 datasheet, mainboard 2.2 PCB, and FSR-array
2.4 PCB.

The 515-byte frame is:

```text
byte 0:       0xA5 frame marker
bytes 1..2:  uint16 sequence, little-endian
bytes 3..514: 256 uint16 ADC samples, little-endian and row-major
```

For raw matrix coordinate `(Rr, Cc)`, where `r,c = 0..15`:

```text
sample index       = 16*r + c
sample low byte    = 3 + 2*(16*r + c)
sample high byte   = 4 + 2*(16*r + c)
row signal         = MUX1 channel Ir
row connector      = ROW_FPC1 pin r+1
FSR row net        = /out r
column signal      = MAX11633 AINc
column connector   = COL_FPC1 pin c+1
FSR column net     = /ain c
physical cell      = intersection of /out r and /ain c
```

The MAX11633 command `0xF8` selects channel 15 with scan mode `00`, which the
datasheet defines as scanning AIN0 through AIN15. FIFO data is read oldest
first, so the firmware's `C0..C15 = AIN0..AIN15` interpretation is correct.
STM32 packs rows first, Teensy forwards all 515 bytes unchanged, and Python
unpacks 256 little-endian values in row-major order. No transpose or reversal
exists in the transport code.

Current PCB geometry confirms:

```text
R0 / out0 = top physical row
R15/out15 = bottom physical row
C0 / ain0 = left physical column
C15/ain15 = right physical column
```

Correction: the previous GUI and inspection note used `column FPC pin =
16-C`. That was incorrect for the current mainboard 2.2 and FSR-array 2.4
files. Both boards map connector pin 1 to AIN0/ain0 and pin 16 to AIN15/ain15.
The GUI hover text now reports both correct row and column connector pins.
Consequently, the four repeatedly abnormal columns map to:

```text
C2  = AIN2  = COL_FPC1 pin 3
C3  = AIN3  = COL_FPC1 pin 4
C12 = AIN12 = COL_FPC1 pin 13
C13 = AIN13 = COL_FPC1 pin 14
```

A 25-frame live capture on COM9 remained stable at approximately 25 frame/s.
It showed repeatable near-zero rows `R3, R6, R7, R8, R11, R12, R15` and
repeatable low columns `C0, C2, C3, C8, C10, C11, C12, C13, C15` under the
current physical condition. The strongest cells were around `R9/R13/R14` and
`C1/C4/C6/C7`. This capture proves the matrix values are stable, but whether
those values agree with applied pressure must be confirmed by pressing one
known physical cell and comparing the raw `(R,C)` peak.

## 2026-07-26 - Heatmap stuck at "Waiting for FSR frames"

COM9 enumeration was checked and the Teensy remained present as
`VID:PID 16C0:0483`. A hidden Python reader process was found holding COM9 and
was terminated. Opening the low-level reader after releasing the port still
produced no valid 515-byte frames.

Raw USB serial diagnostics for three seconds were:

```text
# WAIT_IRQ level=0
# BAD_MAGIC 0x00
# WAIT_IRQ level=0
# BAD_MAGIC 0x00
# WAIT_IRQ level=0
# BAD_MAGIC 0x01
```

This proves that the GUI was not the cause. Teensy was running and could see
occasional STM32 IRQ assertions, but SPI data did not begin with the required
`0xA5` frame marker.

The active `D:\app\python` pyOCD installation was also found incomplete:
`colorama`, then `psutil` and other dependencies were missing. pyOCD 0.45.1
and its complete dependencies were restored in that environment.

Recovery attempts:

```text
pyocd reset -t stm32g474cetx -f 10k -M under-reset -m hw
result: hardware reset completed

pyocd flash -t stm32g474cetx -f 10k -M under-reset ESKIN_STM32.elf
attempt 1: SWD No ACK
attempt 2: SWD No ACK

pyocd flash -t stm32g474cetx -f 100k -M under-reset ESKIN_STM32.elf
result: SWD No ACK
```

Current conclusion: reopening the Heatmap cannot recover this state. The
Teensy USB interface is alive, but STM32 is neither producing a valid SPI
frame nor responding reliably over SWD. Because the mainboard is powered from
Teensy, the next required recovery step is a real target power cycle: unplug
both Teensy and DAPLink USB, wait at least five seconds, reconnect Teensy first,
then reconnect DAPLink while leaving DAPLink 3.3 V disconnected. After power
returns, repeat the low-frequency under-reset flash and verify five binary
frames before opening the Heatmap.

Correction from the user: DAPLink was not connected to the mainboard during
the flash attempts. Therefore, the three `SWD No ACK` results were expected
and do **not** demonstrate unstable STM32 SWD communication. COM10 only showed
that the DAPLink USB device was enumerated by Windows. The valid diagnosis is
limited to the Teensy-side observation: COM9 and the Teensy firmware were
alive, but SPI transactions returned `0x00/0x01` instead of frame marker
`0xA5`.

With Teensy as the only mainboard power source, the first recovery action is
to unplug Teensy USB for at least five seconds and reconnect it. If valid
frames do not resume, DAPLink must then be physically connected to the
mainboard SWD header before any reset or reflash test is meaningful.

Post-power-cycle result:

```text
capture duration: 3 seconds
USB bytes received: 114
valid 0xA5 markers: 0
diagnostics:
  WAIT_IRQ level=0
  BAD_MAGIC 0x01
  WAIT_IRQ level=0
  BAD_MAGIC 0x00
  WAIT_IRQ level=0
  BAD_MAGIC 0x01
```

The Teensy/mainboard power cycle did not restore the binary protocol. Teensy
is running and performing SPI transactions after IRQ assertions, but the
STM32 response is not the complete-FSR firmware frame. The next meaningful
action is to connect DAPLink to the mainboard and reflash the verified
`ESKIN_STM32.elf`; further Heatmap restarts cannot fix this state.

DAPLink was subsequently connected to the mainboard. Recovery completed as
follows:

```text
1. pyocd reset -t stm32g474cetx -f 10k -M under-reset -m hw
   result: target identified; hardware reset completed

2. pyocd flash -t stm32g474cetx -f 10k -M under-reset ESKIN_STM32.elf
   result: completed successfully

3. Initial post-flash state
   result: Teensy reported WAIT_IRQ level=0

4. Explicit post-flash hardware reset at 10 kHz
   result: valid 0xA5 frames resumed

5. Binary validation
   valid frames: 50/50
   sequence: 360..409
   gaps: 0
   ADC range: 0..25
   rate: 25.15 frame/s
```

The important recovery detail is that a successful flash alone did not start
frame production in this session. An explicit hardware reset after flashing
was required. The corrected Heatmap was then launched on COM9 as PID 21120.

disconnected because Teensy supplies the mainboard.

## 2026-07-26 - Full-pressure hardware fault update and corrected axis semantics

The user confirmed the actual hardware meaning:

```text
MUX-selected axis = physical columns
MAX11633 ADC axis = physical rows
```

Therefore the raw protocol matrix remains `matrix[mux_index][ain_index]`, but
its physical cell name is:

```text
physical column = mux_index + 1
physical row    = ain_index + 1
cell            = R(ain_index + 1), C(mux_index + 1)
```

This supersedes earlier notes that described MUX as rows and ADC as columns.
The byte order itself was not wrong; only the physical R/C naming was wrong.

The GUI was corrected consistently:

```text

left/right labels:                          R1..R16
title peak coordinate:                      R=AIN, C=MUX
hover coordinate:                           R=AIN, C=MUX
MUX connector description:                  column FPC
ADC connector description:                  row FPC
```

under pressure:


## 2026-07-27 - Transpose restored and made default after physical motion test

The non-transposed trial was rejected by the physical motion test: moving
across the sensor horizontally from 1 to 16 still moved vertically in the GUI.

Root cause:

```text
raw matrix axis 0 = MUX physical column
NumPy axis 0      = display vertical direction
```

Therefore raw MUX column movement appears vertical unless the matrix is
transposed for display. Transpose is now enabled by default, together with the
existing default left-right mirror:

```text
GUI horizontal axis = MUX physical columns, C16..C1
GUI vertical axis   = ADC physical rows, R1..R16
```

```powershell
python live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9
```

```powershell
python live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9 --no-transpose
```

This correction changes display orientation only. Protocol byte order and raw
calibration coordinates remain unchanged.

`C5/C6/C7` changed between strong and dead across captures, so they are
intermittent rather than confirmed permanently open. The highest-confidence
recurring ADC-side problems are `R12/R13/R14`, with `R11/R16` repeatedly weak
or dead. Other row changes indicate intermittent contact or changed pressure.

The screenshot and later raw capture are not identical (for example R1 appears
active in the screenshot but was near zero in the capture). Fault replacement
decisions must therefore prioritize channels that repeat across captures, not
a single colour image. The Heatmap was reopened as PID 9460.

## 2026-07-27 - Full software-chain re-review after hardware repair

The complete active path was reviewed again under the working assumption that
the connector, power, SPI3 link, MUX address lines, and FSR interconnect are now
electrically sound:

```text
STM32 MUX selection
  -> MAX11633 conversion/FIFO read
  -> STM32 515-byte frame packing
  -> Teensy SPI master and USB forwarding
  -> Python binary decoder
  -> Heatmap orientation and calibration
```

### Verified items

- A clean CMake rebuild of the STM32 Debug target completed successfully with
  `-Wall`; there were no compiler warnings or errors.
- `0x10` correctly resets all MAX11633 registers.
- `0x68` correctly selects clock mode `10`, keeps AIN15 available as an analog
  input, and keeps the internal 2.5 V reference enabled.
- `0xF8` correctly requests one FIFO result from every input AIN0 through
  AIN15.
- EOC polling polarity is correct: EOC is high while the requested operation is
  running and low after the final result is available.
- MAX11633 FIFO results are MSB-first 12-bit values with four leading zero
  bits; the STM32 decoder implements this correctly.
- STM32 stores the raw matrix as
  `frame[mux_column][adc_row]`, packs each sample little-endian, and sends the
  matrix in raw row-major memory order.
- Teensy transfers exactly 515 bytes in SPI mode 0, verifies the `0xA5` marker,
  and forwards the bytes without rearranging the payload.
- A generated 256-value frame passed the Python decoder test, including the
  sequence number, little-endian samples, first cell, last cell, and an interior
  cell.
- All eight combinations of GUI transpose, row flip, and column flip passed an
  exhaustive 16 x 16 display-to-raw inverse-coordinate test.
- The current default display remains physically consistent with the confirmed
  sensor semantics: horizontal is MUX column, vertical is ADC row, with the
  requested left-right mirror.

No software evidence was found for a byte-order error, a one-sample offset, an
accidental matrix transpose in the transport, or a wrong MAX11633 channel-scan
command. Those mechanisms do not explain the recurring low rows and columns.

### Remaining firmware weakness: ADC acquisition settling

The MAX11633 datasheet states that internally timed conversions have a minimum
acquisition time of approximately 1.4 us and that the required acquisition time
increases with analog source impedance:

```text
tACQ = 9 x (RS + 1.5 kOhm) x 24 pF + 1 us
```

The current firmware waits 1 ms after selecting each external MUX column, but
this wait only allows the external FSR/MUX node to settle before the scan. It
does not lengthen the MAX11633 track-and-hold acquisition interval for each
AIN channel. The subsequent `0xF8` command still samples AIN0 through AIN15
using the ADC's internally timed minimum acquisition. An FSR matrix can present
a high and pressure-dependent source impedance, so the present configuration
has not proved that each ADC input settles to 12-bit accuracy. Possible
symptoms are low readings, dependence on the previously converted channel,
crosstalk, and different apparent response among ADC rows.

This is the highest-priority software-side hypothesis remaining. It is not yet
proof that it caused every observed low path. The decisive next firmware A/B
test is to retain the current production image and build a diagnostic variant
that performs repeated conversions/averaging for each AIN result, discards an
initial sample where appropriate, and compares the fixed-pressure raw matrix
against the current single-sample scan.

### Other non-root-cause limitations

- The frame has a magic byte and sequence number but no CRC. A rare payload bit
  error could therefore be accepted silently. This does not match the stable,
  path-shaped patterns seen so far, but a CRC should be added before treating
  the link as production-ready.
- Teensy diagnostic ASCII lines and binary frames share one USB stream. The
  Python decoder deliberately resynchronises on `0xA5`, so this is not the
  matrix-mapping fault, but separate diagnostics or a framed message type would
  make the protocol more robust.
- Zero calibration is a per-cell baseline subtraction followed by scaling to
  ADC code 4095. It does not convert ADC code to force, compensate the nonlinear
  FSR response, or replace a controlled fixed-pressure comparison.

No production source was changed during this diagnostic review. The STM32
clean rebuild did regenerate the verified Debug ELF.

## 2026-07-27 - Targeted R1/R12/R13/R14 end-to-end response test

A new non-destructive PC diagnostic was added:

```text
teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/test_adc_rows.py
```

It retains the current STM32 and Teensy production images and checks the four
physical ADC rows that were repeatedly weak or inactive:

```text
physical R1  = MAX11633 AIN0  = raw matrix axis 1 index 0
physical R12 = MAX11633 AIN11 = raw matrix axis 1 index 11
physical R13 = MAX11633 AIN12 = raw matrix axis 1 index 12
physical R14 = MAX11633 AIN13 = raw matrix axis 1 index 13
```

The test captures 50 unloaded frames, then captures 75 frames while each target
row is pressed separately. It uses the median raw ADC change across every MUX
column and declares a response when the strongest cell changes by at least
50 ADC counts. Both increases and decreases are accepted because this is an
electrical response test rather than a pressure calibration. For every press it
also reports the strongest responding row anywhere in the matrix. If the
requested row remains unchanged but a different row responds, the result points
to physical numbering or AIN mapping rather than a completely missing signal.

The script distinguishes two different statements:

- receipt of a valid 515-byte frame proves that every row slot is present in
  the protocol;
- a significant raw change proves that the corresponding analog row responds
  to pressure.

An ADC value of zero is valid data and is not treated as a missing byte.
Sequence gaps and measured frame rate are included in every result, and the
complete baseline, pressed matrices, and decisions are saved to a timestamped
JSON file.

```text
command-line help: PASS
```

## 2026-07-27 - Analysis of the first targeted row-test result

Result file:

```text
adc_row_test_20260727_150937.json
port: COM9
threshold: 50 ADC counts
targets: R1, R12, R13, R14
```

The unloaded baseline was internally consistent:

```text
frames: 50
sequence: 20158..20207
sequence gaps: 0
elapsed: 1.976 s
accepted rate: 25.30 frame/s
```

The provisional pressure decisions stored by the first test run were:

```text
target   ADC input   strongest target change   provisional result
R1       AIN0        +3137 at C11              response detected
R12      AIN11       +2 at C3                  no target response
R13      AIN12        0                         no target response
R14      AIN13        0                         no target response
```

The individual raw medians reinforce the provisional distinction. During the
R1 press, AIN0 increased by approximately 866--3137 counts on most columns.
During the R12 press, AIN11 stayed between 1 and 3 counts. During the R13 and
R14 presses, every target-row median was exactly 1.

However, the four pressed captures failed the frame-integrity check:

```text
target   accepted frames   sequence range   missing sequence numbers
R1       75                20232..20427     121
R12      75                20452..20792     266
R13      75                20817..21468     577
R14      75                21493..21681     114
```

The R13 sequence advanced by 651 during approximately 2.9 seconds. This is
physically incompatible with the current acquisition loop and 100 kHz,
515-byte Teensy SPI transaction. It cannot be explained by ordinary dropped
USB copies alone.

The likely software mechanism is false frame synchronisation. The protocol has
only a one-byte `0xA5` marker and no length field or CRC. The Python reader
searches for any `0xA5`; an ADC sample whose low byte equals `0xA5` can therefore
be mistaken for a frame start after `reset_input_buffer()` begins reading in
the middle of a continuous binary stream. If the false start remains aligned
to 16-bit sample boundaries, the existing `sample <= 4095` validation can also
pass. This can yield plausible-looking matrices with invalid sequence numbers
and shifted cell coordinates.

Consequently:

- R1 very likely has a real analog response, but its pressed capture was not
  integrity-clean.
- R12, R13, and R14 are strong no-response candidates, but this JSON file is
  not sufficient to confirm them.
- The result must be repeated after strengthening the parser to acquire and
  retain a true 515-byte frame boundary and to reject sequence-discontinuous
  candidates.
- A CRC in the STM32/Teensy frame is the production fix. A short-term PC-only
  fix can require multiple consecutive 515-byte frames with incrementing
  sequence numbers before accepting lock.

## 2026-07-27 - Why one pressed row can appear on multiple rows

The first targeted row test cannot yet be used to measure true row-to-row
crosstalk. Its baseline had zero sequence gaps, but every pressed capture had
large, physically impossible sequence jumps. False synchronisation on an
`0xA5` byte inside the ADC payload can shift the matrix and place values on
unrelated displayed rows. This is the first explanation to eliminate because
it is directly supported by the captured metadata.

After frame locking is corrected, three physical effects can still produce
smaller neighbouring-row responses:

1. Mechanical load spreading. A finger, plate, elastomer, or flexing PCB loads
   more than one FSR intersection. Adjacent rows should respond mainly near the
   pressed column and normally decay with distance from the contact.
2. Passive-matrix ghosting. The FSR sheet is a resistive row/column network
   without a diode at every cell. Current can find additional paths through
   other pressed or partially conductive cells, especially when pressure spans
   multiple columns. This usually forms rectangular or line-shaped patterns.
3. MAX11633 track-and-hold memory. Firmware requests AIN0 through AIN15 as one
   internally timed scan. Each input receives only the ADC's short internal
   acquisition interval. With a high and pressure-dependent FSR source
   impedance, residual charge from the previously sampled AIN channel can
   influence the next result. This normally follows ADC scan order and is
   distinct from symmetric mechanical spreading.

The Heatmap zero calibration can amplify small residual changes visually, but
it does not create raw ADC changes. A correct isolation test must therefore use
raw deltas, not calibrated colours.

The diagnostic pattern should be classified as follows after clean frame lock:

```text
unrelated row + sequence gaps       -> transport/parser misalignment
adjacent rows, same column, decay   -> mechanical pressure spreading
next AIN row in scan order          -> ADC acquisition/charge memory
rectangle across rows and columns   -> passive-matrix ghosting
target row alone                    -> ideal electrical selectivity
```

Use a small rigid probe on one cell rather than pressing an entire row, keep all
other cells unloaded, and require zero sequence gaps before drawing a hardware
conclusion.

## 2026-07-27 - False R8/R8/R15 response parser fix

The one-byte-marker resynchronisation in `read_teensy_fsr.py` was replaced by a
stateful fixed-width `FrameReader`. It no longer accepts the first `0xA5` found
in the stream. A frame boundary is now locked only when three candidates:

1. begin exactly 515 bytes apart;
2. each begin with `0xA5`;
3. each contain 256 valid 12-bit samples; and
4. have strictly consecutive 16-bit sequence numbers.

After acquisition, the reader remains on fixed 515-byte boundaries. A bad
marker, invalid sample, or unexpected sequence causes immediate lock loss and a
fresh three-frame search. This prevents an ADC sample whose low byte is `0xA5`
from moving pressure data onto unrelated displayed rows such as the provisional
R8/R8/R15 results.

The verified reader is now used consistently by:

```text
read_teensy_fsr.py
live_fsr_heatmap.py
test_adc_rows.py
```

`test_adc_rows.py` explicitly clears the reader's internal lock whenever it
flushes the serial input between operator-controlled pressure phases. This
prevents a partial post-flush frame from inheriting the previous boundary.

Offline regression tests:

```text
AST syntax, all three Python files: PASS
many deliberate 0xA5 bytes inside every ADC payload: PASS
expected decoded sequences: 100,101,102,103,104,105
deliberately corrupted frame 203: rejected
lock reacquisition: 200,201,202,204,205,206
matrix[MUX column][ADC row] indexing self-test: PASS
```

Live COM9 validation after the change:

```text
valid frames: 50/50
sequence: 39501..39550
sequence gaps: 0
ADC range: 0..2508
elapsed: 2.087 s
rate: 23.95 frame/s
```

The corrected interactive R1/R12/R13/R14 test was then opened in a new
PowerShell window. Hardware conclusions must use the new timestamped JSON and
require zero sequence gaps in every pressure phase. The earlier
`adc_row_test_20260727_150937.json` remains archived but is not suitable for
final row mapping.

## 2026-07-27 - Isolated black-cell Heatmap explanation

The supplied screenshot included the decisive hover values:

```text
raw ADC: 2160
stored zero: 2564
display gain: 0.25x
MUX I9: physical C10
```

The calibration file confirms that `zero[9][6] = 2564`, so the hovered cell is
raw matrix `[MUX I9][AIN6]`, physically `C10/R7`. Its current normalized value
is:

```text
((2160 - 2564) / (4095 - 2564)) x 0.25
= -0.06597
clipped to 0.0
```

Inferno colour value zero is black. Therefore this particular black cell is
fully explained by the current per-cell calibration calculation; it is not
evidence of a missing frame byte or a failed complete row/column path.

The current zero file was saved at `2026-07-27T14:30:43Z`. Its C10 calibration
values vary substantially by physical row, including:

```text
C10/R7 zero = 2564.0
C10/R8 zero = 2571.5
```

Only these two cells in the complete 16 x 16 zero map have a stored zero of at
least 2500. A zero capture made with residual pressure, FSR hysteresis, baseline
drift, or an opposite-going raw response at one cell can therefore create an
isolated black cell even when all bytes and coordinates are valid.

Current Heatmap processing order:

```text
verified 515-byte frame
-> raw uint16 matrix[MUX column][ADC row]
-> per-cell zero subtraction
-> divide by per-cell (4095 - zero) span
-> multiply by 0.25 display gain
-> clip each cell independently to 0..1
-> transpose for physical axes
-> left-right flip
-> flatten into 256 PCB polygons
-> inferno colour map
```

Because calibration is independent for every intersection, a single cell can
be black while every other cell on the same physical row and column works.
The 0.25x change makes low positive responses darker but does not cause this
cell's zero result; it was already negative before the gain was applied.

## 2026-07-27 - Two-point per-cell ADC calibration restored

The temporary `0.25x` display-gain approach was removed. The Heatmap now uses
two raw ADC endpoint maps for every FSR intersection:

```text
zero[mux][ain] = median raw ADC with no pressure
full[mux][ain] = median raw ADC at the intended full pressure

calibrated = (current_adc - zero) / (full - zero)
calibrated = clipped to 0..1
```

This transformation does not alter the MAX11633 conversion register or the
515-byte STM32/Teensy frame. The transmitted raw ADC code remains available and
is shown in the hover text. The change replaces display-only gain with a
per-cell ADC-domain correction on the PC.

Both response polarities are supported:

```text
increasing cell: zero 1000, full 3000, current 2000 -> 50%
decreasing cell: zero 3000, full 1000, current 2000 -> 50%
```

This directly fixes the previous assumption that every pressed cell must have
`ADC > zero`. A cell such as C10/R7 can now be calibrated correctly if its full
pressure ADC is below its unloaded ADC.

GUI changes:

- `Calibrate zero` captures 40 unloaded frames and stores their per-cell median.
- A new `Calibrate full` button captures 40 full-pressure frames and stores
  their per-cell median.
- Capturing a new zero map deliberately clears the previous full map.
- Full calibration cannot start before a zero map exists.
- The Heatmap switches to calibrated percentage only when both maps exist.
- Hover text reports raw ADC, zero ADC, full ADC, signed span, and percentage.
- A cell is invalid when `abs(full - zero) < 50` ADC counts. The absolute value
  is required so decreasing-response cells remain valid.
- The calibration JSON continues to store both 16 x 16 raw endpoint arrays.

Validation:

```text
Python AST syntax: PASS
increasing endpoint midpoint: 50%, PASS
decreasing endpoint midpoint: 50%, PASS
absolute span below 50 rejected: PASS
zero-only file treated as incomplete: PASS
```

The older Heatmap PID 29092 was closed. The two-point version was opened on

## 2026-07-27 - Feasibility of moving calibration into STM32

The current calibration JSON was checked again after the two-point GUI run:

```text
updated: 2026-07-27T14:52:15Z
zero map: present, 16 x 16
full map: present, 16 x 16
```

The MAX11633 cannot be programmed with per-cell minimum and maximum values. It
always returns its raw 12-bit result from 0 through 4095; its registers control
scan, reference, timing, reset, and averaging, not 256 independent calibration
endpoints.

Calibration can instead be compiled into STM32 Flash as two signed 16 x 16
tables and applied immediately after each raw conversion:

```text
span = full[mux][ain] - zero[mux][ain]
calibrated = (raw - zero) * 4095 / span
calibrated = clipped to 0..4095
```

Signed 32-bit arithmetic is required because some cells have `full < zero`.
Cells with `abs(span) < 50` require an invalid flag.

Changing STM32 to transmit calibrated values without changing the protocol
would make the PC mistake calibrated codes for raw ADC codes and apply the
calibration a second time. A safe embedded implementation therefore also needs
a protocol version or frame flag, and a decision whether to transmit:

- calibrated values only, retaining the current frame size and rate;
- raw and calibrated values together, approximately doubling the payload and
  reducing the current 100 kHz frame rate; or
- raw values in a diagnostic mode and calibrated values in the normal mode.

No STM32 or protocol source was changed during this feasibility check.

## 2026-07-27 - Reference voltage correction from mainboard 2.2 netlist

The current `mainbord/2.2/mainboard.net` was inspected directly. The fitted
converter is identified as `MAX11633EEGT`. Net code 1 is named `+3.3V`, and the
same network contains all four relevant nodes:

```text
COL_ADC1 pin 17: REF
COL_ADC1 pin 19: VDD
COL_ADC2 pin 17: REF
COL_ADC2 pin 19: VDD
```

Therefore mainboard 2.2 is wired for the 3.3 V rail to act as an external ADC
reference. The earlier assumption that REF only had a bypass capacitor for the
MAX11633 internal 2.5 V reference was incorrect for this board revision.

This reveals a firmware/hardware mismatch:

```text
current setup 0x68:
  CKSEL = 10, internally timed conversion
  REFSEL = 10, internal 2.5 V reference always on

hardware expectation:
  CKSEL = 10, internally timed conversion
  REFSEL = 01, external reference on REF pin

matching setup byte = 0x64
```

With the matching external-reference configuration, ADC code represents the
ratio to the actual REF rail:

```text
code approximately equals Vin / VREF x 4096
VREF is the measured 3.3 V rail, not an assumed exact 3.300 V
```

At exactly 3.3 V, one LSB is approximately 0.806 mV. If the rail is 3.2 V, one
LSB is approximately 0.781 mV. Because the FSR network and ADC reference can be
derived from the same rail, the measurement is partly ratiometric and supply
variation can cancel in the code ratio.

Changing `0x68` to `0x64` will change the conversion scale and invalidates
previous zero/full endpoint maps. Both calibration stages must be repeated
after the corrected STM32 firmware is flashed. No firmware was changed during
this netlist diagnosis.

## 2026-07-27 - External 3.3 V ADC reference firmware change and reflashing

The MAX11633 setup command in `ESKIN_STM32/Core/Src/main.c` was changed from
`0x68` to `0x64`.

```text
old 0x68: CKSEL=10, REFSEL=10, internal 2.5 V reference always on
new 0x64: CKSEL=10, REFSEL=01, external reference on the REF pin
```

The associated source comments now state that mainboard revision 2.2 ties
MAX11633 REF and VDD to the `+3.3V` network. Build, flash, and verification
results are recorded below after each operation.

Build result:

```text
SUCCESS
RAM used:    3392 bytes / 128 KiB (2.59%)
Flash used: 16672 bytes / 512 KiB (3.18%)
Output: ESKIN_STM32/build/Debug/ESKIN_STM32.elf
```

First flash attempt:

```text
pyocd flash -t stm32g474cetx -f 10k -M under-reset -e sector
            build/Debug/ESKIN_STM32.elf

The target was opened and sector erase started, but the command did not return
the required Programming/100% completion result.
```

Immediate verification retry:

```text
Target type is stm32g474cetx
Error: already open
```

This retry result means that the DAPLink interface was still occupied or had
not been released after the interrupted first operation. At this point the
firmware build was valid, but successful programming had not yet been proven.

Final flash retry after the stale interface was released:

```text
command:
  pyocd flash -t stm32g474cetx -f 10k -M under-reset -e sector
              build/Debug/ESKIN_STM32.elf

result:
  Erased 2048 bytes (1 sector)
  Programmed 2048 bytes (2 pages)
  Identical 15360 bytes (15 pages)
  Transfer rate 0.25 kB/s
  Process exit code 0
```

The small programmed-byte count is expected for an incremental firmware
change: pyOCD compared the remaining pages and reported them as identical.

A final hardware reset was then issued:

```text
pyocd reset -t stm32g474cetx -f 10k -M under-reset -m hw
Process exit code 0
```

The corrected firmware was therefore built, programmed, and restarted
successfully. The flashed ELF metadata is:

```text
size:     1226304 bytes (ELF file including debug information)
modified: 2026-07-27 16:17:10 +01:00
SHA-256:  99B2663F32D99DF7B722F247425851D0A4C7534603F1C042E4CB5E89A10612E9
```

Required next measurement step: discard the old per-cell zero/full calibration
and capture both endpoints again because the ADC reference selection changed
from the internal 2.5 V source to the board's external 3.3 V rail.

## 2026-07-27 - Calibration procedure after the 3.3 V reference flash

Calibration and STM32 programming are separate in the current implementation.
The corrected STM32 application has already been programmed. The calibration
maps are captured by the PC Heatmap and saved locally; they are not programmed
into STM32 Flash.

Procedure:

1. Power the Teensy and mainboard, close Arduino Serial Monitor/Plotter, and
   start `live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9`.
2. Wait for valid frames at approximately 25 frame/s. Calibration is blocked
   below 10 frame/s.
3. Remove every load from the array, wait for the readings to settle, click
   `Calibrate zero`, and keep all cells unloaded for 40 frames.
4. Apply the intended repeatable full-pressure reference uniformly to all
   256 cells, click `Calibrate full`, and hold the load unchanged for 40
   frames.
5. Confirm that the status reports the zero and full calibration as saved.

Each endpoint is the per-cell median of 40 received frames. Capturing a new
zero endpoint invalidates and removes the previous full endpoint. The result is
saved automatically to:

```text
teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/fsr_calibration.json
```

The PC displays each valid cell using:

```text
pressure = clamp((raw - zero) / (full - zero), 0, 1)
```

The implementation also supports a decreasing ADC response because the
full-minus-zero span may be negative. A cell is considered invalid if the
absolute endpoint span is below 50 ADC counts.

## 2026-07-27 - Heatmap raw-frame CSV save button

The live Heatmap now includes a third front-end button named
`Save raw frame`. Clicking it writes the most recently received, uncalibrated
STM32 ADC frame to a timestamped CSV file.

The transport matrix is stored as `[MUX column][ADC row]`. The CSV writer
transposes this once so the saved table uses physical coordinates:

```text
first column: R1 ... R16
header row:   C1 ... C16
cell Rn,Cm:   raw 12-bit ADC count for physical row n and column m
```

This export deliberately ignores Heatmap transpose, flip, colour scaling, and
zero/full calibration. It therefore preserves the original STM32 data while
presenting it with the corrected physical row/column naming.

Files are saved by default under:

```text
teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/raw_frames/
```

Example filename:

```text
fsr_raw_frame_00042_20260727T123456_123456Z.csv
```

The destination can be changed when starting the Heatmap with
`--raw-output-directory PATH`.

Verification:

```text
Python syntax parse: PASS
CSV header R/C labels: PASS
raw [MUX column][ADC row] to physical [row][column] mapping: PASS
```

The deterministic mapping test used a raw matrix containing 0 through 255.
The exported R1 row was `0,16,...,240`, and R16 was `15,31,...,255`, proving
that the transpose was applied exactly once. The first test interpreter
(`D:/app/python`) could not import Matplotlib because its `packaging`
dependency was missing; rerunning the same test with the installed Anaconda
environment passed. No STM32 or Teensy firmware change or reflashing was
required for this PC-only front-end feature.

Before changing the acquisition region, the complete verified STM32 project was


The mainboard 2.2 netlist was checked before editing. FSR2 uses:

```text
shared SPI1 SCK/MISO/MOSI: PA5 / PA6 / PA7
shared MUX address S0..S3: PA0 / PA1 / PA2 / PA3
ADC2 chip select CS2:      PB1, active low
ADC2 end of conversion:    PB11, active low
MUX2 enable EN2:           PB2, active low
```

The working `ESKIN_STM32` project was updated to scan only FSR2:

- MUX1 EN1 on PA4 is held high, disabling MUX1.
- ADC1 CS1 on PB0 is held high, deselecting ADC1.
- MUX2 is enabled on PB2 only after each address change and disabled after
  each 16-channel ADC scan.
- Every MAX11633 reset, setup, conversion, EOC wait, and read transaction now
  uses ADC2 CS2/PB1 and EOC2/PB11.
- The error-recovery path also disables both MUX devices, releases both ADC
  chip selects, reinitialises SPI3, and then reinitialises ADC2.
- `ESKIN_STM32.ioc` and `Core/Inc/main.h` now contain the PB1, PB2, and PB11
  definitions, with active-low outputs defaulting high.
- MAX11633 remains configured with setup byte `0x64`, using the external 3.3 V
  reference.

## 2026-07-28 - Reverse FSR2 row and column order

The user confirmed that both FSR2 axes run in the opposite direction from

```text

logical MUX index 15 -> physical MUX2 channel 0

logical ADC index 0  <- physical ADC2 AIN15
logical ADC index 15 <- physical ADC2 AIN0
```

```text
abs(full ADC endpoint - zero ADC endpoint) < 50 counts
```

The normalised value is then `NaN`, and the colormap renders it as RGB

This proves an axis exchange, not merely a reversed direction. Reversing an
index can only change left-to-right into right-to-left; it cannot turn vertical
motion into horizontal motion.

```text
frame[MUX index][ADC index]
```

to:

```text
frame[ADC index][MUX index]
```

## 2026-07-28 - Recurrent `Waiting for FSR frames from COM9`

The Heatmap again opened with no valid frames. Direct diagnostics established:

```text
COM9: Teensy USB serial present (VID:PID 16C0:0483)
COM10: DAPLink present (VID:PID C251:F001)
COM9 open: successful
valid frames during initial 10-second check: 0
```

Therefore this occurrence was not caused by a missing COM port, another
process owning COM9, calibration, or Heatmap colour rendering.

A 10 kHz under-reset STM32 hardware reset returned successfully but did not
immediately restore frames. The stuck serial test process was identified
exactly as PID 27128 and stopped to release COM9.

STM32 live diagnostics were then read from the flashed ELF symbols:

```text
captured PC:            0x080011DA
resolved function:      SPI_WaitFifoStateUntilTimeout()
current/observed stage: HOST_TRANSFER / ADC_READ during retry
last error stage:       HOST_TRANSFER
completed scans:        2399
completed frames:       2181
error count:            218
SPI transfer remaining: 0 TX bytes, 0 RX bytes in the last completed record
```

This localises the recurrent fault to intermittent STM32 SPI3-to-Teensy host
handshake loss. ADC2 acquisition, COM9 enumeration, and the Heatmap parser are
not the root cause. The debugger briefly halted and then continued the core;
the host link subsequently recovered.

Post-recovery direct validation:

```text
COM9 valid frames: 5
sequence: 2760..2764
gaps: 0
ADC range: 0..1782
```

The FSR2 Heatmap was reopened with `fsr2_calibration_v4.json` as PID 3976.
After three seconds the process remained running and responsive.

Likely trigger: STM32-only reset/reflash while Teensy remains running can leave
the SPI slave/master handshake temporarily out of phase. If this repeats,
resetting/power-cycling STM32 and Teensy together is the immediate recovery.
A permanent firmware change should target the HOST_IRQ/SPI3 recovery handshake,
not ADC sampling or GUI calibration.

## 2026-07-28 - COM9 waiting after restoring FSR1

Symptom:

```text
Waiting for FSR frames from COM9...
```

COM9 enumeration was verified:

```text
VID:PID = 16C0:0483
serial  = 14220980
device  = Teensy USB serial
```

The original Teensy bridge emitted only:

```text
# WAIT_IRQ level=0
```

An STM32 diagnostic read showed that ADC1 scanning was not the cause:

```text
fsr_completed_scans  = 87
fsr_completed_frames = 86
fsr_error_count      = 0
fsr_stage            = FSR_STAGE_HOST_TRANSFER
```

This proves that STM32 had completed FSR1 scans and successful SPI3 transfers.

The Teensy bridge contained a USB forwarding defect. It required
`Serial.availableForWrite()` to report at least the complete 515-byte frame
before calling `Serial.write()`. On a USB CDC buffer reporting less than 515
immediately available bytes, every valid frame was silently dropped. The
periodic `WAIT_IRQ` ASCII message could also be inserted between binary frames
and force the PC parser to reacquire alignment.

The bridge was changed to:

```text
1. call Serial.write(spiRx, 515) whenever USB Serial is connected;
2. allow Serial.write() to wait after the STM32 SPI transaction is complete;
3. stop inserting periodic WAIT_IRQ text into the binary frame stream.
```

The modified Teensy 4.1 program compiled successfully and was uploaded through
the Teensy Arduino core 1.62.0.

During the SWD diagnostic, an invalid resume sequence left the STM32 Cortex-M4
in `Lockup`. Both hardware reset and SYSRESETREQ were attempted, but the target
continued to report `Lockup`; the external NRST path did not clear it. COM9
therefore remained silent after the Teensy fix. A full removal of all possible
power/back-power sources is required:

```text
disconnect Teensy USB + DAPLink USB -> wait 5 seconds -> reconnect Teensy only
```

After that physical power cycle, the repaired bridge must be retested for
consecutive 515-byte frames before reopening the Heatmap.

### Resolution after the physical power cycle

The target still entered Cortex-M4 `Lockup` immediately after power-up. Flash
inspection identified the direct cause:

```text
vector table reset address: 0x0800406D
expected Reset_Handler:      0x0800406C
actual Flash at 0x0800406C:  0xFFFFFFFF
fault:                       UsageFault UNDEFINSTR -> HardFault Lockup
```

The earlier 10 kHz programming commands were being interrupted before the
complete image was written. Flash pages were populated only up to approximately
`0x080027FF`, while `Reset_Handler` was located later at `0x0800406C`. The
partial command output had shown erasing/programming progress, but it had not
reached a completed programming summary.

The FSR1 ELF was programmed again with a full chip erase at 100 kHz:

```powershell
pyocd load -t stm32g474cetx -f 100k -M under-reset -e chip --format elf `
  "D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_STM32_FSR1\ESKIN_STM32.elf"
```

Verified result:

```text
erased chip
programmed 17408 bytes / 17 pages
Reset_Handler bytes at 0x0800406C match the ELF
Cortex-M4 state: Running
```

COM9 verification over five seconds:

```text
received bytes:          64375
consecutive frame pairs: 123
first sequences:         981, 982, 983, 984, 985
effective rate:          approximately 25 fps
```

The FSR1 Heatmap was then started successfully with:

```powershell
python -u live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9 --region fsr1 --autoscale
```

The visible Python process was PID 27928 and its window was responsive.

## 2026-07-28 - FSR1 fixed-MUX individual ADC channel test

Purpose:

```text
fix one MUX1 position
read AIN0 100 times
read AIN1 100 times
...
read AIN15 100 times
send all unmodified 12-bit ADC codes to the PC
```

The working FSR1 firmware was preserved unchanged. A separate STM32 test
project was created:

```text
firmware/FSR1_ADC_CHANNEL_TEST/
```

The selected MUX position is controlled by:

```c
#define TEST_MUX_CHANNEL 0U
```

The initial test therefore fixes MUX1 at physical channel 0 / logical R1.

Each MAX11633 input is requested separately with conversion scan mode `11`
(one conversion from the selected AIN). Samples are stored channel-major:

```text
samples[AIN channel][repeat index]
16 channels x 100 repeats = 1600 raw samples
```

A separate Teensy test bridge and PC reader were created:

```text
teensy41/FSR1_ADC_CHANNEL_TEST_BRIDGE/
|-- FSR1_ADC_CHANNEL_TEST_BRIDGE.ino
`-- read_adc_channel_test.py
```

The dedicated binary test frame is 3206 bytes:

```text
byte 0       0xA6 test-frame magic
bytes 1-2   uint16 sequence, little-endian
byte 3       fixed MUX1 channel
byte 4       AIN channel count = 16
byte 5       repeat count = 100
bytes 6-3205 1600 uint16 raw ADC codes, little-endian, channel-major
```

No calibration, averaging, filtering, pressure conversion, normalisation, or
coordinate transformation is applied.

The STM32, Teensy, and Python sources compiled successfully. The Teensy test
bridge was uploaded first. Several pyOCD attempts stalled because the DAPLink
CMSIS-DAP HID endpoint remained occupied; all confirmed residual processes were
terminated. After physically reconnecting DAPLink, the STM32 test firmware was
successfully programmed at 100 kHz:

```text
chip erase complete
programmed 17408 bytes / 17 pages
```

The first STM32 test revision timed out at `FSR_STAGE_ADC_EOC_WAIT`. A
single-channel MAX11633 conversion completes in only a few microseconds, so its
EOC-low interval could finish before the blocking HAL SPI transmit returned and
GPIO polling began. The test firmware was corrected to wait a conservative
1 ms after every single-channel command before reading its two result bytes.
This changes test duration only and does not change the raw ADC result.

The corrected build and 100 kHz programming both passed. One complete test
frame was captured:

```text
sequence:       2
fixed MUX1:     channel 0
data rows:      100
CSV columns:    repeat + AIN0..AIN15
raw ADC values: 1600
```

AIN10-AIN13 are the clear low-response group at this fixed MUX position.
AIN0 and AIN15 have measurable signals but are substantially below

## 2026-07-28 - Live AIN10 monitor across all MUX1 positions

The fixed-MUX 1600-sample test was replaced by a direct serial-monitor test.
The purpose is to isolate AIN10 while checking every MUX1 input without saving
another CSV file.

Current STM32 behaviour:

```text
select MUX1 channel 0
wait 2 ms for settling
read MAX11633 AIN10 once
send one compact frame to Teensy
wait 1 second
repeat for MUX1 channels 1..15
wrap from channel 15 back to channel 0
```

The STM32 test source remains in:

```text
firmware/FSR1_ADC_CHANNEL_TEST/Core/Src/main.c
```

The matching Teensy source remains in:

```text
firmware/teensy41/FSR1_ADC_CHANNEL_TEST_BRIDGE/
FSR1_ADC_CHANNEL_TEST_BRIDGE.ino
```

The new eight-byte SPI frame is:

```text
byte 0      0xA7 live-frame magic
bytes 1-2  uint16 sequence, little-endian
byte 3      MUX1 channel, 0..15
byte 4      MAX11633 AIN channel, fixed at 10
bytes 5-6  raw 12-bit ADC code, little-endian
byte 7      XOR checksum of bytes 0..6
```

The Teensy validates the magic byte, MUX range, AIN number, and checksum, then
prints a human-readable line directly through USB Serial:

```text
seq=42  MUX=9 (position 10/16)  AIN10  ADC=1234/4095  V=0.9944
```

The displayed voltage is an estimate calculated as:

```text
voltage = raw ADC code * 3.3 / 4095
```

No calibration, filtering, averaging, pressure conversion, file logging, or
coordinate transformation is applied. The Python CSV reader is not required
for this test. Open the Arduino IDE Serial Monitor at 2,000,000 baud after both
matching firmwares have been programmed.

### Build, programming, and live verification

Both modified sources compiled successfully. The STM32 build used a short path
because the OneDrive project path exceeded the ARM compiler's object-file path
limit:

```text
D:\study\programming\builds\FSR1_AIN10_LIVE\ESKIN_STM32.elf
FLASH used: 16676 bytes
RAM used:    1864 bytes
```

The matching Teensy build was generated in:

```text
D:\study\programming\builds\TEENSY41_AIN10_LIVE
```

Teensy 4.1 upload completed successfully. The STM32 was then programmed through
DAPLink at 100 kHz with connect-under-reset and a chip erase:

```text
erased chip
programmed 17408 bytes / 17 pages
```

COM9 was monitored directly for 20 seconds at 2,000,000 baud. Sequence numbers
were continuous, MUX1 advanced through channels 4..15, wrapped to 0, and
continued through channel 8 with approximately one line per second. Example:

```text
seq=62  MUX=14 (position 15/16)  AIN10  ADC=3/4095  V=0.0024
seq=63  MUX=15 (position 16/16)  AIN10  ADC=3/4095  V=0.0024
seq=64  MUX=0  (position 1/16)   AIN10  ADC=3/4095  V=0.0024
seq=65  MUX=1  (position 2/16)   AIN10  ADC=3/4095  V=0.0024
```

All 16 MUX1 positions returned approximately 1..4 ADC counts during this

### Permanent GUI waiting-state fix

The Heatmap later remained on `Waiting for FSR frames from COM9` even after a
restart. Two independent checks proved that acquisition was still healthy:

```text
standalone fixed-frame reader: valid consecutive frames
Heatmap SerialReader thread:   frame 2387, 16 x 16, gaps 0
```

The remaining fault was therefore the GUI refresh mechanism. The script used
`figure.canvas.new_timer()` and started that backend timer before
`matplotlib.pyplot.show()`. On the active Windows Matplotlib backend, the timer
could fail to begin dispatching callbacks after the window event loop started.
The serial thread continued receiving frames, but the GUI never removed its
initial waiting message.

`live_fsr_heatmap.py` was changed to use `matplotlib.animation.FuncAnimation`,
whose event source starts after the canvas is drawn. A one-time terminal
confirmation was also added:

```text
Heatmap synchronized on COM9: frame 4120, 16x16
```

Python syntax validation passed and the corrected Heatmap remained running
after synchronizing. No STM32 or Teensy firmware change was required.

## 2026-07-28 - R13 and R14 remain high resistance under full pressure

Physical resistance measurements found that rows R13 and R14 remained much
higher resistance than the other rows even when the affected FSR area was
fully compressed.

For the current software convention, one-based GUI rows map to zero-based ADC
inputs:

```text
R13 -> ADC1 AIN12
R14 -> ADC1 AIN13
```

In the FSR-array 2.4 PCB, these are adjacent `/ain12` and `/ain13` nets on FPC
tail pads 13 and 14. In the mainboard 2.2 PCB, the corresponding adjacent nets
connect the FPC to `COL_ADC1-AIN12` and `COL_ADC1-AIN13`. Both nets have routed
objects in the KiCad PCB files, so the design files do not indicate a simple
completely missing net. This does not rule out a fabrication crack, connector
fault, assembly fault, or mismatch between the design revision and the
fabricated hardware.

Because two complete adjacent rows are affected, the leading fault classes are:

```text
1. adjacent FPC contacts 13/14 not making reliable contact
2. a cracked or delaminated flex/tail region affecting both row conductors
3. common mechanical stack-up preventing the R13/R14 active region from closing
4. printed conductive ink/electrode discontinuity across the two rows
5. mainboard AIN12/AIN13 routing, soldering, or ADC input damage
6. an invalid in-circuit resistance measurement through powered/connected ICs
```

The decisive isolation test is to power everything off, disconnect both FPC
tails from the mainboard, and compare R12/R13/R14/R15 directly on the bare FSR
tail using the same known-good column and a repeatable pressure fixture. If
R13/R14 remain high on the isolated sensor, firmware, Teensy, SPI, USB, the
MAX11633, and mainboard circuitry are excluded.

### FSR2 raw-frame line-fault analysis

The saved FSR2 frame
`fsr_raw_frame_07387_20260728T203307_577881Z.csv` was analysed using a raw-code
threshold of 100 counts. It contains 146/256 low cells (57.0%), compared with
48/256 (18.8%) in the selected FSR1 comparison frame. This comparison is valid
as a sensor-quality comparison only if both frames were captured under the same
mechanical load.

The FSR2 failures are structured as complete lines:

```text
complete low rows:    R3, R4, R5, R7, R12, R13
complete low columns: C1, C3, C8, C13, C15
```

With the saved-CSV physical convention:

```text
rows    -> logical MUX2 selections
columns -> ADC2 inputs
```

Therefore the implicated shared channels are:

```text
MUX2 logical rows: R3, R4, R5, R7, R12, R13
MUX2 physical inputs (reverse selection): I13, I12, I11, I9, I4, I3
ADC2: AIN0, AIN2, AIN7, AIN12, AIN14
```

This mapping follows the actual two-stage storage path:

```text
STM32: frame[adc_index][mux_index] = adc2_samples[adc_index]
CSV:   physical_matrix = matrix.T
```

Consequently, a CSV column that remains low for every row identifies an ADC2
input path, not one MUX2 input. The earlier row/column interpretation in this
section was reversed and has been corrected here.

The union of six failed rows and five failed columns explains 146 apparent bad
cells:

```text
6 x 16 + 5 x (16 - 6) = 146
```

This pattern does not support 146 independent FSR-cell failures. It points to
multiple common line paths on both FSR2 FPC interfaces, their connectors, flex
traces, or the corresponding ADC2/MUX2 board paths. A single stuck MUX address
bit is also unlikely because the affected MUX indices do not follow one binary
address-bit pattern.

### FSR2 C5 and C12 fixed-low investigation

A later raw table showed C5 and C12 remaining at approximately 23--28 ADC
counts while the other active columns were approximately 1450--1600. Under the
corrected saved-CSV mapping:

```text
C5  -> adc_index 4  -> ADC2 AIN4
C12 -> adc_index 11 -> ADC2 AIN11
```

The two values remain low across every active MUX2 row. This makes the MUX2
address logic and individual FSR intersections unlikely causes. The first
inspection targets are the two ADC-side signal paths: FSR/FPC pin mapping,

possible loading or clamping to ground, and finally the two ADC input channels.

The raw codes correspond to only about 19--23 mV for a 3.3 V, 12-bit scale.
Because these are uncalibrated raw values, the Heatmap calibration cannot create
this fault. If the user-confirmed physical FSR columns work, the physical names
being tested may not be the same lines as CSV C5/C12; a one-line-at-a-time
stimulus is required to establish the connector-to-CSV permutation.

### FSR2 full-matrix irregular jumping after the failed SWD connection

The live Heatmap subsequently showed a rapidly changing checkerboard-like
matrix with raw codes spanning approximately 0--3845. The transport status was
still stable at about 25.1 frame/s with `gaps 0`.

This status proves that complete 515-byte STM32-to-Teensy frames are arriving in
sequence. It does **not** validate the separate MAX11633 ADC2-to-STM32 SPI
transaction contained inside each frame. The Heatmap displays the received
12-bit samples directly and does not generate random per-cell values.

The leading causes, in priority order, are:

1. unstable mainboard/ADC2 VDD, ground, or external 3.3 V reference;
2. intermittent ADC2 CS2, EOC2, SCK, MISO, or ground connection, causing
   corrupted or floating 12-bit conversion words;
3. floating/high-impedance ADC inputs due to a poorly seated replacement FSR
   tail, allowing sample-and-hold carryover and environmental noise;
4. unstable mechanical contact within the replacement FSR stack;
5. software frame alignment, which is unlikely because the frame sequence has
   no gaps and the parser remains synchronized.

The simultaneous inability to obtain any SWD acknowledgement at 10 kHz, 1 kHz,
or 100 Hz increases the priority of target power and shared-ground inspection.
The decisive isolation test is to leave only the known power arrangement,
select one MUX2 channel for one second, and repeatedly sample a known ADC input:
a grounded input must remain near code 0 and a known reference input must remain
stable. Stable fixed inputs implicate the FSR/FPC; unstable fixed inputs
implicate ADC2 power/reference/SPI rather than the sensor.

### Heatmap waits after every FSR reinsertion

The repeated trigger is reinserting the FSR/FPC while the Teensy-powered
mainboard remains energised. During insertion, connector contacts do not mate
simultaneously. Power, ground, MUX, and ADC inputs can be briefly shorted,
floated, or exposed to ESD. This can brown out or reset STM32/ADC2 while the
Teensy continues running. The two ends of the HOST_IRQ/SPI3 handshake then
start in different states, so COM9 remains present but no valid `0xA5`
515-byte frames reach the Heatmap.

The mandatory reinsertion procedure is:

```text
1. close Heatmap and all COM9 serial monitors;
2. unplug Teensy USB and DAPLink USB;
3. wait at least five seconds for the mainboard rails to discharge;
4. insert and lock both FPC tails while unpowered;
5. reconnect Teensy first and wait about two seconds;
6. if debugging is required, reconnect DAPLink afterward with its 3.3 V supply
   wire left disconnected;
7. launch exactly one Heatmap process.
```

If the Heatmap still waits, remove both USB connections again and repeat the
full power cycle. Restarting only the GUI cannot repair a stopped STM32 or an
out-of-phase STM32/Teensy SPI transaction. Resetting only STM32 can also
reproduce the mismatch; if DAPLink is used, Teensy must already be running
before the STM32 hardware reset.

The durable hardware-assisted solution is to connect a spare Teensy GPIO to
STM32 NRST through an appropriate open-drain/reset interface and let the Teensy
perform a coordinated STM32 reset at bridge startup or after repeated IRQ
timeouts. Until that reset connection is implemented, an unpowered FPC
reinsertion followed by a complete power cycle is the reliable procedure.

### Cutting the unused tail from the two-sided FSR FPC

The FSR FPC artwork contains two complete overlapping copper networks: one
network and tail on each copper side. The unused tail is therefore not merely
an unconnected plastic tab. Cutting it can affect the assembled array even
when continuity of every retained-side trace appears correct.

Important mechanisms are:

1. Scissors or side cutters plastically smear copper across the thin polyimide
   cut edge. Copper from the two sides, or adjacent fingers on the removed
   network, can become bridged.
2. The cut exposes copper ends. Conductive debris, carbon/pressure-sensitive
   film, adhesive residue, moisture, or a metal support can create leakage from
   the retained network to the nominally unused network.
3. Cutting and bending at the narrow Y-junction can delaminate copper or create
   microcracks in the retained fan-out. A continuity beeper can still pass
   while resistance changes under bending or pressure.
4. The remaining unconnected copper grid becomes a floating electrode across
   the complete sensing area. It can retain charge and capacitively couple MUX
   switching edges into the high-impedance ADC network, producing ghost cells
   and irregular jumping.
5. Removing one tail changes local thickness, stiffness, and strain relief.
   The two FSR sheets can then sit with nonuniform spacing or preload, producing
   apparently electrical row/column faults.

Required unpowered checks are stronger than a simple continuity test:

```text
retained pad -> its electrode: low and stable resistance while gently flexing
retained pad -> every other retained pad: open/high resistance
retained pad -> every cut-edge/opposite-side conductor: open, preferably > MOhm
cut unused conductors -> each other: no unintended bridges
```

Inspect the cut edge under magnification for copper burrs, folded copper,
delamination, and conductive debris. Clean it and fully isolate it with a thin
polyimide/Kapton cover after confirming no shorts.

For a future revision, use separate one-axis FPC parts or provide a declared
copper-free cut neck. Keep all copper back from the mechanical cut line by the
FPC manufacturer's required fabrication clearance, have the board house form
the outline, and avoid manually cutting through a copper-bearing flex tail.

## 2026-07-29 - FSR1 calibration embedded into the STM32 image

The requested calibration architecture was implemented as a two-stage flashing
workflow. Calibration cannot be measured automatically by the programmer
because it cannot know when the sensor is unloaded or under the intended full
pressure. The raw firmware is therefore used to acquire both physical states,
then a helper embeds the resulting 256-cell endpoint maps into the final STM32
image.

New and modified files:

```text
FSR_left/Core/Inc/fsr_mcu_calibration.h
FSR_left/Core/Src/main.c
teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/build_flash_normalized_fsr1.py
teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/live_fsr_heatmap.py
```

For every cell, the STM32 final image calculates:

```text
increasing response: (raw - zero) * 4095 / (full - zero)
decreasing response: (zero - raw) * 4095 / (zero - full)
```

The result is rounded and clamped to `0..4095`. A 12-bit value has 4096
possible codes but its maximum numeric value is 4095. Cells whose absolute
`full-zero` span is below 50 ADC counts transmit zero, preventing division by a
noise-only calibration span.

The generated header currently has:

```text
FSR_MCU_CALIBRATION_VALID = 0
```

so the source continues to produce raw MAX11633 values until a complete
calibration is generated. The current `data/calibration/fsr1.json`, updated at
2026-07-28 23:35:20 UTC, contains `zero` but `full` is null. The generation
tool correctly refuses to produce a final calibration image in this state.

Required procedure:

```text
1. Run the raw FSR1 firmware.
2. Leave all cells unloaded and click Calibrate zero.
3. Apply the intended full pressure uniformly and click Calibrate full.
4. Release the pressure and close Heatmap so COM9 is free.
5. Keep the DAPLink SWD connection and stable Teensy mainboard power.
6. Run:

   python build_flash_normalized_fsr1.py --flash

7. After programming, launch:

   python live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9 --region fsr1 --mcu-normalized
```

`build_flash_normalized_fsr1.py` validates JSON dimensions and raw values,
generates both C endpoint arrays, reports the calibration-span range and invalid
cell count, uses the short Windows build directory
`D:/study/programming/builds/ESKIN_FSR1_NORMALIZED`, builds the Release image,
and flashes it at 10 kHz under reset. `--require-all-valid` may be added to
refuse programming if even one cell has a span below the threshold.

The Heatmap `--mcu-normalized` mode disables PC-side zero/full calibration,
uses a fixed `0..4095` scale labelled as pressure, and shows normalized
percentages. This prevents the embedded calibration from being applied a
second time.

Validation completed:

```text
Python AST syntax:                 passed
missing-full calibration refusal: passed
raw/fallback STM32 build:          passed, Flash 16880 bytes
enabled-normalization STM32 build: passed, Flash 18072 bytes
```

No normalized firmware was flashed yet because the full-pressure endpoint has
not been captured.

### False pressure after programming the first MCU-normalized FSR1 image

The user completed both endpoints, generated the header, programmed the
normalized image, and launched the Heatmap with `--mcu-normalized`. With no
intentional touch, the displayed frame still contained many nonzero cells and
reported:

```text
MCU range: 0..3302
peak:      80.6% at displayed R7, C13
```

The embedded header SHA-256 matches the current `fsr1.json`, so this is not a
stale calibration file or PC-side double normalization.

Calibration audit:

```text
span range:       3..1858.5 ADC counts
span median:      1544 ADC counts
|span| < 50:      2 cells
|span| < 100:     6 cells
|span| < 200:    11 cells
|span| < 500:    31 cells
negative spans:   6 cells
positive spans: 250 cells
```

Two screenshot cells demonstrate the problem:

```text
displayed R7,C13 -> raw matrix [12][6]
zero=1920, full=1858, span=-62
MCU code 3302 implies current raw about 1870
only a 50-count downward drift was magnified to 80.6%

displayed R5,C8 -> raw matrix [7][4]
zero=807, full=1858, span=+1051
MCU code 865 implies current raw about 1029
the unloaded baseline moved +222 counts, producing 21.1%
```

The first implementation deliberately supported both increasing and decreasing
cell responses and accepted `abs(full-zero) >= 50`. That is too permissive for
this FSR1 hardware, where 250/256 cells show the expected increasing response.
Small inverted spans are calibration noise/nonuniform loading, not valid
reverse-response cells. The firmware also has no zero deadband, so every
post-calibration baseline drift is converted directly into pressure.

Recommended correction before the next normalized image:

```text
accept only full > zero;
raise the minimum span from 50 to at least 200 counts;
add a per-cell unloaded deadband before scaling;
repeat zero after the assembled FSR has mechanically settled;
apply full pressure uniformly and without moving/reseating the stack afterward.
```

The zero deadband should be subtracted before normalization and the remaining
range rescaled to 0..4095. A longer unloaded capture or a stored zero-noise
envelope is preferable to a fixed global deadband.

### Calibration capture extended to approximately 10 seconds

The default Heatmap calibration length was increased:

```text
previous:  40 frames, approximately 1.6 seconds at 25 fps
current:  250 frames, approximately 10 seconds at 25 fps
```

This applies to both `Calibrate zero` and `Calibrate full`. Each cell still
uses the median of all captured frames. The longer window reduces sensitivity
to brief ADC noise and short mechanical transients, but the sensor must remain
completely unloaded or uniformly fully loaded for the entire progress count
from `0/250` to `250/250`.

The value can still be overridden explicitly, for example:

```powershell
python live_fsr_heatmap.py --port COM9 --region fsr1 --calibration-frames 500
```

At 25 fps, 500 frames is approximately 20 seconds. Python syntax validation
and the updated command-line help both passed.

### MCU calibration pipeline now enforces the 10-second captures

The MCU does not measure calibration during SWD programming; it embeds the
zero/full endpoint maps captured immediately before the final build. To ensure
that the MCU image cannot be generated from an older short capture, the
calibration JSON now records:

```json
"zero_frames": 250,
"full_frames": 250
```

`build_flash_normalized_fsr1.py` now requires both fields to be at least 250
frames by default. Old JSON files without frame-count metadata, or captures
shorter than 250 frames, are rejected before the C header is generated:

```text
Calibration generation failed: zero calibration was not captured with the
required 250 frames; recapture Calibrate zero
```

The generated STM32 header also records the zero and full capture counts in
its metadata comment. This makes the 10-second requirement auditable in the
final firmware source.

The threshold can be raised explicitly:

```powershell
python build_flash_normalized_fsr1.py `
  --minimum-calibration-frames 500 `
  --flash
```

It should not normally be lowered. Python syntax validation and the expected
rejection of the previous metadata-free calibration both passed.

The existing generated header from the earlier short calibration was also set
back to `FSR_MCU_CALIBRATION_VALID = 0`. Consequently, an accidental manual
build now falls back to raw ADC output instead of reusing the stale endpoint
tables. Only a successful new 250-frame zero/full generation run will enable
MCU normalization again.

### Peak-cell 3 x 3 neighbourhood test Heatmap

A separate diagnostic viewer was added:

```text
teensy41/ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE/test_peak_neighborhood.py
```

It leaves the STM32 firmware, Teensy bridge, 515-byte protocol, and main
Heatmap unchanged. For every received 16 x 16 frame it:

1. transposes the transport `[MUX column][ADC row]` matrix into the physical
   `[R][C]` convention;
2. finds the global maximum of that frame;
3. retains the complete 16 x 16 physical grid and all R1--R16/C1--C16 labels;
4. colours only the maximum and its eight immediate physical neighbours,
   leaving every other cell black;
5. marks the maximum at its true 16 x 16 location with a cyan border;
6. writes values only inside the visible neighbourhood;
7. naturally shows fewer than eight neighbours when the maximum lies on an
   array edge;
8. reports sequence, frame rate, gaps, and peak coordinates.

Raw-ADC launch:

```powershell
python -u test_peak_neighborhood.py --port COM9
```

MCU-normalized launch:

```powershell
python -u test_peak_neighborhood.py --port COM9 --mcu-normalized
```

Only one program may own COM9, so close the main Heatmap and Arduino serial
monitor before launching this diagnostic. Python syntax, a centre-peak 3 x 3
case, an edge-peak four-valid-neighbour case, and command-line help were
validated successfully.
