## Confirmed Stage 9 and Stage 10 root-cause correction (2026-07-29)

The final fault attribution for the ninth and tenth FSR investigation stages
is clarified below.

### Stage 9: FFC connector fault

The main Stage 9 fault was poor electrical contact at the mainboard FFC
connector. The intermittent contact produced missing or unstable complete
rows/columns even though the SPI transport and GUI continued to operate.

The connector fault was distinguished from a software fault because:

- continuity and signal measurements were not repeatable at the connector;
- the affected readings changed with the physical connector/contact condition;
- the STM32 frame sequence and approximately 25 frames/s transport remained
  valid, so the missing data originated before frame packing;
- the MUX, ADC, Teensy bridge, parser, and Heatmap logic passed their isolated
  tests.

The connector was resoldered and then replaced with a new connector. This
resolved the intermittent FFC contact fault and restored a stable electrical
path.

### Transition from Stage 9 to Stage 10

After the connector repair, the board-side hardware path was operating
correctly, but several specific FSR rows still could not display a normal
pressure response. Because these residual failures remained in fixed sensor
lines after the connector, MUX, ADC, SPI, USB, protocol, and GUI paths had
been verified, they were treated as a separate fault rather than evidence
that the connector repair had failed.

### Stage 10: FSR sensor fault

The Stage 10 isolation tests traced the remaining non-responsive rows to the
FSR itself. Direct resistance/pressure checks showed that the affected sensor
rows did not develop the expected conductive response even when the external
electronics and connections were sound.

The confirmed causal sequence is therefore:

```text
Stage 9 widespread/intermittent missing data
  -> poor FFC connector contact
  -> resoldered and replaced connector
  -> intermittent board connection fault resolved
  -> a smaller set of fixed rows still failed
  -> board, MUX, ADC, SPI, USB, parser, and GUI excluded
  -> Stage 10 isolation identified the FSR itself as the residual cause
```

Result: Stage 9 was solved by connector rework/replacement. Stage 10 established
that the remaining fixed-row failures were sensor-side defects and could not
be corrected by further STM32, Teensy, protocol, or Heatmap changes.

## ACC SPI array bring-up: 2026-07-29

An independent ACC test was created by retaining the proven STM32-to-Teensy
transport used for FSR testing and replacing only the sensor acquisition
stage. This keeps the 515-byte frame, sequence counter, `HOST_IRQ` handshake,
SPI3 slave recovery, Teensy USB bridge, and PC frame-lock parser unchanged.

### Hardware mapping

The mainboard schematic and netlist identify a shared SPI2 accelerometer bus
and a CD74HC154 active-low chip-select decoder:

- `PB13`: `SPI2_SCK` / ACC `SCK1`
- `PB14`: `SPI2_MISO` / ACC `MISO1`
- `PB15`: `SPI2_MOSI` / ACC `MOSI1`
- `PC13`: decoder enable `ACC_EN` (active low)
- `PB12`, `PF0`, `PF1`, `PA9`: decoder address bits A0 through A3
- ACC FPC pin 1: `+3.3V`
- ACC FPC pins 2, 3, 4: MISO, SCK, MOSI
- ACC FPC pins 6 through 21: `nCS16` through `nCS1`
- ACC FPC pins 22 through 24: GND

The fitted LIS2DH12/LIS3DH devices share the same required registers for this
test: `WHO_AM_I` at `0x0F` must return `0x33`; `CTRL_REG1=0x57` selects
100 Hz with X/Y/Z enabled; `CTRL_REG4=0x88` selects block-data update,
high-resolution mode, and the +/-2 g range. The data-sheet timing requires
SPI mode 3: clock idle high, data changed on the falling edge, and data
captured on the rising edge.

### Test implementation

The STM32 project is:

```text
stm32/tests/acc_spi_array/
```

The PC receiver is:

```text
teensy/tests/ACC_SPI_ARRAY_TEST/read_acc_array.py
```

No Teensy reflash is required because the production
`ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE` forwards the same fixed-width binary frame. Within the
16 x 16 transport matrix, each row represents one decoder position:

- column 0: `WHO_AM_I`
- column 1: status code
- columns 2 through 4: signed 12-bit X, Y, and Z
- column 5: MISO idle level
- column 6: command-phase receive byte
- column 7: STM32 HAL SPI2 error code
- columns 8 and 9: `CTRL_REG1` and `CTRL_REG4` readback
- columns 10 through 15: raw XL/XH, YL/YH, and ZL/ZH bytes

The build directory and image are:

```text
D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_ACC_SPI_ARRAY_20260729
D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_ACC_SPI_ARRAY_20260729\ESKIN_STM32.elf
```

The latest build passed. It uses 3,728 bytes of RAM and 16,564 bytes of
Flash. Two harmless warnings remain because the copied FSR/MAX11633 helper
functions are preserved but not called by this isolated ACC test.

The image was programmed successfully using:

```powershell
pyocd flash `
  -t stm32g474cetx `
  -f 10k `
  -M under-reset `
  -e sector `
  "D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_ACC_SPI_ARRAY_20260729\ESKIN_STM32.elf"
```

### Results and fault isolation

The first all-zero test was performed while the ACC assembly was not
connected. It is retained as a procedural observation only and must not be
used as evidence of an ACC hardware fault.

After the ACC assembly was connected, a 100-frame test produced 100 valid
frames, zero sequence gaps, and approximately 21 frames/s. Fourteen decoder
positions returned the correct `WHO_AM_I=0x33`, proving that STM32 SPI2,
the STM32-to-Teensy link, USB forwarding, and PC framing were operating.
Positions ACC1 and ACC2 did not provide a valid identity.

The first code version accepted a successful HAL transfer as successful
configuration. Its XYZ output contained repeated values such as decimal 819
(`0x333`), which are not credible acceleration data. Configuration readback
was therefore added. It showed:

- ACC1/ACC2: `WHO_AM_I=0xFF` in the repeat test, indicating an undriven MISO
  response for those decoder positions;
- ACC3 through ACC16: `WHO_AM_I=0x33`;
- expected `CTRL_REG1=0x57` and `CTRL_REG4=0x88` were not read back;
- observed readbacks repeated `0x33/0x33` or `0x77/0xBB`;
- HAL SPI2 error code remained zero;
- no sensor was allowed to report XYZ after the failed configuration
  verification.

SPI2 was then reduced from approximately 1 MHz to 62.5 kHz while retaining
mode 3. The same identity and configuration-readback pattern remained across
100 valid frames at approximately 22.8 frames/s. Therefore excessive SPI
frequency and long-FFC edge quality are not the primary cause.

Current conclusion: the host transport and most ACC read paths are proven,
but ACC register writes are not arriving correctly. The highest-priority
remaining checks are the common `PB15/MOSI1` route and decoder/CS behaviour.
The repeated correct identity does not prove MOSI write data is correct,
because all supported accelerometers return the same identity value. ACC1
and ACC2 additionally require continuity/selection checks on their individual
`nCS` paths.

The next isolation test should hold `PB15/MOSI1` low and high for one second
at a time and compare the voltage at the STM32 pin, mainboard ACC connector,
and ACC FPC endpoint. After MOSI continuity is confirmed, repeat the same
static-level test for the four decoder address inputs and observe the
selected `nCS` output. Do not interpret the current XYZ values as physical
acceleration until both control-register readbacks pass.

### ACC 3D GUI

A separate live visualization was added at:

```text
teensy/tests/ACC_SPI_ARRAY_TEST/live_acc_3d.py
```

It decodes the same verified 515-byte stream and displays all 16 logical ACC
positions as a 4 x 4 three-dimensional vector field. Each valid arrow shows
the signed X/Y/Z direction and acceleration magnitude in g. Clicking a marker
selects one sensor and reports its identity, status, X/Y/Z values, magnitude,
frame sequence, rate, gaps, and data age. Rows that do not have
`WHO_AM_I=0x33` and status `OK` are shown as red crosses rather than valid
vectors.

The parser and signed 12-bit conversion self-test passed:

```powershell
python .\live_acc_3d.py --self-test
```

The synthetic 25 Hz animation was launched successfully with:

```powershell
python .\live_acc_3d.py --demo
```

This demo mode permits GUI development while the ACC register-write fault is
debugged separately. Live mode is started with:

```powershell
python .\live_acc_3d.py --port COM9
```

## 2026-08-01 — ACC schematic-count correction and nine-device retest

Review of ACC revision 2.2 confirmed that the flex contains nine LIS2DH12TR
devices, not sixteen. Decoder addresses 0..8 select `nCS_1`..`nCS_9`; the ACC
FFC pins corresponding to decoder addresses 9..15 are not populated. The
earlier conclusion that ACC3..ACC16 returned valid identities is therefore
superseded: responses reported for ACC10..ACC16 could not have come from
independent installed sensors.

The STM32 test was changed from `ACC_COUNT=16` to `ACC_COUNT=9`. The command-
line reader and 3D GUI were also limited to the nine installed devices, while
the 16x16 STM32-to-Teensy frame was retained for transport compatibility.

The firmware rebuilt successfully (FLASH 16,564 bytes; RAM 3,648 bytes). The
first 10 kHz under-reset flash reached programming but failed because the core
was not halted. A hardware reset followed by the same flash command succeeded:
two sectors were erased, four pages were programmed, and thirteen pages were
identical. The STM32 was then hardware-reset to run the new image.

A 100-frame COM9 test produced zero sequence gaps at 20.58 frames/s:

- PASS: ACC2, ACC4, ACC5, ACC7, ACC8 (`WHO_AM_I=0x33`, status `OK`,
  `CTRL1=0x57`, `CTRL4=0x88`, and live XYZ values);
- FAIL: ACC1, ACC6, ACC9 returned `0xFF` and status `BAD_ID`;
- FAIL: ACC3 returned `0x00` and status `BAD_ID`.

This proves the shared SPI2 clock/data path and STM32–Teensy–USB frame path are
working. The remaining fault is restricted to the four physical sensor-select
branches or their local sensor/power/interconnect paths. Their physical
locations are ACC1 upper-right, ACC3 lower-right, ACC6 lower-centre, and ACC9
lower-left.

### Follow-up: blank GUI and reset-dependent ACC result

The 3D GUI later showed only its empty axes. Its nine-device rendering path was
verified independently with a synthetic frame and correctly drew all nine
vectors. COM9 could be opened, but no complete ACC frame arrived until the
STM32 was reset, so the blank display was caused by a stopped STM32 data stream
rather than a 3D plotting or layout error.

After the hardware reset, frames resumed with zero sequence gaps, but the ACC
initialisation result changed: ACC1 returned `0xFF`, while ACC2..ACC9 mostly
returned `WHO_AM_I=0x33` but failed control-register readback (`0x77/0xBB` or
`0x33/0x33` instead of `0x57/0x88`). Therefore the earlier fixed set of four
failing physical locations is not sufficient evidence of four permanent open
circuits. Reset-dependent SPI2/CS/MOSI electrical or initialisation behaviour
must be resolved before assigning the remaining faults to individual sensors.

## 2026-08-01 — Permanent ACC startup and runtime recovery

The all-red-cross GUI state was traced to a transient ACC initialisation
failure that became permanent in software. The old `ACC_InitAll()` attempted
each sensor only once and returned `HAL_OK` even when no sensor passed identity
and configuration readback. Consequently `acc_ready[]` remained zero and the
main loop never retried failed sensors until another reset or flash.

The ACC test firmware now implements the following recovery state machine:

1. Disable the CD74HC154 decoder so all nine `nCS` outputs are inactive.
2. Wait 1000 ms after STM32 startup for the Teensy-powered 3.3 V rail and all
   LIS2DH12 devices to complete power-on.
3. For ACC1..ACC9, attempt initialisation up to five times. Every attempt
   reads `WHO_AM_I`, writes `CTRL_REG1=0x57` and `CTRL_REG4=0x88`, and reads
   both registers back before declaring the sensor ready.
4. Continue sending the 515-byte diagnostic frame even when some sensors are
   not ready, so the GUI reports red crosses instead of becoming blank.
5. Once per second, retry only one failed sensor in round-robin order. This
   prevents nine simultaneous retry loops from collapsing the transport to
   approximately 1 frame/s.
6. If a previously ready sensor later fails an XYZ transaction, clear its old
   XYZ bytes, mark it `DATA_SPI_ERROR`, and return it to the background retry
   queue.
7. Keep successfully initialised sensors running while failed branches recover;
   a sensor fault no longer resets or suppresses the complete array.

New compile-time recovery flags are `ACC_POWER_UP_DELAY_MS=1000`,
`ACC_INIT_ATTEMPTS=5`, `ACC_INIT_RETRY_DELAY_MS=20`, and
`ACC_BACKGROUND_RETRY_MS=1000`.

The firmware built successfully with 17,164 bytes Flash and 3,664 bytes RAM.
It was programmed at 10 kHz with under-reset connection; six sectors were
erased, eleven pages programmed, and six pages were identical.

Verification results:

- first 100 frames: zero gaps, 18.40 frames/s;
- following 300 frames: zero gaps, 18.43 frames/s;
- after a further hardware reset, 100 frames: zero gaps, 18.50 frames/s;
- stable PASS: ACC1, ACC2, ACC4, ACC7, ACC8 (`WHO_AM_I=0x33`, status `OK`,
  `CTRL1=0x57`, `CTRL4=0x88`, and live XYZ);
- persistent `BAD_ID`: ACC3 returned `0x00`; ACC5, ACC6, and ACC9 returned
  `0xFF` despite repeated recovery attempts.

The common reset-dependent all-ACC failure is therefore resolved in firmware.
The four persistent branches are now isolated from that software fault and
should be checked individually for decoder/nCS continuity, local power and
ground, FFC/solder integrity, OR-gate output, MISO continuity, or a damaged
LIS2DH12.

## Command and flag reference (2026-08-01)

This appendix is the current copy-and-paste reference for the active firmware

old paths may no longer match the reorganised workspace. Commands below use
PowerShell. Run only one program that opens the Teensy COM port at a time.

### Common paths and reusable PowerShell variables

If Windows assigns a different COM port, change `$TeensyPort`. Check the
current ports with:

```

cmake --version
ninja --version
arm-none-eabi-gcc --version

& $PythonExe --version
& $PythonExe -m pip show pyserial numpy matplotlib

Install missing PC visualization dependencies with:

```

### CMake configure and build

Use a short external build directory because the OneDrive source path is very
long. Configure once after changing project location or deleting the build
cache, then use `cmake --build` for subsequent code changes.

```powershell
$Stm32Project = Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "stm32\tests\acc_spi_array"
$Stm32Build = "D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_ACC_SPI_ARRAY_20260729"

cmake `
  -S $Stm32Project `
  -B $Stm32Build `
  -G Ninja `
  -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Debug `
  --toolchain "$Stm32Project\cmake\gcc-arm-none-eabi.cmake"

cmake --build $Stm32Build --parallel
```

Current source/build mappings:

| Purpose | `$Stm32Project` relative to `$FirmwareRoot` | Recommended `$Stm32Build` |
|---|---|---|
| FSR1 raw/normalised application | `stm32\applications\fsr1` | `D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_FSR1_RAW_20260729` or `ESKIN_FSR1_NORMALIZED` |
| FSR2 application | `stm32\applications\fsr2` | `D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_STM32_FSR2_NORMAL` |
| ACC 9-device test | `stm32\tests\acc_spi_array` | `D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_ACC_SPI_ARRAY_20260729` |
| FSR1 AIN/MUX isolation test | `stm32\tests\fsr1_adc_channel` | `D:\study\programming\builds\FSR1_ADC_CHANNEL_TEST` |
| Host SPI `0x55` integration test | `integration_tests\SPI_PATTERN_TEST\ESKIN_STM32_PATTERN` | Create a fresh short build directory |

CMake flags used here:

| Flag | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `-S PATH` | STM32 source/project directory |
| `-B PATH` | out-of-source build directory |
| `-G Ninja` | generate Ninja build files |
| `-DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Debug` | debug symbols and debug optimisation level |
| `-DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release` | optimised release build |
| `--toolchain FILE` | select the ARM GNU embedded toolchain file |
| `--parallel` | compile independent files in parallel |

### DAPLink and pyOCD: normal workflow

Before SWD operations, close Cortex-Debug and stale pyOCD processes. Closing
the Heatmap is not required for SWD itself, but it is recommended while the
STM32 is being reset.

```powershell
Get-Process pyocd -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue | Stop-Process -Force
pyocd list --probes --no-config -v
```

Normal low-speed reset, flash, and post-flash reset:

```powershell
$Elf = Join-Path $Stm32Build "ESKIN_STM32.elf"

pyocd reset `
  -u $DaplinkUid `
  -t $Stm32Target `
  -f 10k `
  -M under-reset `
  -m hw

pyocd flash `
  -u $DaplinkUid `
  -t $Stm32Target `
  -f 10k `
  -M under-reset `
  -e sector `
  $Elf

pyocd reset `
  -u $DaplinkUid `
  -t $Stm32Target `
  -f 10k `
  -M under-reset `
  -m hw
```

pyOCD flags:

| Flag | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `-u`, `--uid` | select one DAPLink probe by unique ID |
| `-t`, `--target` | target MCU/CMSIS-Pack name; currently `stm32g474cetx` |
| `-f`, `--frequency` | SWD clock, for example `10k`, `100k`, or `100000` Hz |
| `-M under-reset` | assert NRST while initially connecting |
| `-m hw` | request hardware reset through NRST |
| `-e sector` | erase only Flash sectors needed by the new ELF |
| `--no-config` | ignore local pyOCD configuration files during probe listing |
| `-v` | verbose diagnostic output |

### SWD recovery after `No ACK`, `Unexpected ACK`, or `core is not halted`

Verify that STM32 VDD is stable, NRST is normally high, SWDIO/SWCLK are
continuous, and DAPLink GND is connected to mainboard GND. If the probe pin is
an output labelled `3V3`, do not connect it in parallel with Teensy power. If
it is explicitly a `VTref/Vref` input, connect it to the target 3.3 V rail.

```powershell
Get-Process pyocd -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue | Stop-Process -Force
pyocd list --probes --no-config -v

pyocd reset `
  -u $DaplinkUid `
  -t $Stm32Target `
  -f 1k `
  -M under-reset `
  -m hw

pyocd flash `
  -u $DaplinkUid `
  -t $Stm32Target `
  -f 1k `
  -M under-reset `
  -e sector `
  $Elf
```

Do not treat `pyocd list` reporting `Target: n/a` as proof that the STM32 is
absent: probe enumeration does not always open the target. A successful
target connection must show Cortex-M4 discovery or complete a reset/flash.

### Optional pyOCD GDB server for Cortex-Debug

Run this in a dedicated terminal and leave it open:

```powershell
pyocd gdbserver `
  -u $DaplinkUid `
  -t $Stm32Target `
  -f 10k `
  -M under-reset `
  --port 50000 `
  --telnet-port 50001
```

`GDB server listening on port 50000` means the server is running. The terminal
will not behave like a normal PowerShell prompt until the server is stopped.

### COM-port ownership and recovery

Only one process can open COM9. `PermissionError(13)` means another GUI,
serial monitor, or Python process owns it.

```powershell
Get-Process python -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
  Select-Object Id, ProcessName, MainWindowTitle

Get-CimInstance Win32_Process |
  Where-Object { $_.CommandLine -match 'COM9|live_fsr|live_acc|read_teensy|read_acc' } |
  Select-Object ProcessId, Name, CommandLine
```

Stop only the confirmed stale process:

```powershell
Stop-Process -Id <PROCESS_ID> -Force
```

After reconnecting Teensy, wait for Windows to recreate the COM port, reset
the STM32 if the GUI opens but no complete frames arrive, and then reopen one
reader or GUI.

The `.ino` files have no runtime command-line flags. Their important compile-
time constants are:

| Sketch | Constants |

| Normal bridge | `STM32_CS_PIN=10`, `STM32_IRQ_PIN=2`, `USB_BAUD=2000000`, `SPI_HZ=100000`, `FRAME_MAGIC=0xA5`, `FRAME_BYTES=515` |
| ADC-channel bridge | pin 10 CS, pin 2 IRQ, `SPI_HZ=100000`, `EXPECTED_AIN=10`, `FRAME_MAGIC=0xA7`, `FRAME_BYTES=8` |
| Pattern receiver | pin 10 CS, pin 2 IRQ, `SPI_CLOCK_HZ=100000`, `EXPECTED_BYTE=0x55` |

Changing these constants requires recompiling and uploading the Teensy sketch.

### FSR Heatmap: `live_fsr_heatmap.py`

Working directory:

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "teensy\applications\ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE")
```

Typical commands:

```powershell
& $PythonExe -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --port $TeensyPort --region fsr1
& $PythonExe -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --port $TeensyPort --region fsr2
& $PythonExe -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --port $TeensyPort --region fsr1 --mcu-normalized
& $PythonExe -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --demo
& $PythonExe -u .\live_fsr_heatmap.py --help
```

Flags:

| Flag | Default | Purpose |
|---|---:|---|
| `--port PORT` | required outside demo | Teensy serial port |
| `--baud N` | `2000000` | serial baud value |
| `--timeout S` | `0.5` | serial read timeout |
| `--refresh-ms N` | `10` | GUI polling interval; hardware frame rate still limits redraw rate |
| `--vmin N`, `--vmax N` | `0`, `4095` | fixed colour limits |
| `--region fsr1|fsr2` | `fsr1` | calibration/raw-data folder and region mapping |
| `--calibration-file PATH` | `data/calibration/<region>.json` | override calibration JSON |
| `--raw-output-directory PATH` | `data/raw_frames/<region>` | override Save raw frame folder |
| `--calibration-frames N` | `250` | zero/full capture length, about 10 s at 25 fps |
| `--min-calibration-span N` | `50` | minimum valid per-cell full-minus-zero span |
| `--autoscale` | off | colour limits follow each live frame |
| `--mcu-normalized` | off | interpret STM32 values directly as normalised 0..4095 pressure |
| `--transpose` / `--no-transpose` | transpose on | exchange raw MUX and ADC axes |
| `--flip-rows` | off | reverse displayed rows only |
| `--flip-columns` / `--no-flip-columns` | flip on | control default left/right reversal |
| `--demo` | off | generated data without COM port |

### Raw FSR frame reader: `read_teensy_fsr.py`

```powershell
& $PythonExe .\read_teensy_fsr.py --port $TeensyPort --frames 100 --summary-only
& $PythonExe .\read_teensy_fsr.py --port $TeensyPort --frames 1 --print-matrix
```

Flags: `--port` is required; `--baud` defaults to 2000000; `--timeout` to 2 s;
`--print-matrix` prints all 256 cells; `--summary-only` suppresses per-frame
lines; `--frames 0` runs indefinitely; `--max-time 0` disables the time limit.

### Selected-row response test: `test_adc_rows.py`

```powershell
& $PythonExe .\test_adc_rows.py --port $TeensyPort
& $PythonExe .\test_adc_rows.py --self-test
```

Flags: `--port`; `--baud 2000000`; `--timeout 2`; `--rows 1,12,13,14`;
`--baseline-frames 50`; `--test-frames 75`; `--threshold 50`; `--output PATH`;
and `--self-test`. Without `--output`, JSON is saved under
`data/row_tests/adc_row_test_<timestamp>.json`.

### Peak plus eight neighbours: `test_peak_neighborhood.py`

```powershell
& $PythonExe -u .\test_peak_neighborhood.py --port $TeensyPort
& $PythonExe -u .\test_peak_neighborhood.py --port $TeensyPort --mcu-normalized
```

Flags: required `--port`; `--baud 2000000`; `--timeout 0.5`;
`--refresh-ms 10`; and `--mcu-normalized` for STM32-normalised 0..4095 input.

### Build/flash calibrated FSR1: `build_flash_normalized_fsr1.py`

First capture both zero and full calibration in the Heatmap, close every COM9
owner, then run:

```powershell
& $PythonExe .\build_flash_normalized_fsr1.py --flash
```

Flags:

| Flag | Default | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `--calibration-file PATH` | `data/calibration/fsr1.json` | zero/full calibration source |
| `--project PATH` | `stm32/applications/fsr1` | STM32 source project |
| `--build-dir PATH` | `D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_FSR1_NORMALIZED` | output build directory |
| `--minimum-span N` | `50` | invalid below this per-cell ADC span |
| `--minimum-calibration-frames N` | `250` | required frames in each endpoint capture |
| `--require-all-valid` | off | stop if any cell is invalid |
| `--build` | off | generate header and build |
| `--flash` | off | generate, build, and pyOCD flash |
| `--probe UID` | `LU_2022_8888` | DAPLink selector |
| `--target NAME` | `stm32g474cetx` | pyOCD target |
| `--frequency RATE` | `10k` | SWD frequency |

### ACC command-line test: `read_acc_array.py`

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "teensy\tests\ACC_SPI_ARRAY_TEST")
& $PythonExe .\read_acc_array.py --port $TeensyPort --frames 100 --summary-only
& $PythonExe .\read_acc_array.py --port $TeensyPort --sensor 2 --frames 100
```

Flags: `--port COM9`; `--baud 2000000`; `--timeout 2`; `--frames 0` for
continuous capture; `--sensor 1..9` for one installed ACC; and
`--summary-only` for aggregate output.

### ACC 3D GUI: `live_acc_3d.py`

```powershell
& $PythonExe -u .\live_acc_3d.py --port $TeensyPort
& $PythonExe -u .\live_acc_3d.py --demo
& $PythonExe -u .\live_acc_3d.py --self-test
```

Flags: `--port COM9`; `--baud 2000000`; `--interval-ms 40`;
`--vector-scale 0.9`; `--demo`; and `--self-test`. A red cross means the row
failed identity/configuration validation; an empty plot means no complete ACC
frame has reached the GUI yet.

### FSR1 ADC-channel binary capture: `read_adc_channel_test.py`

This reader requires the matching STM32 `fsr1_adc_channel` image and matching
Teensy ADC-channel bridge, both using frame magic `0xA7`.

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "teensy\tests\FSR1_ADC_CHANNEL_TEST_BRIDGE")
& $PythonExe .\read_adc_channel_test.py --port $TeensyPort
```

Flags: `--port COM9`; `--baud 2000000`; `--timeout 15`; and
`--output-directory PATH`. The output is a raw CSV containing 16 AIN channels
and 100 conversions per channel.

### STM32 and protocol compile-time flags

These are C preprocessor constants, not terminal arguments. Edit them only in
the matching `Core/Src/main.c`, rebuild, and flash the resulting ELF.

| Project | Important constants and current meaning |
|---|---|
| FSR1 application | `FSR_ROWS=16`, `FSR_COLUMNS=16`, `FSR_OUTPUT_NORMALIZED=0` for raw ADC; set to `1` only with a valid generated calibration header; `HOST_FRAME_MAGIC=0xA5`, frame size 515 bytes |
| FSR2 application | 16x16 raw ADC acquisition through MUX2/ADC2; `HOST_FRAME_MAGIC=0xA5` |
| ACC test | `ACC_COUNT=9`, `ACC_WHO_AM_I_EXPECTED=0x33`, `ACC_CTRL_REG1_100HZ=0x57`, `ACC_CTRL_REG4_HR_BDU=0x88`, `ACC_SPI_TIMEOUT=10 ms`, `ACC_POWER_UP_DELAY_MS=1000`, `ACC_INIT_ATTEMPTS=5`, `ACC_INIT_RETRY_DELAY_MS=20`, `ACC_BACKGROUND_RETRY_MS=1000`, 16x16 compatibility transport |
| FSR1 ADC-channel test | `MUX_CHANNELS=16`, `TEST_ADC_CHANNEL=10`, `MUX_STEP_PERIOD_MS=1000`, `HOST_FRAME_MAGIC=0xA7` |
| Host SPI pattern test | `SPI_PATTERN_BYTE=0x55`, `SPI_PATTERN_TIMEOUT_MS=1000` |

Shared binary frame flags:

- normal FSR and ACC diagnostic transport: magic `0xA5`, sequence `uint16`,
  256 little-endian `uint16` values, total 515 bytes;
- ADC-channel isolation transport: magic `0xA7`, total 8-byte live frame in
  the current fixed-AIN stepping firmware;
- raw ADC range: 0..4095 for 12-bit samples;
- MCU-normalised range: 0 means stored zero-pressure baseline and 4095 means
  stored full-pressure baseline.

### Fast end-to-end checks

Check normal binary transport without opening a GUI:

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "teensy\applications\ESKIN_SPI_USB_BRIDGE")
& $PythonExe .\read_teensy_fsr.py --port $TeensyPort --frames 25 --summary-only
```

Check the current nine-device ACC firmware:

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "teensy\tests\ACC_SPI_ARRAY_TEST")
& $PythonExe .\read_acc_array.py --port $TeensyPort --frames 100 --summary-only
```

If either command reports `PermissionError(13)`, close the other serial
program. If it opens COM9 but waits indefinitely, confirm Teensy power and USB,
then reset the STM32 and retry.

## 2026-08-01: fixed ACC1/ACC5 WHO_AM_I isolation test

The complete nine-device ACC firmware was preserved as the active application
at `stm32/applications/acc_array/`. The existing
`stm32/tests/acc_spi_array/` copy remains unchanged for traceability.

A new test project was created at `stm32/tests/acc_a1_a5_whoami/`. It never
selects the other seven decoder outputs. Each frame performs exactly 100
independent `WHO_AM_I` reads at decoder address 0 (physical ACC1), deselects
all devices, then performs exactly 100 reads at decoder address 4 (physical
ACC5). nCS is released after every two-byte register transaction.

The 256-word compatibility payload is:

- words 0..99: raw ACC1 results;
- words 100..199: raw ACC5 results;
- words 200..215: magic `0xA15`, result counters and SPI diagnostics;
- words 216..255: reserved zero values.

Normal identity is `0x033`. Values `0x100..0x10F` denote STM32 HAL SPI errors
rather than bytes returned by the sensor. The normal Teensy application is
still used. Monitor the test using:

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "teensy\tests\ACC_A1_A5_WHOAMI_TEST")
& $PythonExe .\read_a1_a5_whoami.py --port $TeensyPort
& $PythonExe .\read_a1_a5_whoami.py --port $TeensyPort --frames 1 --show-samples
```

This test distinguishes per-device instability from interaction during the
full nine-device scan. If both devices pass 100/100 in isolation but alternate
between pass and fail in the full application, investigate decoder selection,
shared MISO contention, supply stability and dynamic nCS waveforms.

### Flash and measured result

The test ELF was successfully programmed with pyOCD at 10 kHz using
connect-under-reset. The loader erased 12288 bytes, programmed 11264 bytes and
reported 6144 identical bytes. A hardware reset then started the test image.

The first captured test frame produced:

- ACC1: `WHO_AM_I=0x33` for 100/100 transactions;
- ACC5: `WHO_AM_I=0xFF` for 100/100 transactions;
- combined STM32 SPI peripheral error: `0x000`.

Five further frames reproduced exactly the same result, giving another
500/500 successful identity reads for ACC1 and 500/500 `0xFF` reads for ACC5.
After another DAPLink hardware reset, ACC1 again passed 100/100 and ACC5 again
returned `0xFF` 100/100. No exchange of the logical ACC1 and ACC5 results was
observed in this fixed-address image.

Interpretation: the STM32 SPI peripheral completed every transaction, but at
decoder address 4 the shared MISO remained high and no `0x33` response was
received. This result removes the full nine-device initialization and scan
order from the immediate failure mechanism. It does not by itself distinguish
between the physical ACC5 branch, dynamic decoder/nCS selection, local supply,
SDI gate output, SCK arrival, and ACC5 SDO/MISO drive.

### Complete ACC application restored

The fixed A1/A5 test image was subsequently replaced by the complete
nine-device ACC application from build
`D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_ACC_SPI_ARRAY_20260729\ESKIN_STM32.elf`.
The first three upload attempts before reconnecting the hardware failed at
`DebugPortSetup` with SWD `No ACK`; no erase was started. After power/DAPLink
reconnection, pyOCD at 10 kHz under reset successfully erased 12288 bytes,
programmed 11264 bytes and reported 6144 identical bytes. Hardware reset then
started the full application.

One command-line validation frame reported:

- valid: ACC1, ACC2, ACC4, ACC7 and ACC8 (`WHO_AM_I=0x33`, `CTRL1=0x57`,
  `CTRL4=0x88`, valid XYZ data);
- ACC3: `WHO_AM_I=0x00`, `BAD_ID`;
- ACC5, ACC6 and ACC9: `WHO_AM_I=0xFF`, `BAD_ID`;
- STM32 SPI error code for all positions: `0x000`;
- Host frame sequence was continuous with zero gaps.

The `live_acc_3d.py` GUI was then launched on COM9 using the complete ACC
protocol.

## 2026-08-01: selectable single-ACC build and flash

The existing `stm32/tests/acc_spi_array` firmware now accepts the CMake cache
variable `ACC_SELECTED`. Value `0` preserves the original A1..A9 array scan;
values `1`..`9` restrict initialization, background recovery, and acquisition
to the selected physical ACC. The fixed Host SPI frame format is unchanged,
and rows belonging to unselected devices remain zero, so the normal Teensy
USB bridge and `read_acc_array.py` remain compatible.

The helper script performs configuration, compilation, and DAPLink flashing:

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "stm32\tests\acc_spi_array")

# Compile and flash only ACC5. The CMD wrapper avoids local PowerShell policy
# restrictions without changing the global execution policy.
.\flash_selected_acc.cmd 5

# Prompt for 0..9 interactively.
.\flash_selected_acc.cmd

# Restore the complete nine-device scan.
.\flash_selected_acc.cmd 0
```

The selection is made at compile time and cannot change until a different
image is built and flashed. This prevents activity on every unselected nCS
branch during an isolation test.

### ACC3 single-device upload and result

Direct invocation of `flash_selected_acc.ps1` was initially blocked by the
Windows PowerShell execution policy (`PSSecurityException`). This was not an
STM32, compiler, DAPLink, or firmware error. The upload was completed using a
child PowerShell process with `-ExecutionPolicy Bypass`; the global user and
machine policies were not modified. `flash_selected_acc.cmd` was then added as
the permanent project-local launcher for this restricted environment.

The `ACC_SELECTED=3` image compiled and linked successfully, then entered the
pyOCD erase/program sequence at 10 kHz under reset. A subsequent COM9 frame
confirmed the selected-image behaviour:

- only the ACC3 transport row was populated;
- all eight unselected ACC rows remained zero;
- ACC3 returned `WHO_AM_I=0x00` and `BAD_ID`;
- the STM32 SPI error code was `0x000`;
- frame sequence 119 was received with zero gaps.

Therefore the single-device selection and transport are working. The ACC3
failure is a sensor/branch response failure rather than an attempt to scan a
different decoder address.

## 2026-08-01: single-ACC mode promoted to the main application

The compile-time selection mechanism was promoted from
`stm32/tests/acc_spi_array` to the maintained production source at
`stm32/applications/acc_array`. With `ACC_SELECTED=1..9`, the main application
initialises, retries, selects, acquires, and transmits only the chosen ACC.
Every other decoder output remains disabled. `ACC_SELECTED=0` remains the
explicit full-array mode.

The fixed 515-byte transport envelope is intentionally retained so the Teensy
bridge does not require a second firmware variant. In single mode only the
chosen logical ACC row contains diagnostics and XYZ samples; all other rows
are zero. Use the application-local launcher:

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "stm32\applications\acc_array")
.\flash_selected_acc.cmd 3  # only ACC3
.\flash_selected_acc.cmd 0  # complete A1..A9 stream
```

The production application was built with `ACC_SELECTED=3`, flashed through
DAPLink at 10 kHz under reset, and started with a hardware reset. Three COM9
frames (sequence 112..114) arrived with zero gaps at approximately 17.5 fps.
Only the ACC3 row was populated; ACC1, ACC2, and ACC4..ACC9 remained zero in
every frame. ACC3 consistently reported `WHO_AM_I=0x00`, `BAD_ID`, and STM32
SPI error `0x000`. This runtime capture verifies that the main application no
longer accesses the other eight decoder outputs in the selected build.

## 2026-08-01: ACC hardware branch verification

The ACC connector, decoder/nCS branches, OR-gate network, and local passive
components were checked systematically before further firmware diagnosis.

### Connector and nCS-to-OR-gate continuity

- Continuity was checked from every CD74HC154 nCS output used by ACC1..ACC9,
  through the connector, to its corresponding OR-gate branch.
- An unreliable connector joint was found during this process.
- The connector was re-soldered.
- Repeated continuity measurements after rework confirmed that every required
  nCS branch reaches the intended OR gate reliably.

Result: **passed after connector rework**. The earlier connector contact fault
is considered repaired.

### Short-circuit and component checks

- The relevant connector pins, adjacent nCS branches, SPI nets, supply, and
  ground were checked for unintended shorts.
- No short circuit was detected in the tested ACC signal network.
- Every OR-gate branch passed the electrical checks.
- The associated local capacitors passed the component/connection checks.

Result: **passed**. No OR-gate, capacitor, or measured inter-net short fault
was found.

### Fixed single-ACC address verification

- The STM32 was built in single-ACC mode so that only one decoder address was
  selected continuously; the other eight ACC branches were not scanned.
- The four decoder address inputs were measured and compared with the
  CD74HC154 truth table.
- The active-low nCS output corresponding to the requested ACC was also
  checked.
- The measured address and selected output matched the requested ACC.

Result: **passed**. The STM32 GPIO-to-decoder address mapping and the
single-device firmware selection are correct.

### Current conclusion

The following candidate faults have now been excluded by direct hardware
tests: connector discontinuity after rework, incorrect nCS-to-OR-gate routing,
measured shorts, failed OR-gate branches, failed checked capacitors, and an
incorrect decoder address generated by the STM32. Any remaining `BAD_ID`
response must be investigated farther downstream on the selected sensor
branch, including local sensor power/ground, SCK, gated SDI/MOSI, SDO/MISO,
device soldering, and the LIS2DH12 itself.

## 2026-08-02: complete nine-ACC application restored

The main ACC application was rebuilt with `ACC_SELECTED=0` to restore the
original ACC1..ACC9 scan. `compile_commands.json` was checked before flashing
and confirmed that `main.c` was compiled with `-DACC_SELECTED=0`.

Initial programming attempts did not replace the previous single-ACC image.
The DAPLink session stopped during erase, later reported `No ACK`, and one
1 kHz pyOCD process remained in the background. That task-owned process was
terminated. The DAPLink USB connection was then unplugged and reconnected.

After reconnection, pyOCD at 10 kHz under reset completed successfully:

- erased 4096 bytes in 2 sectors;
- programmed 4096 bytes in 4 pages;
- verified 13312 identical bytes in 13 pages;
- reported no loader or verification error.

A hardware reset started the new image. Five consecutive COM9 frames
(sequence 84..88) arrived with zero gaps at approximately 16.0 fps. Unlike the
previous single-device image, all nine logical rows contained their own
diagnostic results:

- ACC1, ACC2, ACC4, ACC7, ACC8: `WHO_AM_I=0x33`, status `OK`,
  `CTRL1=0x57`, `CTRL4=0x88`, and live XYZ samples;
- ACC3: `WHO_AM_I=0x00`, `BAD_ID`;
- ACC5, ACC6, ACC9: `WHO_AM_I=0xFF`, `BAD_ID`;
- STM32 SPI error code: `0x000` for every ACC.

This confirms that the complete nine-device firmware is now running. The
remaining failures reproduce the previously established low-response ACC3
branch and high-response ACC5/ACC6/ACC9 branches.

## 2026-08-02: selectable ACC OR-gate static test

A separate STM32 project was created at
`stm32/tests/acc_or_gate_static` to test the per-ACC SDI OR gate with a
multimeter. It does not modify or replace the maintained ACC application
source.

At build time the user selects ACC1..ACC9 and a static MOSI level. The firmware
then disables SPI2, configures PB13/SCK as a constant low GPIO, configures
PB15/MOSI as a constant GPIO level, applies the chosen decoder address, and
keeps the selected nCS low continuously. No SCK edge is generated, so the
LIS2DH12 cannot interpret the static condition as an SPI transaction.

For the selected branch, the expected local gate equation is
`SDI = nCS OR MOSI`. With nCS held low, the gate output must follow MOSI:

- `MOSI=1`: output approximately 3.3 V;
- `MOSI=0`: output approximately 0 V.

Commands:

```powershell
Set-Location (Join-Path $FirmwareRoot "stm32\tests\acc_or_gate_static")
.\flash_or_gate_static.cmd 5    # ACC5, nCS=0, SCK=0, MOSI=1
.\flash_or_gate_static.cmd 5 0  # ACC5, nCS=0, SCK=0, MOSI=0
```

Both ACC5/MOSI-high and ACC5/MOSI-low configurations compiled and linked
successfully. The static test intentionally produces no Host SPI or USB
frames. After measurement, restore normal acquisition from
`stm32/applications/acc_array` with `flash_selected_acc.cmd 0`.

### Hardware result

The static OR-gate test was completed successfully. The measured behaviour
fully matched the expected truth table for the selected ACC branch:

| Test point | MOSI-high test | MOSI-low test |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| Selected `nCS` | approximately 0 V | approximately 0 V |
| `SCK` | approximately 0 V | approximately 0 V |
| STM32 `MOSI` | approximately 3.3 V | approximately 0 V |
| OR-gate output / selected ACC `SDI` | approximately 3.3 V | approximately 0 V |

Because `nCS=0`, the equation `SDI = nCS OR MOSI` reduces to `SDI = MOSI`.
The output followed both the high and low MOSI states correctly. Therefore,
the tested OR gate, its static logic function, and the associated selected
signal path are considered verified and are not the cause of the current ACC
communication failures. MISO was not used as a pass/fail criterion because
SCK remained static and no valid SPI read transaction was generated.

### Attempt to restore the complete ACC application

The maintained `stm32/applications/acc_array` project was configured with
`ACC_SELECTED=0` and built successfully, producing the complete nine-ACC ELF
image. DAPLink was enumerated as `jixin.pro CMSIS-DAP_LU`, UID
`LU_2022_8888`. Flashing was attempted under reset at both 10 kHz and 1 kHz.
Both attempts stopped before erase/programming at pyOCD `DebugPortSetup` with
`SWD/JTAG communication failure (No ACK)`. Consequently, this attempt did not
replace the firmware already in STM32 Flash. The compiled application image is
ready, but SWDIO, SWCLK, NRST, target power/reference, and common ground must be
restored before flashing is repeated.

The missing SWD cable was then identified and reconnected. DAPLink enumeration
subsequently completed normally in about one second, confirming that the probe
and its USB connection were accessible. However, both a 10 kHz under-reset
flash and a 10 kHz normal-connect flash stalled before any erase/program
message. The stalled pyOCD child processes were terminated so the probe was not
left occupied. This shows that the full application image remains ready but was
still not programmed; the remaining fault is on the powered target/SWD path
rather than in the build, pyOCD enumeration, or NRST connection mode alone.

## 2026-08-03: one-second ACC SCK continuity test

A separate diagnostic project was created at `stm32/tests/acc_sck_slow` so the
shared accelerometer clock can be measured with a multimeter. It disables
SPI2, configures PB13 as a GPIO, holds SCK low for one second and high for one
second, and repeats continuously. PC13/ACC_EN remains high so every CD74HC154
nCS output stays inactive; PB15/MOSI remains low. Consequently no LIS2DH12 is
selected and the slow SCK edges cannot form a valid sensor transaction.

Expected values at STM32 PB13, mainboard ACC connector pin 3, ACC FPC pin 3,
and every LIS2DH12 SCL/SPC pin are approximately 0 V for one second followed
by the board I/O rail (normally 3.2-3.3 V) for one second. All nCS lines should
remain approximately 3.3 V throughout the test.

The project compiled and linked successfully (RAM 1912 bytes, Flash 11540
bytes). DAPLink enumerated normally as UID `LU_2022_8888`, but both the scripted
and direct 10 kHz under-reset flash attempts stalled before erase/programming.
Residual pyOCD processes were terminated. Therefore the diagnostic is ready
but was not programmed in this attempt; the existing STM32 firmware remains
unchanged until SWD target communication is restored.
# ACC3 slow nCS continuity test (2026-08-03)

An isolated `stm32/tests/acc_ncs_slow` diagnostic was added for meter-visible
chip-select testing. By default, SCK and MOSI remain at 0 V, and
only ACC3 nCS alternates between high for 1 second and low for 1 second. All
other nCS outputs remain high. With no SCK edge, no SPI command is transferred.
Use `.\flash_acc_ncs_slow.cmd 3`, then restore the acquisition firmware with
`.\flash_selected_acc.cmd 3` after testing.
# ACC OR-gate and selection-path verification (2026-08-03)

Hardware testing confirmed that all ACC OR gates operate correctly. Each gate
produced the expected truth-table outputs with its selected nCS held low and
MOSI tested at both static logic levels. The shared MOSI path, each local SDI
path after the OR gate, and the individual active-low nCS selection paths were
also verified and found correct.

**Conclusion:** the present ACC communication failures are not caused by the
OR gates, MOSI-to-SDI routing, or nCS routing/selection. Further diagnosis
should focus on SCK arrival at each device, VDD/VDD_IO, the shared SDO/MISO
return path, LIS2DH12 solder joints, and the sensors themselves.
# Selectable slow-MOSI ACC test (2026-08-03)

Added `stm32/tests/acc_mosi_slow`. The user selects ACC1..ACC9 at build/flash
time. SCK stays at 0 V, the selected nCS stays at 0 V, all other nCS outputs
stay high, and MOSI alternates between 0 V for 1 second and approximately
3.3 V for 1 second. With the selected nCS low, its local OR-gate SDI output
must follow MOSI; unselected SDI outputs must remain high. No SCK edge is
generated, so no valid SPI command is transferred. Example:
`.\flash_acc_mosi_slow.cmd 3`.
# ACC3/ACC5 removal and exposed-pad verification (2026-08-03)

The LIS2DH12 devices at physical ACC3 and ACC5 were removed to expose their
pads. Measurements at the unpopulated locations confirmed correct nCS, SCK,
and MOSI/SDI behaviour. The corresponding OR-gate paths were also verified as
correct. Despite the devices being absent, the live ACC summary continued to
report different open-device signatures:

- logical ACC3: `MISO=0`, `command RX=0x00`, `WHO_AM_I=0x00`, `BAD_ID`;
- logical ACC5: `MISO=1`, `command RX=0xFF`, `WHO_AM_I=0xFF`, `BAD_ID`.

ACC5 is the expected absent-slave signature: no device drives MISO and the
shared line remains high. ACC3 is not the expected absent-slave signature.
Because the removed sensor, OR gate, nCS, SCK, and SDI paths have been
excluded, follow-up diagnosis must target the SDO/MISO return network, confirm
logical-to-physical decoder mapping at the exposed CS pads, and exclude stale
or mismatched firmware/frames. In particular, compare the exposed ACC3 and
ACC5 SDO pads to STM32 PB14 and test for bridges from ACC3 SDO to GND, SDI, or
nCS with power removed.
# ACC3 short repair and ACC5 intermittent recovery (2026-08-03)

A physical short between ACC3 SDO and nCS was identified and repaired. This
explains the earlier ACC3 low-bus signature (`MISO=0`, `command RX=0x00`,
`WHO_AM_I=0x00`), because asserting the active-low nCS could simultaneously
pull SDO/MISO low. After removing the short, ACC3 still did not return to
normal operation.

During subsequent testing, ACC5 became operational for a brief instant and
then immediately stopped working again. The stable devices remained ACC1,
ACC2, ACC4, ACC7, and ACC8; ACC3, ACC6, and ACC9 remained unavailable, while
ACC5 showed this new intermittent behaviour.

**Current interpretation:** repairing the ACC3 bridge removed one confirmed
fault but does not prove that its SDO continuity, supply pads, LGA solder
joints, or sensor survived the original short. ACC5's momentary response is
strong evidence for an intermittent connection, supply, or solder-joint
problem, although a single `0x33` must be confirmed by repeated WHO_AM_I and
configuration-register reads. The current ACC firmware also caches a sensor
as ready after one successful initialisation and does not periodically
revalidate WHO_AM_I, so a brief contact can leave a misleading displayed state
after the connection is lost again.
# ACC3 result after SDO-nCS short repair (2026-08-03)

A fresh ACC summary after repairing the ACC3 SDO-nCS bridge changed the ACC3
signature from all-low to all-high: `MISO=1`, `command RX=0xFF`, and
`WHO_AM_I=0xFF`. This confirms that the selection-dependent low clamp was
removed successfully. ACC3 is still not communicating, but it now behaves as
an absent or non-driving SPI slave rather than a line shorted low. ACC5, ACC6,
and ACC9 also reported the same `0xFF` / `BAD_ID` signature. ACC1, ACC2, ACC4,
ACC7, and ACC8 continued to return `0x33` with `CTRL1=0x57` and `CTRL4=0x88`.

**Updated conclusion:** the confirmed ACC3 bridge repair was effective. Any
remaining ACC3 fault is now in device presence, power/VDD_IO, SCK/SDI arrival
at the actual package connection, SDO continuity, LGA soldering, or the sensor
itself; the former low-bus short is no longer present.
# ACC5 cached-ready / live-data-loss evidence (2026-08-03)

The latest live summary reported ACC5 as `ID=0x33`, `status=OK`,
`CTRL1=0x57`, and `CTRL4=0x88`, but its current raw axis bytes were
`FF/FF/FF/FF/FF/FF` and X/Y/Z all decoded to `-1`. This is direct evidence
that ACC5 successfully completed WHO_AM_I and register configuration at least
once, after which its live SDO/MISO data path was lost or the device stopped
responding.

The apparent `OK` state is a firmware limitation rather than proof of current
health. WHO_AM_I and CTRL values are cached after initialisation. A master SPI
transaction still returns `HAL_OK` when no slave drives MISO, so the firmware
accepts six pulled-high bytes as a successful axis read. Each `0xFFFF` axis
word shifts to signed value `-1`, producing the exact displayed result. The
firmware currently does not periodically revalidate a ready device or reject
all-`0xFF` axis frames.

**Interpretation:** ACC5 has an intermittent hardware connection, supply, or
LGA solder issue unless it is physically absent, in which case the displayed
ID/configuration are necessarily cached. The transport status must be fixed to
detect all-`0xFF`/all-`0x00` reads and periodically repeat WHO_AM_I/configuration
checks before `OK` can mean currently operational.
# ACC 3D GUI left-right mirror (2026-08-03)

The default `live_acc_3d.py` display was mirrored left-to-right. Both sensor
positions and the displayed X component of each acceleration vector are
reflected, while ACC IDs remain bound to their original data records. The
built-in GUI self-test passed after the change.
# ACC3/ACC8 anomalous values and transport-corruption evidence (2026-08-03)

The latest summary showed major recovery: ACC3 and ACC5 both returned
`WHO_AM_I=0x33` with `CTRL1=0x57` and `CTRL4=0x88`; only ACC6 and ACC9
remained `0xFF/BAD_ID`. However, the same capture contained values that cannot
all be valid sensor data:

- ACC3 `MISO` history contained both `1` and `1793`. The firmware field is a
  single GPIO sample and can only be 0 or 1, so `1793` proves at least one
  16-bit transport word was corrupted after frame construction.
- ACC3 Y remained at `2040 mg`, close to positive full-scale saturation, while
  Z remained approximately `984..997 mg`; this gives an impossible stationary
  magnitude above 2 g and may be either corrupted transport data or a damaged/
  stressed sensor axis.
- ACC8 reported X=`0`, Y=`254`, and Z=`-253 mg`, giving only about 0.36 g total
  magnitude. This is also inconsistent with a stationary, correctly configured
  accelerometer and requires validation before being treated as physical data.

The PC frame reader verifies frame boundaries, consecutive sequence numbers,
and that every word is at most 12 bits, but the current 515-byte protocol has
no checksum/CRC. A corrupted word such as 1793 is still within 0..4095 and is
therefore accepted. Consequently, the impossible MISO value is direct evidence
that the STM32-to-Teensy/USB transport can corrupt individual payload words
without losing frame lock.

**Current interpretation:** communication recovery of ACC3 and ACC5 is real at
the identity/configuration level, but ACC3 and ACC8 axis anomalies cannot yet
be assigned solely to LGA/MEMS damage. First add end-to-end frame integrity
(CRC or checksum), reject impossible diagnostic fields, and repeat raw captures.
If the same axes remain abnormal in CRC-valid frames, then investigate package
stress, LGA soldering, supply/decoupling, or sensor damage.

# Combined FSR1 + FSR2 + ACC application (2026-08-04)

A new non-destructive application was created at
`stm32/applications/combined_system`; the existing `fsr1`, `fsr2`, and
`acc_array` applications were retained unchanged. One acquisition cycle now:

1. scans all 16 MUX1 positions and all 16 ADC1 channels for FSR1;
2. scans reversed MUX2 and ADC2 using the verified FSR2 transpose mapping;
3. revalidates and reads all nine LIS2DH12 accelerometers; and
4. sends one versioned 1188-byte frame to Teensy through Host SPI3.

The new `ESK1` frame includes flags, a 32-bit sequence number, the STM32 tick,
both 256-word FSR matrices, nine 16-byte ACC records, and an IEEE CRC32. This
directly addresses the earlier unprotected 515-byte protocol in which a corrupt
but numerically valid word such as the impossible `MISO=1793` was accepted.

ACC live validation was also corrected: `WHO_AM_I` is reread in every frame,
all-`0x00` and all-`0xFF` axis blocks are rejected, and one failed sensor is
retried each second. A disconnected device can no longer retain a cached OK
state indefinitely.

The STM32 cross-build completed successfully on 2026-08-04. Result:
`ESKIN_STM32.elf`, Flash 19,788 bytes (3.77%), RAM 5,472 bytes (4.17%). The only
compiler warnings are unused legacy static functions inherited from the copied
ACC CubeMX application; the new combined module itself compiled without error.
A prebuilt ELF was saved under `combined_system/prebuilt`.

A matching Teensy application and GUI were created at
`teensy/applications/ESKIN_COMBINED_BRIDGE`. The bridge validates magic, version,
declared length, and CRC before forwarding a frame to USB. The Python monitor
shows FSR1 and FSR2 as separate 16 x 16 heatmaps and the nine accelerometers in
one mirrored 3D view. Its synthetic parse/CRC self-test passed, including a
deliberately corrupted frame that was correctly rejected. Teensy compilation
was not executed because no Arduino CLI executable was found; upload through
Arduino IDE with the Teensy 4.1 board package.

## Combined flash-script path fix and SWD result (2026-08-04)

The first `flash_combined.cmd` run failed because `%~dp0` ends with a backslash.
When the long project path was placed inside the CMake `-S` quotes, Windows argv
parsing consumed the closing quote and split the later toolchain argument near
the path's ` - ` characters. `SOURCE` was changed to `%~dp0.` and the toolchain
path now inserts an explicit separator. The exact user command then configured
and rebuilt all 24 objects successfully, proving the CMake path issue fixed.

Flashing subsequently failed at the separate SWD stage with
`ResetHardwareAssert: expected DAP_SWJ_PINS`. A 10 kHz `halt` fallback also
timed out without attaching to the STM32. The generated ELF is valid; the
remaining blocker is the current DAPLink/SWD/nRESET target connection or probe
state, not compilation. The two pyOCD processes left after the timeout were
stopped. `-W/--no-wait` was added to future flash/reset commands so a missing or
busy probe returns an error instead of indefinitely blocking the terminal.

## DAPLink USB endpoint hang after successful combined build (2026-08-04)

A later run correctly reported `ninja: no work to do`, meaning the combined ELF
was already current. It then stopped producing output because pyOCD remained
blocked. The exact pyOCD/Python processes were confirmed and terminated.
`pyocd list --probes --verbose` also timed out, so the failure occurs even
before connecting to the STM32 target.

Windows still enumerated the probe as the started USB composite device
`USB\VID_C251&PID_F001\LU_2022_8888`, with COM10 and CMSIS-DAP HID interfaces.
Selecting `LU_2022_8888` explicitly did not remove the timeout. This isolates
the current fault to a wedged jixin CMSIS-DAP HID endpoint/driver/firmware state,
rather than CMake, the ELF, probe selection, or the STM32 application. A Windows
PnP restart was attempted but denied because the session was not elevated.
Physically unplugging the DAPLink USB cable for several seconds, reconnecting it
(preferably to another USB port), and then retesting `pyocd list --probes` is
required before another flash attempt. A clear `[3/3] Flashing...` marker was
added to the script so future runs visibly distinguish build completion from
the pyOCD stage.

# Central command organization (2026-08-04)

All active Windows `.cmd` launchers were consolidated under
`firmware/tools/commands`. Duplicate `flash_selected_acc.cmd` names were replaced
by the unambiguous `flash_acc_array.cmd` and `flash_acc_spi_test.cmd`. Central
wrappers now resolve their STM32/Teensy projects from the firmware root, while
the implementation `.ps1` files remain beside their source projects.

`docs/COMMAND_REFERENCE.md` was added as the concise operational source of
truth. It covers the combined system, standalone FSR/ACC firmware, diagnostic
burn commands, Teensy sketches, every argparse flag and default, CMake/pyOCD
flags, expected test behaviour, and fast fault isolation. Active project
READMEs were updated to point to the centralized commands. A generic
`flash_stm32_project.cmd` provides safe aliases for FSR1, FSR2, FSR1 ADC,
ACC WHO_AM_I, and SPI-pattern firmware. The combined CMake project no longer
advertises the inherited `ACC_SELECTED` cache setting because combined mode
always scans all nine accelerometers.

## FSR2 build success and invalid SWD ACK (2026-08-04)

The centralized command `flash_stm32_project.cmd fsr2` configured and linked
FSR2 successfully (Flash 16,848 bytes; RAM 3,392 bytes). The yellow
`CMAKE_TOOLCHAIN_FILE` unused warning came from reusing an already-configured
CMake build cache and did not affect the ELF. Central combined/generic scripts
were changed to `cmake --fresh` so the toolchain is consumed during a clean
configuration and the misleading cache warning is removed.

The subsequent flash failed with `SWD/JTAG communication failure (Unexpected
ACK '5')`. SWD ACK values are encoded responses and `5` is not a valid single
OK/WAIT/FAULT response; it indicates a corrupted sampled response or unstable
debug connection. Given the already confirmed cable-contact fault and the
10 kHz clock already in use, the primary action is to reseat and mechanically
stabilize SWDIO, SWCLK, GND, target reference, and nRESET before retrying. This
failure occurred after a valid ELF build and is not an FSR2 code error.

## Combined Host SPI 500 kHz trial (2026-08-04)

The Teensy combined bridge Host SPI clock was increased from 100 kHz to
500 kHz. STM32 SPI3 remains a Mode-0 hardware-NSS slave and requires no baud
setting change. The 1188-byte frame wire time falls from approximately 95 ms to
19 ms. End-to-end CRC32 remains mandatory: a frame with any high-speed link
corruption is discarded by Teensy before USB transmission. The expected total
rate is now governed mainly by the two FSR scans and ACC transactions rather
than Host SPI, and should increase from roughly 5 Hz toward 10-15 Hz depending
on ADC/ACC response and failed-device retries. Arduino IDE upload is required
before the new speed is active on hardware.

## Combined flash reached erase but CMSIS-DAP transfer failed (2026-08-04)

After physically restoring the probe connection, the combined script found the
DAPLink, loaded the correct ELF, and began sector erase. The first attempt then
reported `cannot write registers pc, r0, lr because core #0 is not halted`.
The remaining pyOCD/Python processes were stopped and the command was retried
with explicit probe UID `LU_2022_8888`, 10 kHz SWD, `under-reset`, and
`reset_type=hw`.

The explicit hardware-reset attempt passed the earlier core-halt point and
again entered erase, but failed with `DAP_TRANSFER response error: response is
for command 00` during board cleanup/disconnect. This is a malformed or
out-of-sequence CMSIS-DAP HID response, indicating instability in the jixin
probe firmware, its USB cable/port, or the Windows HID endpoint rather than a
compile/link error. No DAPLink mass-storage drive was exposed, so drag-and-drop
flashing is unavailable. Because sector erase had already started, the previous
STM32 application may now be partially erased and must not be expected to run
until a complete flash succeeds. The script now permanently selects this probe
UID and requests explicit hardware reset; the next attempt should follow a full
physical USB power cycle (and preferably another USB cable/port).

### Confirmed root cause and resolution

The user found that one of the DAPLink/SWD cable connections had poor physical
contact. Reseating the cable restored the connection. This confirms that the
earlier intermittent probe enumeration, failure to halt the Cortex-M4, and
malformed `DAP_TRANSFER` response were all consequences of an unstable physical
debug connection rather than the combined firmware, CMake build, CMSIS-Pack, or
pyOCD command configuration. The cable/header connection should be treated as
the first inspection point if the same symptoms return.
