# 2026-08-05 — Combined acquisition first-stage speed increase

Objective: raise the complete FSR1 + FSR2 + nine-ACC source rate well above the
measured approximately 9 frame/s without changing the 1188-byte `ESK1` protocol.

## Implemented changes

| Path | Previous implementation | New implementation | Expected saving |
|---|---|---|---:|
| FSR MUX settling | 32 separate `HAL_Delay(1)` calls per frame | DWT cycle-counter delay, 100 us per shared MUX address | approximately 30 ms/frame |
| FSR scanning | complete FSR1 scan followed by complete FSR2 scan | both independent MUX enables active for one shared address; read ADC1 then ADC2 | 16 settle events instead of 32 |
| ACC SPI2 | 62.5 kHz, prescaler `/256` | 500 kHz, prescaler `/32` | approximately 8x ACC bus speed |
| ACC `WHO_AM_I` | all ready ACCs checked every frame | one ACC health/recovery slot every 100 ms; all nine checked in approximately 0.9 s | removes repeated identity traffic |
| Host SPI | 500 kHz | 1 MHz | 19.0 ms to approximately 9.5 ms/frame |
| Teensy handshake | IRQ settle 1000 us, CS setup 1000 us, CS hold 100 us | 100 us, 100 us, 50 us | approximately 1.85 ms/frame |

FSR2 keeps the established reverse-MUX/transposed logical storage. CRC32, frame
length, magic, timestamps, flags, and all GUI decoding remain unchanged.

## Verification before formal integration

- Arm GNU Debug build: PASS.
- Linked image: FLASH 19,872 B / 512 KiB; RAM 5,472 B / 128 KiB.
- Combined protocol and GUI mapping self-test: PASS.
- Synthetic old-versus-new FSR1/FSR2 logical mapping equivalence: PASS.
- The first attempted full build under the long OneDrive path failed only
  because Windows exceeded the object-file path limit. Repeating the identical
  build in a short system temporary path linked successfully.
- Arduino CLI is not installed in the command-line environment; upload the
  revised Teensy sketch with Arduino IDE/Teensyduino before hardware testing.

## Hardware acceptance sequence

1. Upload the revised Teensy bridge, then flash the revised STM32 combined ELF.
2. Start the combined GUI and record `source fps`, `FSR flags`, `ACC x/9`, and
   whether `CRC OK` remains continuous for at least 60 s.
3. Press isolated cells near the corners and centre of both FSRs. Reject this
   speed if the response moves to an adjacent row/column, becomes unstable, or
   retains the preceding MUX value.
4. Move the board and confirm every currently healthy ACC changes plausibly.
5. If unstable, fall back in this order: Host SPI 500 kHz; ACC SPI 250 kHz;
   FSR settle 200 us, then 500 us; Teensy waits 1000/1000/100 us.

## Further stepwise values after the first stage passes

| Phase | FSR settle | ACC SPI | Host SPI | Teensy IRQ/CS/hold | Expected complete source rate |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Current verified-by-build stage | 100 us | 500 kHz | 1 MHz | 100/100/50 us | approximately 25-50 Hz |
| Next conservative test | 50 us | 1 MHz | 2 MHz | 50/50/20 us | approximately 40-80 Hz |
| Aggressive blocking test | 25 us | 2 MHz | 4 MHz | 20/20/10 us | hardware-dependent; validate CRC and analogue settling |
| DMA/double-buffer phase | measured minimum | 2-5 MHz | 4-8 MHz | measured minimum | approximately 80-150 Hz target |

The final phase also allows increasing the STM32 system clock from the present
16 MHz to 80 MHz first, then up to the device-specific validated maximum. All
SPI prescalers, ADC timing, DWT delay conversion, power, and signal integrity
must be revalidated after a system-clock change. Never use GUI `display fps` to
judge the acquisition result; use `source fps` based on STM32 sequence/timestamp.
# 2026-08-05 — No-valid-frame LED after first Host-link speed trial

Observed after correctly flashing the paired combined firmware: COM9 was
present and opened by the GUI, but the GUI remained on `Waiting for combined
frames` and the Teensy status LED stayed off. Connections were reported correct.

The existing LED expression becomes true only when all of the following pass:
STM32 IRQ detected, 1188 bytes clocked, `ESK1` magic/version/length valid, CRC32
valid, and USB `Serial.write()` accepts the full frame. Therefore LED off does
not by itself distinguish no IRQ from a corrupted Host SPI frame.

Isolation action: retain all STM32-side speed work (100 us shared FSR settle,
500 kHz ACC SPI, periodic ACC health checks), but restore the Teensy Host link
to the last known stable values:

| Parameter | Failed trial | Isolation fallback |
|---|---:|---:|
| Host SPI | 1 MHz | 500 kHz |
| IRQ settle | 100 us | 1000 us |
| CS setup | 100 us | 1000 us |
| CS hold | 50 us | 100 us |

Expected interpretation after uploading the fallback Teensy sketch:

- LED on and GUI frames return: Host SPI clock/setup margin caused the failure.
- LED still off, Teensy GPIO2 always 0 V: STM32 does not reach/assert HOST_IRQ;
  investigate STM32 execution before `send_frame()`.
- LED still off, GPIO2 goes high or pulses: Host transfer occurs but magic/CRC
  is invalid; retain 500 kHz and instrument the bridge error category.
- GPIO2 remains high continuously: STM32 is waiting in the blocking SPI3 slave
  transfer and Teensy did not recognize/clock the request.

Do not change STM32 sampling parameters during this isolation test. After the
link is restored, test Host timing in this order: 500 kHz with 100/100/50 us,
then 1 MHz with 1000/1000/100 us, then shorten the 1 MHz waits.
# 2026-08-05 — Teensy onboard LED is shared with Host SPI SCK

After the 500 kHz Host-link fallback, the onboard Teensy LED remained generally
off but flashed periodically. Code inspection identified that Teensy 4.1
`LED_BUILTIN` is GPIO13 and the default SPI SCK is also GPIO13. The observed
flash is therefore SPI clock activity, not a valid/invalid frame indication.
It proves that at least some Host SPI transactions are being clocked, but it
does not prove magic, header, CRC, or USB success.

Correction:

- Removed `pinMode(LED_BUILTIN, OUTPUT)` and all status `digitalWrite()` calls.
- Kept GPIO13 exclusively under the SPI peripheral.
- Added cumulative result categories and a once-per-second `#ESKDBG` USB line:
  IRQ count, valid frames, bad magic, bad header, bad CRC, USB unavailable,
  USB short write, IRQ-release timeout, and current IRQ level.
- The binary `ESK1` protocol is unchanged. The GUI synchronizer ignores the
  diagnostic text while searching for the next `ESK1` magic.

Diagnosis procedure: close the GUI, open Arduino Serial Monitor, and copy one
complete `#ESKDBG` line. Rising `irq` with rising `magic` indicates byte/frame
alignment or wrong data; rising `crc` indicates signal/timing corruption;
`release_timeout` indicates STM32 HOST_IRQ did not drop after the transaction;
an unchanged `irq=0` indicates STM32 never asserted or Teensy never detected
GPIO2.
# 2026-08-05 — Host SPI diagnostic result: every transaction fails magic

Observed diagnostic counters:

```text
irq=2..5, ok=0, magic=2..5, header=0, crc=0,
usb_off=0, usb_short=0, release_timeout=0, irq_level=0
```

Interpretation: Teensy detects each STM32 HOST_IRQ and completes/release-handshakes
the transfer, but all received frames fail before header, CRC, or USB checks
because bytes 0..3 are not `45 53 4B 31` (`ESK1`). This excludes the Python GUI,
USB port ownership, CRC calculation, and post-transfer IRQ release. Remaining
classes are MISO not driven (usually all `00` or all `FF`), SPI mode/NSS timing,
or a leading-byte/frame alignment offset.

Diagnostic update: added a second once-per-second line, `#ESKFIRST`, containing
the first 16 bytes actually received by Teensy. Interpret the result as follows:

- `00 ... 00`: MISO held low, STM32 SPI3 not driving, or wrong MISO route.
- `FF ... FF`: MISO floating/high, STM32 not selected, or wrong MISO route.
- `45 53 4B 31 ...` at a later position: byte offset caused by CS/NSS/arming.
- changing random bytes: signal integrity, clock phase/polarity, or contention.
# 2026-08-05 — Valid ESK1 header but every long frame fails CRC

New diagnostic output showed stable prefixes such as:

```text
45 53 4B 31 01 07 A4 04 ...
```

`45 53 4B 31` is the correct `ESK1` magic, version is `01`, flags are `07`,
and little-endian length `A4 04` is 1188 bytes. Counters showed `magic=0`,
`header=0`, `crc` increasing with every IRQ, and `ok=0`. Therefore GPIO2 IRQ,
CS/NSS frame start, SPI mode, initial MISO bytes, length, and USB availability
are working. Corruption or disagreement occurs within the remaining long frame
or its final four CRC bytes.

Next controlled isolation:

- Reduce only Teensy Host SPI from 500 kHz to the original 100 kHz value.
- Retain 1000/1000/100 us IRQ/CS/hold waits.
- Add `#ESKCRC wire=XXXXXXXX calc=XXXXXXXX spi_hz=100000`, where `wire` is
  the STM32 CRC carried in the final four bytes and `calc` is Teensy's CRC32 of
  the received preceding 1184 bytes.
- Leave all STM32 acquisition changes untouched.

Interpretation: `ok` increasing at 100 kHz proves long-frame timing/signal
integrity at 500 kHz is inadequate under the current firmware/hardware state.
CRC still failing at 100 kHz, especially with stable but different CRC values,
requires comparison against STM32 `pack_frame()` and a captured raw frame.
# 2026-08-05 — Root cause isolated: fixed two-byte Host SPI prefix

At 100 kHz the raw first bytes repeatedly showed:

```text
92 BC | 45 53 4B 31 ...
26 2D | 45 53 4B 31 ...
69 4D | 45 53 4B 31 ...
39 0F | 45 53 4B 31 ...
```

The valid `ESK1` magic was consistently at byte offset 2. The old Teensy loop
clocked exactly 1188 bytes, so it captured two stale prefix bytes plus only the
first 1186 bytes of the logical frame, necessarily omitting the final two CRC
bytes. The displayed zero CRC values were not measurements: validation returned
at bad magic before assigning/calculating CRC.

Implemented correction on both sides:

- STM32 `send_frame()` now aborts and reinitializes SPI3 before asserting
  HOST_IRQ, clearing stale slave TX/RX/FIFO state before every transaction.
- Teensy keeps nCS low while searching the first eight clocks for `ESK1`.
  When found, it discards only the prefix and clocks the remaining bytes needed
  to obtain one complete 1188-byte logical frame.
- Added `#ESKALIGN discarded_prefix=N` to confirm whether STM32 reinitialization
  removes the offset (`N=0`) or Teensy recovery is still required (`N=2`).
- Host SPI remains at 100 kHz for this root-cause verification. Do not raise
  speed until `ok` increments and `wire == calc` continuously.

Acceptance result to capture after paired reflash/upload: `magic=0`, `crc=0`,
`ok` increasing, matching wire/calculated CRC, and stable alignment count.

# 2026-08-10 — Controlled Host SPI increase to 500 kHz

The Teensy combined bridge Host SPI clock was increased from 100 kHz to
500 kHz. The IRQ settle, CS setup, and CS hold delays remain unchanged at
1000/1000/100 us, so this test changes only the wire clock. STM32 SPI3 remains
an externally clocked Mode-0 slave and does not require reflashing for this
step. One 1188-byte frame now has a wire time of approximately 19.0 ms and a
wire-only ceiling of approximately 52.6 frames/s.

Upload command:

```powershell
& "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\tools\commands\upload_teensy_sketch.cmd" `
  "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\teensy\applications\ESKIN_COMBINED_BRIDGE" `
  COM9 TEENSY_COMBINED_BRIDGE
```

Acceptance criteria are continuously increasing `ok`, `magic=0`, `header=0`,
`crc=0`, `release_timeout=0`, matching `wire`/`calc` CRC values, no sequence
gaps in the GUI, and a stable alignment count. If any criterion fails, restore
`SPI_HZ=100000` before changing acquisition or handshake timing.

500 kHz runtime result: **FAIL**. During a six-second DTR-enabled COM9 capture,
the cumulative counters changed from approximately `ok=14, crc=695` to
`ok=63, crc=732`: about 49 accepted frames and 37 new CRC failures. Magic
remained zero, alignment remained `discarded_prefix=0`, IRQ released normally,
and individual frames alternated between matching and mismatched wire/calculated
CRC values. This localises the failure to intermittent corruption within the
long frame rather than frame-start alignment. The active test setting was
therefore reduced to 250 kHz with all waits unchanged.

At 250 kHz, one 1188-byte frame takes approximately 38.0 ms on the wire and has
a wire-only ceiling of approximately 26.3 frames/s. Apply the same acceptance
criteria before retaining it; otherwise restore the verified 100 kHz fallback.

250 kHz runtime result: **FAIL**. A ten-second DTR-enabled COM9 capture initially
showed accepted frames, then CRC failures became continuous and the transfer
rate degraded to approximately one transaction per second. The observed
counters progressed from `irq=310, ok=0, crc=116` through
`irq=331, ok=15, crc=122`, then to `irq=336, ok=15, crc=127`. Throughout the
failure, `magic=0`, `header=0`, `release_timeout=0`, and
`discarded_prefix=0`; wire/calculated CRC values diverged on the failed frames.
This setting was rejected and the active bridge was restored to the 100 kHz
fallback with 1000/1000/100 us IRQ/CS/hold waits.

Final 100 kHz recovery result: **still intermittent CRC failure**. The combined
STM32 image rebuilt successfully (19,884 bytes Flash, 5,472 bytes RAM), pyOCD
confirmed all 20 programmed pages were identical, and a hardware reset
completed. The matching 100 kHz Teensy bridge was also rebuilt and uploaded.
During the following ten-second capture, counters progressed from approximately
`irq=379, ok=31, crc=175` to `irq=517, ok=128, crc=216`: about 97 newly accepted
frames and 41 new CRC failures. `magic=0`, `header=0`, `release_timeout=0`, and
`discarded_prefix=0` remained stable. Therefore Host clock alone is not the
root cause; current evidence points to corruption within the 1188-byte transfer
or the STM32/physical SPI path. Leave the device at 100 kHz, but do not mark the
current long-frame acceptance as passed.

# 2026-08-10 — Host SPI low-frequency CRC isolation

Objective: determine whether the intermittent 1188-byte CRC failures depend on
Host SPI clock frequency. Change only the Teensy SCK setting; keep the STM32
image, Mode 0, 1188-byte `ESK1` protocol, CRC32 implementation, and
1000/1000/100 us IRQ/CS/hold waits unchanged.

First isolation point: reduce Host SPI from 100 kHz to 50 kHz. One frame takes
approximately 190.1 ms on the wire, for a wire-only ceiling of approximately
5.26 frames/s. A new capture utility saves every test instead of retaining data
only in terminal memory:

```powershell
powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File `
  "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\tools\capture_combined_diagnostics.ps1" `
  -Port COM9 -DurationSeconds 20 -Label host_spi_50khz
```

Outputs are written under `docs\test_results`:

- `.bin`: complete raw USB serial capture, including accepted binary frames and
  ASCII diagnostics;
- `.txt`: test parameters, counter deltas, acceptance/CRC percentages, transfer
  rate, and extracted `#ESK...` lines.

Decision rule: if 50 kHz materially lowers the CRC error rate relative to the
approximately 29.7% 100 kHz sample, frequency/signal timing is involved. If it
does not, test 25 kHz; persistent errors at both lower rates make a pure SCK
frequency limit unlikely and shift priority to STM32 SPI3 FIFO/HAL state,
buffer integrity, or a physical intermittent connection.

The first 50 kHz hardware-SPI capture is **invalid as a frequency comparison**.
Although firmware reported `spi_hz=50000`, 20 seconds produced 362 transactions
(18.1/s), which is physically impossible for 1188 bytes at 50 kHz. Inspection
of Teensyduino 1.62.0 `SPI.h` showed that Teensy 4.x `beginTransaction()` limits
the LPSPI clock divisor to 257. Requests below the resulting hardware minimum
are therefore clamped rather than generated exactly. The capture is retained at
`docs/test_results/20260810_140929_host_spi_50khz.*` for audit, but its 39.503%
CRC result must not be labelled an actual 50 kHz result.

For the controlled low-frequency test only, the bridge now uses GPIO11 MOSI,
GPIO12 MISO, and GPIO13 SCK as a software Mode-0 SPI master with a 10 us
half-period. This generates approximately 50 kHz independently of the LPSPI
divisor clamp. CS, IRQ, frame parsing, CRC, and all handshake delays are
unchanged. Diagnostics identify this build with `soft_spi=1`.

Valid software-SPI 50 kHz result: **preliminary PASS**. The saved 20-second
capture contained 85 measured transactions, all 85 accepted, with zero CRC,
magic, header, or IRQ-release errors. Measured transaction rate was 4.25/s,
which is physically plausible for a 1188-byte frame at approximately 50 kHz.
Every sampled `wire` and `calc` CRC matched, and diagnostics confirmed
`spi_hz=50000 soft_spi=1` and `discarded_prefix=0`.

Saved evidence:

- `docs/test_results/20260810_141218_host_spi_50khz_soft.bin`
- `docs/test_results/20260810_141218_host_spi_50khz_soft.txt`

Compared with the approximately 29.7% CRC error observed in the earlier
hardware-SPI run labelled 100 kHz, this strongly associates the failures with
Host SCK timing/signal integrity. It is not yet proof of frequency alone because
software SPI also changes GPIO edge shape and the sampling instant. Retain the
50 kHz software test build while collecting a longer run; then test controlled
software-SPI steps such as 75 and 100 kHz to locate the boundary.

## Controlled software-SPI 100 kHz point

After the valid 50 kHz result, change only `SPI_HZ` from 50000 to 100000 while
retaining `USE_SOFTWARE_SPI=true`, Mode 0, the 1188-byte frame, CRC32, and
1000/1000/100 us IRQ/CS/hold waits. The software half-period changes from 10 us
to 5 us. The expected wire time is approximately 95.0 ms per frame and the
wire-only ceiling is approximately 10.5 frames/s. Save the test as
`host_spi_100khz_soft` and compare its CRC error rate directly with the saved
50 kHz software result.

100 kHz software-SPI result: **CRC PASS, complete-system FAIL**. The saved
20-second capture contained 13 measured transactions and all 13 were accepted:
`delta_crc=0`, `delta_magic=0`, `delta_header=0`, and
`delta_release_timeout=0`. Diagnostics confirmed `spi_hz=100000 soft_spi=1`,
so the observed CRC error rate was 0%.

However, the measured transaction rate was only 0.65/s. Consecutive STM32 frame
timestamps increased by approximately 1556 ms, closely matching
`HOST_TIMEOUT_MS=1500`. Teensy therefore received a complete CRC-valid frame,
but STM32's blocking `HAL_SPI_TransmitReceive()` did not finish normally and
waited for its timeout/recovery path before producing the next frame. This point
is rejected for normal operation even though its received CRC acceptance was
100%.

Saved evidence:

- `docs/test_results/20260810_142110_host_spi_100khz_soft.bin`
- `docs/test_results/20260810_142110_host_spi_100khz_soft.txt`

Interpretation: raw SCK frequency alone does not explain the earlier CRC
corruption, because software Mode-0 at 100 kHz delivered CRC-clean frames. The
hardware-LPSPI edge/sampling behaviour is implicated in the CRC errors, while
the STM32 full-duplex polling completion path is separately implicated in the
software-100-kHz throughput collapse. Restore the active bridge to the
50 kHz software-SPI point, which passed both CRC and throughput acceptance.

# 2026-08-10 — STM32 SPI3 full-duplex DMA stage 1

The STM32 Host SPI3 transport was changed from blocking CPU polling to
full-duplex DMA. DMA1 Channel1 uses the `SPI3_RX` DMAMUX request to drain all
Teensy MOSI dummy bytes into `host_rx`; DMA1 Channel2 uses the `SPI3_TX` request
to feed the 1188-byte `ESK1` frame from `host_tx` to MISO. Both channels use
byte alignment, incrementing memory, fixed peripheral address, normal mode,
and very-high priority. DMA and SPI3 error interrupts are enabled.

The ordering is deliberately:

1. abort/reinitialize SPI3 to clear stale state;
2. arm `HAL_SPI_TransmitReceive_DMA()` for both directions;
3. assert `HOST_IRQ` only after DMA is ready;
4. let Teensy assert NSS and supply SCK;
5. complete through `HAL_SPI_TxRxCpltCallback()` or recover through the DMA/SPI
   error and timeout paths;
6. lower `HOST_IRQ` and reuse the buffers only after completion.

This is DMA stage 1: it removes CPU FIFO polling but intentionally waits for DMA
completion before starting the next acquisition. It therefore validates FIFO,
IRQ, timeout, alignment, and CRC behaviour without simultaneously introducing
double-buffer ownership. Stage 2 will add two frame buffers so CPU acquisition
of frame N+1 can overlap DMA transmission of frame N.

Initial validation target remains the accepted Teensy software Mode-0 50 kHz
configuration. Required result: increasing DMA completion count, no DMA errors
or timeouts, `crc=0`, `release_timeout=0`, `discarded_prefix=0`, and normal
approximately 4–5 frame/s throughput before testing higher SCK rates.

## Full-duplex DMA hardware validation

Build and flash completed successfully. The DMA image uses 24,300 bytes Flash
and 5,672 bytes RAM. PyOCD erased/programmed 24 pages and performed a hardware
reset.

At the initial software-SPI 50 kHz point, the saved 20-second capture reported:

```text
delta_irq=87
delta_ok=87
delta_crc=0
acceptance_percent=100
crc_error_percent=0
transfer_rate_hz=4.35
```

Evidence:

- `docs/test_results/20260810_145656_host_spi_50khz_soft_stm32_dma.bin`
- `docs/test_results/20260810_145656_host_spi_50khz_soft_stm32_dma.txt`

The same DMA image was then tested with a true software Mode-0 100 kHz SCK. The
saved 20-second result was:

```text
delta_irq=147
delta_ok=147
delta_crc=0
delta_magic=0
delta_header=0
delta_release_timeout=0
acceptance_percent=100
crc_error_percent=0
transfer_rate_hz=7.35
```

Evidence:

- `docs/test_results/20260810_145806_host_spi_100khz_soft_stm32_dma.bin`
- `docs/test_results/20260810_145806_host_spi_100khz_soft_stm32_dma.txt`

This replaces the pre-DMA 100 kHz result of 0.65 frame/s and approximately
1556 ms between STM32 timestamps. Full-duplex DMA therefore removed the primary
blocking-completion timeout at this clock while preserving zero CRC errors.
The active accepted configuration is now software Mode-0 100 kHz on Teensy and
full-duplex SPI3 DMA on STM32. It is still stage 1 (DMA completion is awaited);
double buffering and hardware-SPI frequency increases remain separate steps.

# 2026-08-10 — STM32 DMA software ping-pong buffers

Stage 2 introduces two independent 1188-byte TX buffers and two matching RX
dummy buffers. `host_send_index` is owned exclusively by SPI3 DMA, while the CPU
scans FSR1, FSR2, and all ACC devices and packs the next CRC-protected frame into
`host_fill_index`. After DMA completion, the indices swap. The CPU never writes
the buffer marked for transmission.

The first loop iteration primes one complete frame. Steady-state operation is:

```text
DMA sends frame N from buffer A
    concurrently with
CPU acquisition/CRC packing of frame N+1 into buffer B

DMA completion -> A becomes free -> swap A/B
```

DMA completion and error callbacks immediately lower `HOST_IRQ`, so Teensy does
not wait for the CPU acquisition running in parallel. If DMA start fails, the
ready frame is retained for retry. If an active transfer fails or times out, the
newly filled alternate frame becomes the next send candidate after SPI3
recovery. SPI3 reinitialization remains in the per-transfer start path for this
validation stage because it previously removed stale-prefix state; removing
that reinitialization is deferred until long-run alignment remains zero.

Expected 100 kHz result: wire time remains approximately 95 ms, but acquisition
and CRC work should overlap most of it. Complete source rate should rise from
7.35 fps toward the wire ceiling of 10.5 fps, with zero CRC, magic, header,
alignment, DMA, and IRQ-release errors.

## DMA ping-pong validation result

Build and flash passed. The ping-pong image uses 24,624 bytes Flash and 8,056
bytes RAM; the additional RAM is the expected second 1188-byte TX buffer and
second 1188-byte RX dummy buffer. PyOCD programmed the image and performed a
hardware reset.

The saved 20-second 100 kHz software-SPI capture reported:

```text
delta_irq=209
delta_ok=209
delta_crc=0
delta_magic=0
delta_header=0
delta_release_timeout=0
acceptance_percent=100
crc_error_percent=0
transfer_rate_hz=10.45
```

Independent parsing of the saved raw stream found 215 complete binary `ESK1`
frames, sequence 95 through 309, with zero sequence gaps. This confirms that
the throughput gain did not come from retransmitting an old buffer or
overwriting the DMA-owned buffer.

Evidence:

- `docs/test_results/20260810_151704_host_spi_100khz_soft_stm32_dma_pingpong.bin`
- `docs/test_results/20260810_151704_host_spi_100khz_soft_stm32_dma_pingpong.txt`

Result: **PASS**. Relative to DMA stage 1, throughput increased from 7.35 to
10.45 frame/s and reached 99.3% of the 100 kHz wire-only ceiling of 10.52
frame/s, while CRC and sequence integrity remained perfect for this short run.
The active configuration is now 100 kHz software Mode-0 Host SPI, STM32 SPI3
full-duplex DMA, and two software ping-pong frame buffers.

# 2026-08-10 — Raise Host SPI to the acquisition-matched point

The pre-overlap single-buffer measurements imply approximately 41 ms of FSR,
ACC, frame-pack, and CRC work per complete frame. A 1188-byte frame contains
9504 wire bits, so the Host clock that makes transport time approximately equal
to acquisition time is `9504 / 0.041`, or about 232 kHz. Select the standard
250 kHz test point rather than increasing directly to 500 kHz.

Only the Teensy software Mode-0 `SPI_HZ` changes from 100000 to 250000. STM32
full-duplex DMA, ping-pong ownership, 1188-byte protocol, CRC32, IRQ/CS/hold
waits, and all sensor acquisition settings remain unchanged. At nominal
250 kHz, wire time is 38.016 ms and the wire-only ceiling is 26.3 frame/s. The
expected complete rate is approximately 20–25 frame/s because acquisition now
becomes the similar or slower pipeline stage.

Acceptance requires zero CRC/magic/header/release errors, zero sequence gaps,
and a substantial increase over the accepted 10.45 frame/s at 100 kHz. If it
fails, restore the accepted 100 kHz DMA ping-pong configuration.

The first two 250 kHz captures each showed zero SPI/CRC/header/IRQ errors and
21.3 transactions/s, but exactly one `USB short write` immediately after DTR
opened. Each capture therefore reported 425 accepted frames out of 426 IRQs;
the first raw stream contained one matching sequence gap. The short write was a
USB connection-start race, not Host SPI corruption: `crc`, `magic`, `header`,
and `release_timeout` remained unchanged, and all later frames were accepted.

The Teensy USB forwarding path now retries partial `Serial.write()` operations
until all 1188 bytes are queued or a 100 ms timeout expires. It never starts a
diagnostic/result decision after merely writing a prefix of a binary frame.
Revalidate 250 kHz after upload; acceptance now requires no new
`usb_short` count and zero binary sequence gaps as well as zero CRC errors.

The partial-write retry alone still observed exactly one 100 ms USB timeout
after each DTR connection, while all Host SPI/CRC counters remained clean. This
confirms a USB CDC endpoint-start race rather than sustained bandwidth pressure.
The bridge now tracks the disconnected-to-connected transition and waits 500 ms
before forwarding the first binary frame. Frames acquired during this settling
window are counted as `usb_off` and discarded whole; no partial frame enters the
USB stream. Diagnostic text may flow during the window to let the endpoint
become ready. The existing full-frame retry remains active after settling.

## 250 kHz final validation

With the 500 ms USB connection-settling window, the saved 20-second capture
reported 460 acquisition/Host transactions. Twelve complete frames were
deliberately classified `usb_off` during connection settling; after that, all
448 measured frames were accepted. There were no new `usb_short`, CRC, magic,
header, or IRQ-release errors.

```text
delta_irq=460
delta_ok=448
delta_usb_off=12 (intentional connection settling)
delta_usb_short=0
delta_crc=0
transaction_rate=23.0 Hz
USB output rate=22.4 frame/s
USB-ready acceptance=448/448 = 100%
```

Independent parsing found 457 complete binary frames, sequence 9610 through
10066, with zero sequence gaps. The differing binary/count window endpoints are
caused by diagnostics being sampled inside the raw-capture interval, not by
lost frames.

Evidence:

- `docs/test_results/20260810_152754_host_spi_250khz_soft_dma_pingpong_usb_settle.bin`
- `docs/test_results/20260810_152754_host_spi_250khz_soft_dma_pingpong_usb_settle.txt`

Result: **PASS**. The active configuration is nominal 250 kHz software Mode-0
Host SPI, STM32 full-duplex DMA, and ping-pong buffers. At 23.0 transactions/s,
one complete pipeline cycle is approximately 43.5 ms. The nominal Host wire
time is 38.0 ms, so sensor acquisition/packing is now the slightly slower stage.
Further Host-frequency increases alone should give little improvement until the
FSR/ACC phase timings are instrumented and reduced.

## 2026-08-10 documentation update and next optimisation flow

The measured progression, line chart, bilingual summary and full acceptance
criteria are consolidated in:

```text
docs/updates/2026-08-10/PROGRESS_UPDATE.md
```

The next work keeps the `ESK1` frame and CRC32 unchanged and changes only one
timing variable per experiment:

1. Instrument FSR1, FSR2, ACC, packing/CRC, DMA and total cycle time.
2. Start ADC1 and ADC2 conversions together so conversion time overlaps.
3. Test MUX settling at 100, 75, 50 and 25 us, validating analogue accuracy and
   crosstalk in addition to CRC.
4. Test ACC SPI2 at 1 and 2 MHz while preserving valid data from all positions.
5. Revalidate Teensy hardware LPSPI at 500 kHz, 1 MHz, 2 MHz and 4 MHz now that
   the STM32 full-duplex DMA receiver is active.
6. Raise the STM32 clock from 16 MHz to a validated 80 MHz configuration, then
   consider 170 MHz only after recalculating timing and peripheral dividers.
7. Require zero CRC errors, sequence gaps, DMA errors and post-settle USB short
   writes for 1000 frames, followed by 10,000 frames or at least 60 seconds.

Target cycle limits are 20 ms for 50 complete frames/s and 10 ms for 100
complete frames/s. Additional buffers may reduce jitter, but will not raise
sustained throughput unless acquisition or transfer time is reduced.

## 2026-08-10 — STM32 combined-system clock raised to 80 MHz

The combined STM32 application now derives an 80 MHz SYSCLK/HCLK from the
internal 16 MHz HSI through PLLM=4, PLLN=40 and PLLR=2. Voltage scale 1 remains
active and Flash latency is increased from zero to two wait states.

SPI1 and SPI2 only provide power-of-two baud prescalers, so their exact former
8 MHz and 500 kHz rates cannot be retained from an 80 MHz peripheral clock.
For the first CPU-clock validation, both sensor buses are deliberately kept
conservative:

```text
SPI1 ADC: 80 MHz APB2 / 16 = 5 MHz
SPI2 ACC: 20 MHz APB1 / 64 = 312.5 kHz
SPI3 Host: external Teensy software Mode-0 SCK remains nominal 250 kHz
```

The 100 us MUX settling delay uses DWT cycles calculated from
`SystemCoreClock`, so its real-time duration remains 100 us after the clock
change. HAL millisecond timeouts continue to use the reconfigured SysTick.

Initial 80 MHz tests exposed a timing race that the slower 16 MHz acquisition
had hidden. The STM32 could finish the next acquisition and start aborting and
reinitialising SPI3 before Teensy released NSS/CS after its 100 us hold. This
produced alternating incomplete transfers, stale prefix bytes, approximately
50% CRC failures and about 0.6 transactions/s. Reducing APB1 to 20 MHz and
replacing `__WFI()` with polling did not change the failure, ruling out the
peripheral clock and CPU sleep as root causes.

The accepted fix waits for hardware NSS on PA15 to return high before SPI3 is
aborted, reinitialised and armed for the next DMA transaction. The wait has a
10 ms diagnostic timeout and increments `host_dma_timeout_count` if Teensy
does not release CS.

The final 60-second capture is:

```text
docs/test_results/20260810_195415_stm32_80mhz_nss_release_guard_soft250_60s.txt
STM32 IRQ / completed: 1450 / 1438
CRC / magic / header errors: 0 / 0 / 0
post-settle USB short writes / NSS release timeouts: 0 / 0
transaction rate: 24.167 Hz
effective USB output: 23.967 frames/s
USB-ready acceptance: 1438 / 1438 = 100%
```

Independent raw parsing found one capture/USB-startup boundary gap, followed
by 1427 consecutive CRC-valid frames (sequence 3756 through 5182) with zero
steady-state gaps. This passes the required 1000-frame hardware gate. Compared
with the accepted 16 MHz result of 23.0 transactions/s, the transaction rate
increased by 5.1%; Host SPI wire time at nominal 250 kHz is now the dominant
limit rather than the STM32 CPU clock.

## 2026-08-10 — 700 Hz packet transport achieved

The next optimisation cycle reached stable 700 Hz complete-packet transport.
The final architecture uses 10 MHz hardware Host SPI, one shared FSR MUX
address per packet, 100 Hz ACC refresh, table-driven CRC32, STM32 SPI3
full-duplex DMA ping-pong buffers, a 16-frame Teensy USB queue, and explicit
700/s pacing. STM32 is built at 80 MHz; the accepted Teensy 4.1 build uses its
default 600 MHz `F_CPU` setting.

The Release build initially exposed an NSS race that caused alternating DMA
timeouts and stale prefixes. Increasing only Teensy CS timing did not fix it.
Requiring PA15/NSS to remain high for 50 us before STM32 abort/reinitialise/
rearm fixed the race. An unpaced test then reached 883 transactions/s but
overflowed sustained USB output, so the final producer is paced at 700/s.

Final 60-second result: 41,331/41,331 diagnostic-window transfers accepted,
700.181 packets/s, and zero CRC, magic, header, USB-short, sequence-gap, or NSS
release-timeout errors. Because one of 16 MUX addresses is refreshed per
packet, complete FSR matrix freshness is 43.76 Hz; ACC freshness is 100 Hz.

Full bilingual attempt table, flow diagram, profile timings, and evidence:

```text
docs/updates/2026-08-10-700hz/PROGRESS_UPDATE.md
```

## 2026-08-11 — 700 Hz fresh full-scan experiment

The stable rolling-acquisition version was first preserved and pushed as
commit `5468ec8` on branch `firmware-integration-20260810`. A new experimental
cycle then changed the acceptance target from 700 repeated full-size packets/s
to 700 genuinely fresh cycles/s: each frame must scan both 16x16 FSR matrices,
read all nine ACC positions, pack, and transfer within 1.4286 ms.

Protocol v2 was introduced for the requested periodic-CRC test. Sequence
multiples of 32 set flag `0x10` and carry IEEE CRC32; other frames have a zero
trailer. Teensy and Python accept v1 and v2, enforce v2 cadence and trailer
rules, and report checked/skipped counts. This reduces average STM32 CRC time
from about 193 us to 6 us/frame, but leaves 31/32 payloads without CRC
protection and is not a production integrity scheme.

Measured software progression:

```text
full 16-address baseline             145.725/s
overlap ADC1/ADC2 conversion         174.009/s
overlap next MUX settle/FIFO reads   237.991/s
block-copy two FSR matrices          248.577/s (ACC still old cadence)
read all ACC positions every frame   238.014/s
final 30-second diagnostic window    238.475/s
```

The final diagnostic window accepted 6,916/6,916 transfers with zero sampled
CRC, magic, header, sequence, USB-short, or NSS-release errors and 3.123% CRC
coverage. Independent parsing decoded 7,154 frames, sequence 43,962..51,115,
zero gaps, zero rejected candidates, 16 fresh MUX addresses per frame, and a
237.965/s payload-timestamp rate over 30.059 seconds.

The remaining limit is physical. A 700/s, 16-address target permits only
89.29 us/address. In MAX11633 external-clock mode, one 16-input ADC stream
needs at least 53.3 us at the specified 4.8 MHz maximum; the two current ADCs
share one serialized DOUT path and therefore need at least 106.7 us/address
before MUX, ACC, packing, or software overhead. Reaching 700 fresh scans/s
requires independent concurrent ADC readout paths and DMA, plus verified
approximately 25-30 us analogue settling. Full evidence and the hardware plan:

```text
docs/updates/2026-08-11-full-scan-700hz/PROGRESS_UPDATE.md
```

## 2026-08-11 - Isolated reproduction of the old SCLK scan

Before changing the scan implementation, the complete 238.475 Hz full-scan
state was compiled for STM32 and Teensy, committed as `9291d8b`, and pushed to
`firmware-integration-20260810`. Build artefacts and raw `.bin` captures were
excluded from that checkpoint.

A separate project was then added under
`stm32/experiments/combined_system_sclk33`. It reuses the current board code,
ACC path, protocol v2, CRC cadence, SPI3 DMA transport, Teensy bridge and GUI,
but replaces the MAX11633 acquisition function with the old Teensy pattern:
one 33-byte full-duplex transaction per ADC and MUX address, next-channel
commands interleaved with current results, and no EOC polling.

The old setup byte `0x78` was not copied because mainboard 2.2 ties REF to the
external 3.3 V rail. The experiment uses `0x74`, retaining SCLK conversion but
selecting the correct external reference. Three build profiles separate byte
alignment, clock and MUX-settling effects: `safe2p5`,
`legacy10_settle100`, and `legacy10`. The two 10 MHz profiles explicitly exceed
the ADC's 4.8 MHz external-clock specification.

Release compilation passed for `safe2p5` and `legacy10_settle100` (14,648 B
Flash / 9,104 B RAM) and `legacy10` (14,616 B Flash / 9,104 B RAM). Hardware
testing then produced a clear separation between packet integrity and ADC data
integrity:

- `safe2p5` produced 2,773 consecutive parsed experiment frames with no frame
  rejection or sequence gap. Both FSR arrays were valid and all 16 MUX
  addresses were updated. Its parsed rate was 138.392 Hz and median FSR time
  was 6,745 us.
- `legacy10_settle100` reached 229.210 Hz with no transport or sequence error
  and every sampled CRC check passing, but recent unique FSR codes collapsed
  from 407/638 to 44/55 and were concentrated at bit-boundary values. This is
  an ADC conversion-data failure, not a Host SPI or USB packet failure.
- zero-settle `legacy10` was deliberately skipped after the clock-only 10 MHz
  stage failed. Removing MUX settling could not repair that failure and would
  have introduced another variable.
- the unchanged normal flash script restored the current full-scan image. A
  recovery capture passed at 238.333 Hz with 2,145/2,145 accepted frames and
  all tracked error counters at zero.

CRC cannot detect this 10 MHz failure because the incorrect ADC values already
exist before the STM32 constructs and checksums the frame. The staged commands,
exact 33-byte sequence, captures, rollback command, risks and acceptance gates
are recorded in:

```text
docs/updates/2026-08-11-sclk33-replication/PROGRESS_UPDATE.md
```

## 2026-08-12 - Isolated ACC maximum-rate benchmark

An isolated STM32 benchmark separated ACC register polling speed from genuine
sensor ODR. It used a 160 MHz STM32G474, 10 MHz SPI2 and one auto-increment
read from `STATUS_REG` through all XYZ bytes for every decoder address A1..A9.
`ZYXDA` counted only genuinely new physical samples; Host SPI, USB, FSR and GUI
time were excluded.

The 12-bit high-resolution profile (`0x97/0x88`) completed 4235.717 nine-slot
passes/s but produced 1244.917..1357.909 fresh samples/s per online sensor, as
expected for the nominal 1.344 kHz ODR. The 8-bit low-power profile
(`0x9F/0x80`) completed 4233.012 passes/s and every visit contained fresh data;
an independent window repeated at 4233.009/s. All windows had zero SPI errors.

Only ACC1/2/3/4/7/8 passed identity and configuration. ACC5 returned `0x11`
and ACC6/9 returned `0xFF`, although all nine address transactions remained in
the timed loop. The complete method, limitations, evidence and commands are in:

```text
docs/updates/2026-08-12-acc-rate-benchmark/PROGRESS_UPDATE.md
```

## 2026-08-17 - SWD comparison: fresh versus original STM32 devices

An unused STM32 programmed successfully with the same DAPLink probe
(`LU_2022_8888`), target (`stm32g474cetx`), build and combined ELF that were
used for the two original devices. The original devices repeatedly returned
`Target: n/a`, `DebugPortSetup: No ACK`, `Unexpected ACK '5'`, and Cortex-M4
debug-component/reset-catch failures. The latest failed attempt stopped before
any `Erased` or `Programmed` line; the compiler had already completed and only
reported unused legacy functions/variables.

This comparison shifts the leading suspicion from the command/toolchain to
target-specific state or hardware: RDP/write-protection option bytes, or
damage/marginal behaviour of the original MCU's SWDIO, SWCLK, NRST or power
pins. If a complete module was exchanged rather than only the MCU, its SWD
traces, connector and 3.3 V path must also be compared. Firmware contents alone
should not prevent an under-reset attach when SWD is electrically available.

The exact evidence and the required fields for every subsequent attempt are
recorded here:

```text
docs/test_results/20260817_swd_fresh_vs_original_mcu.txt
docs/updates/2026-08-17-swd-mcu-comparison/PROGRESS_UPDATE.md
```

### `pyocd list` target-column clarification

After a replacement STM32 was programmed successfully, `pyocd list` still
reported the probe UID but showed `Target: n/a`. This is not a failed target
identification: the list command primarily enumerates the CMSIS-DAP probe and
may not open a target session. The successful flash session itself identified
the STM32 and completed erase/program. Use `DP IDR`, `Cortex-M4`, `Erased`,
`Programmed` and the pyOCD exit code as authoritative evidence; keep the
`pyocd list` line only as probe-enumeration evidence.

The original-device comparison then performed a read-only commander access at
1 kHz:

```text
address: 0x40022020 (FLASH_OPTR)
value:   0xFBEFF8AA
```

The low byte `0xAA` is RDP Level 0. `nSWBOOT0` is clear and `nBOOT0` is set,
matching the known post-correction configuration (`0xFBEFF8AA`) rather than
the old physical-BOOT0 configuration (`0xFFEFF8AA`). No Option Bytes were
written. Because the original target answered this SWD memory read,
`Target: n/a` from `pyocd list` must not be interpreted as proof that the chip
is absent or protected; intermittent failures should now be investigated as
SWD session, power, reset or signal-integrity issues.

## 2026-08-17 - 1 kHz no-reset flash path succeeded

The following manual command was confirmed to complete the combined STM32
flash operation successfully:

```powershell
pyocd flash -v -W -u LU_2022_8888 -t stm32g474cetx `
  -f 1k -M under-reset --no-reset -e sector `
  "D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_COMBINED_SYSTEM\ESKIN_STM32.elf"
```

This result proves that the STM32 programming/verification path works at
1 kHz when the post-program reset is suppressed. It does not by itself prove
that the new application has booted.

Compared with the normal command, this path:

- lowers SWD from 10 kHz to 1 kHz;
- adds `--no-reset`, avoiding the automatic post-program reset;
- does not execute the separate `pyocd reset -m hw` step.

The current gate is therefore:

| Stage | Status |
|---|---|
| STM32 flash programming and verification | PASS at 1 kHz with `--no-reset` |
| Normal 10 kHz reset/session cleanup | UNRESOLVED |
| Automatic application boot after flash | NOT PROVEN |
| Runtime SPI/USB frame output | NOT TESTED by this command |

Because the successful command changes both SWD frequency and reset handling,
the root cause cannot yet be assigned exclusively to reset wiring or 10 kHz
signal integrity. The next controlled comparison is 1 kHz with reset enabled
and 10 kHz with `--no-reset`, followed by a separate runtime reset or complete
power cycle and frame capture.

## 2026-08-17 - 1 MHz SWD comparison also reported successful

The user additionally reported that a 1 MHz pyOCD operation completed normally.
Because 1 MHz is higher than both 1 kHz and 10 kHz, this rules out a simple
monotonic conclusion that the SWD clock is generally too fast.

The exact 1 MHz command and its reset flags were not provided, so this result
does not yet separate reset/session behaviour from frequency-dependent timing:

| Interpretation | Status |
|---|---|
| General SWD frequency ceiling below 1 MHz | Unlikely |
| 10 kHz-specific reset/session timing interaction | Still possible |
| Reset-option-independent proof | Not established |

Repeat the 1 MHz test with the normal reset options and with `--no-reset`
separately. Record programming, session uninitialisation, reset and runtime
boot as independent outcomes.

## 2026-08-17 - 1 MHz automatic reset passed

The controlled 1 MHz test was repeated with automatic hardware reset enabled:

```powershell
pyocd flash -v -W -u LU_2022_8888 -t stm32g474cetx `
  -f 1M -M under-reset -O reset_type=hw -e sector `
  "D:\study\programming\builds\ESKIN_COMBINED_SYSTEM\ESKIN_STM32.elf"
```

The command completed with exit code `0`. The log showed reset assertion before
connect, successful DP IDR/Cortex-M4 discovery, reset deassertion after
connect, and `Erased 0 bytes, programmed 0 bytes, identical 15360 bytes`.

This proves that automatic hardware reset works at 1 MHz. Together with the
earlier 1 MHz `--no-reset` success, a generally broken NRST path is unlikely.
The remaining comparison is the normal 10 kHz sequence: run the 10 kHz flash
with `reset_type=hw` but without the script's second standalone `pyocd reset`,
then test that second reset separately.

## 2026-08-17 - Maintained Combined flash command switched to 1 MHz no-reset

The maintained Combined flash entry point was updated after the controlled
comparison showed that 1 MHz with `--no-reset` reliably completes programming,
while the former 10 kHz automatic-reset path can fail during board
uninitialisation. The script now uses:

```text
pyocd flash -W -u LU_2022_8888 -t stm32g474cetx
  -f 1M -M under-reset --no-reset -e sector ESKIN_STM32.elf
```

The trailing standalone `pyocd reset -m hw` was removed. The script prints a
manual-reset/power-cycle instruction after a successful flash. This change is
limited to `tools/commands/original/flash_combined.cmd` and therefore also
affects the wrapper and paired Combined commands that call it; historical and
diagnostic scripts remain unchanged.

## 2026-08-17 - Single-module scalability capture: no HOST_IRQ and no frames

The first runtime capture after returning to the single-module Combined-style
bridge showed the GUI waiting on COM9. The Teensy emitted diagnostics, proving
that its USB serial path was alive, but the selected module produced no ready
interrupts:

| Field | Observed value |
|---|---:|
| `spi_hz` | 10,000,000 |
| `soft_spi` | 0 (hardware SPI) |
| `irq` | 0 |
| `ok` | 0 |
| `magic`, `header`, `sequence`, `crc` | 0 |
| `irq_level` | 0 |
| `wire`, `calc` CRC | `00000000`, `00000000` |
| `discarded_prefix` | 0 |

This is not a CRC error: no SPI frame reached the validation stage. It means
the selected Teensy IRQ input stayed low and no transaction was started. The
most likely causes are that the target STM32 was not running after the
no-reset flash, the physical module does not match the Teensy compile-time
module ID, or the selected module's `HOST_IRQ`/`HOST_nCS` lines are open or
miswired. The module ID was not visible in the screenshot, so the next capture
must record it explicitly. Raw evidence is saved in
`docs/test_results/20260817_scalability_single_module_no_irq.txt`.

## 2026-08-17 - Controlled result: Teensy connection prevents SWD flashing

The user completed the A/B comparison and confirmed that the module is powered
entirely by its external supply. The Teensy is connected only for Host-data
processing and transport; it is not being used as the module power source.
With the same DAPLink, target and flash command, STM32 programming succeeds
when the Teensy is disconnected and fails when the Teensy Host connection is
attached. The observed failure is during `DebugPortSetup` with
`SWD/JTAG communication failure (Unexpected ACK '5')`, before `Loading` or
`Programming` begins.

This excludes Teensy 3.3 V back-powering as the primary explanation. The
current working conclusion is an electrical or reset-time interaction on the
Host signal harness, with the following priority:

1. `PB8/HOST_IRQ/BOOT0` is sampled or driven at an unsafe level while STM32
   reset is asserted. Teensy Pin 2 is an input only after the sketch has
   configured it, so its level during Teensy boot/upload is not a sufficient
   hardware guarantee.
2. The Host `nCS`, SCK, MOSI or MISO lines are not high-impedance/idle during
   STM32 reset and disturb the target's reset or boot timing.
3. The connector wiring accidentally couples the Teensy harness to `NRST`.
   J1-10 `NRST` must remain a DAPLink-only connection; J1-8 is the separate
   `HOST_IRQ` signal.

The failure is therefore not currently attributed to SWD frequency, FSR
algorithm, CRC or GUI. The immediate isolation test is to keep external power
unchanged, disconnect only `HOST_IRQ`, and repeat the same flash command. If
that succeeds, reconnect `HOST_IRQ` and add a defined approximately 10 kOhm
pull-down from PB8/BOOT0 to GND. If it still fails, restore the remaining Host
signals one at a time to identify the offending line.

During SWD programming the safe procedure is to disconnect the Teensy Host
harness, leave DAPLink SWDIO/SWCLK/NRST/GND/VTref connected, flash with the
`--no-reset` path, manually reset or power-cycle the STM32, and only then
reconnect Teensy for runtime acquisition.

### Ongoing record policy

From this point, meaningful hardware steps, command outcomes, error messages,
measurements and controlled comparisons will be appended to this
`docs/WORKFLOW.md`. Progress-update reports are not changed for routine
debugging records unless the user explicitly requests a new progress update.

## 2026-08-18 - Root cause confirmed: damaged FFC connector plug

The user identified physical damage to the FFC cable connector/plug. This is
the confirmed hardware root cause for the previously observed single-module
runtime failure:

- Teensy diagnostics showed `irq=0` and `irq_level=0`;
- the FSR1 reader repeatedly timed out waiting for a complete 515-byte frame;
- no `HOST_nCS`/SPI transaction was observed and the Pin 13 activity LED stayed
  dark;
- the GUI remained at `Waiting for ... frames` even though COM9 opened.

The damaged FFC interface interrupted or destabilised the module Host signal
path, so the software chain could not receive `HOST_IRQ` or a valid SPI frame.
The earlier apparent dependence on whether Teensy was connected is therefore
not evidence that Teensy power was the root cause; connecting the harness
changed the electrical state of the damaged connector and exposed the fault.

This supersedes the earlier provisional hypotheses of a pure protocol mismatch,
SWD-clock problem or Teensy LED fault for this test setup. Replace the damaged
FFC plug/cable or repair the connector, then repeat the single-module test with
the same firmware and commands. Record connector replacement and a new
`HOST_IRQ`/SPI capture before changing firmware.

## 2026-08-17 - FSR1 serial reader timed out waiting for frames

The FSR reader was started on `COM9` with the FSR1 binary parser. The serial
port opened successfully and reported:

```text
Listening on COM9; expecting 515 bytes per frame
Warning: Timed out while waiting for a complete frame
```

The timeout repeated during the visible capture. This proves that Windows and
the Python process could open COM9, but it does not prove that the active
Teensy sketch is the FSR1 bridge or that STM32 is producing frames. The FSR1
reader expects the `0xA5 + uint16 sequence + 256 uint16 ADC` 515-byte protocol.
If the active Teensy image is the selectable/Combined bridge, it emits a
different 1188-byte `ESK1` protocol and this reader will also time out. The
next controlled step is to upload `ESKIN_FSR1_FAST_BRIDGE` explicitly, close
all other COM9 users, reset the STM32, and repeat the reader. If the timeout
persists with the correct bridge, inspect FSR1 `HOST_IRQ`, `HOST_nCS`, STM32
boot state and the SPI3 path.

## 2026-08-18 - Restarting multi-module validation after FFC repair

After the damaged FFC connector was identified, multi-module validation is
being restarted from the beginning. The first phase remains conservative
single-module selection: one physical module is connected to the shared Teensy
bus at a time, and the Teensy is compiled with that module's CS/IRQ ID. The
same `combined_system` STM32 image is used on every module; no simultaneous
round-robin or `MUL1` protocol is claimed yet.

The controlled order is:

1. Module 0: flash/verify STM32, manually reset, upload selectable bridge ID 0,
   capture diagnostics and open the existing Combined GUI.
2. Module 1: repeat with bridge ID 1 and its CS/IRQ pair.
3. Module 2: repeat with bridge ID 2.
4. Module 3: repeat with bridge ID 3.

For each module, record the FFC replacement state, `irq`, `ok`, magic/header/
sequence/CRC counters, frame rate, and whether the GUI displays both FSR maps
and ACC data. A module is not marked PASS from GUI appearance alone; it needs
valid frames and a nonzero IRQ count in the diagnostic capture.

The first execution command after the target STM32 is connected to DAPLink is:

```powershell
& "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\tools\commands\original\flash_combined.cmd"
```

After the manual reset/power-cycle, select Module 0 with:

```powershell
& "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\tools\commands\flash_scalablity_test.cmd" COM9 0 all
```

The corresponding IDs for later modules are `1`, `2`, and `3`. During each
STM32 flash, keep the repaired Teensy Host harness disconnected; reconnect it
only for runtime acquisition. After closing the GUI, save a ten-second
diagnostic capture before moving to the next module. Routine results and errors
will continue to be appended here without changing a Progress Update report.

## 2026-08-18 - GUI module label/code audit

The selectable-module upload path was checked after the observation that every
test appeared to open a Module 0 GUI. The result is a presentation limitation,
not evidence that the STM32 images were flashed incorrectly:

- `upload_scalablity_test.cmd` is intended to compile
  `teensy/applications/scalablity` with
  `-DSCALABILITY_MODULE_ID=<0..3>` and upload the matching build directory.
- The Teensy sketch maps the ID to CS/IRQ as 0→(10,2), 1→(14,3),
  2→(15,4), and 3→(16,5). Existing build metadata confirms separate builds
  for IDs 0, 1, 2, and 3 with the expected compiler flag.
- `flash_scalablity_test.cmd` then starts the generic
  `live_combined_monitor.py` with only `--port` and `--view`; the GUI has no
  module argument, no module title, and the ESK1 frame format contains no
  module ID. Therefore the window looks identical for every selected module.
- `flash_combined_pair.cmd` is a different path: it uploads the normal
  `ESKIN_COMBINED_BRIDGE` without a module define, whose default is Module 0.
  It must not be used to test Modules 1–3.

Use `flash_scalablity_test.cmd COM9 <module-id> all` for the selectable test and
verify the corresponding CS/IRQ wiring and Teensy diagnostics. GUI appearance
alone cannot identify the physical module. A future module label can be added
to the GUI/launcher without changing the STM32 image or ESK1 payload.

## 2026-08-18 - Corrected selectable-module compiler flag

The Module 1 runtime test then provided decisive evidence that the previous
selector had not reached the Teensy compiler: pressing Module 0 changed the
GUI while pressing Module 1 did not. The installed Teensy 1.62.0
`platform.txt` uses `build.flags.defs` directly in `recipe.cpp.o.pattern` and
does not reference `compiler.cpp.extra_flags`. Consequently the previous
script recorded the requested value in `build.options.json`, but the generated
compiler command had no `-DSCALABILITY_MODULE_ID` and the source defaulted to
ID 0. The old Module 0/1/2/3 HEX artifacts were byte-identical, confirming
that all four images selected Module 0.

`tools/commands/upload_scalablity_test.cmd` was corrected to inject the module
selector through the Teensy-specific variable while retaining the required
core definitions:

```text
build.flags.defs=-D__IMXRT1062__ -DTEENSYDUINO=160 -DSCALABILITY_MODULE_ID=<id>
```

A temporary Module 1 rebuild succeeded, its `compile_commands.json` contains
`-DSCALABILITY_MODULE_ID=1`, and its HEX hash differs from the old Module 0
image. No hardware upload is claimed by this source-level correction yet.
The next test is to run `flash_scalablity_test.cmd COM9 1 all` again, then
capture `#ESKDBG` and press Module 1 only. Do not use the old HEX artifact or
`flash_combined_pair.cmd`, which intentionally defaults to Module 0.

## 2026-08-18 - Four-module combined bridge implementation

All four independently selectable module tests were reported as passing. A
new combined bridge was therefore added under
`teensy/applications/scalablity/four_module/`.

The Teensy now services the four independent HOST_IRQ/CS pairs in a round-robin
pass while sharing SCK 13, MOSI 11 and MISO 12. It retains the most recent
valid 1188-byte ESK1 frame for each module and emits a fixed `MUL1` outer
packet after a complete four-module update round, or after a 100 ms partial
round timeout. The packet contains:

- a 20-byte outer header with version, module count, updated mask, length,
  block size, aggregate sequence and Teensy timestamp;
- four 1192-byte blocks (`module_id`, status, reserved bytes and one ESK1
  frame); and
- a CRC32 over the complete packet except the 4-byte trailer.

The resulting packet length is 4,792 bytes. The existing ESK1 inner protocol
and per-frame CRC checks are unchanged. A module that has not produced a valid
frame is marked non-zero in its block status and is rendered as `waiting` by
the new GUI instead of being confused with a valid zero-valued sensor map.

New commands:

```powershell
& "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\tools\commands\flash_scalablity_four_module.cmd" COM9
& "D:\study\programming\ESKIN\firmware\tools\commands\start_scalablity_four_module.cmd" COM9
```

Validation completed at source level: the new Python parser self-test passed,
the 4-column GUI layout instantiated with 8 FSR artists and 4 ACC axes, and
the four-module Teensy sketch compiled successfully for Teensy 4.1. Hardware
upload and a four-board runtime capture remain the next acceptance step. The
aggregate packet rate is not claimed to be 700 complete four-module packets/s;
it is limited by the round-robin service time of four STM32 frames.

## 2026-08-18 - Four-module GUI module selector

The four-module GUI was refined with an independent top module selector:
`All Modules`, `Module 0`, `Module 1`, `Module 2`, and `Module 3`. The existing
right-side sensor selector (`All`, `FSR`, `ACC`) remains independent. This
allows combinations such as Module 2 + FSR or Module 3 + ACC without changing
the MUL1 protocol or Teensy firmware. The selector/layout self-test passed for
all-module, single-module and sensor-specific views.

## 2026-08-18 - Four-module theoretical transfer volume

The current four-module bridge transmits one 1,188-byte ESK1 frame per
module. Per frame, the wire layout is 1,024 bytes of two 16x16 FSR maps,
144 bytes of nine ACC records, 16 bytes of header and 4 bytes of inner CRC.
The MUL1 packet adds 20 bytes of outer header, 4 bytes of block metadata per
module and 4 bytes of outer CRC:

```text
4 × 1,188-byte ESK1 frames = 4,752 bytes
MUL1 overhead              =    40 bytes
Full MUL1 packet           = 4,792 bytes
```

At the requested but not currently achievable 700 complete four-module rounds
per second, this would be 3,354,400 bytes/s on USB (26.8352 Mbit/s), including
the MUL1 envelope. The inner sensor frames alone would be 3,326,400 bytes/s
(26.6112 Mbit/s). A 10 MHz Host SPI can clock only 10 Mbit/s, so it cannot
sustain 700 full four-module rounds/s.

Ignoring STM32 scan time, the current code needs 950.4 us of wire time per
1,188-byte frame. With the 50 us IRQ settle, 10 us CS setup and 10 us CS hold
delays, four modules need at least 4.0816 ms per complete round, or about
245 full MUL1 packets/s. That corresponds to approximately 1,174,049 bytes/s
(9.392 Mbit/s) of USB data. The pure-wire lower bound without those fixed
delays is about 263 complete rounds/s and 1.261 MB/s of USB data. STM32 scan
and readiness time will reduce the measured rate further.
